\hypersetup{pdftitle={Permutations with exactly one occurrence of 1234, 1243 or 12345 (OEIS A217057, A224179, A224248)},pdfauthor={},pdfsubject={Merged report a217057-unique-pattern-occurrences (batch 105): the leading amplitude and the refutation of R = 1/2 (Report 134), rational structure and the first logarithm (Report 135), every order (Report 137), one 1243 (Report 136), one 12345: exact formulas and the leading ratio (Report 138), every order (Report 140)}}
\title{\textbf{Permutations with exactly one occurrence of\\ 1234, 1243 or 12345}\\[4pt]\large OEIS A217057, A224179 and A224248\\[7pt]
\large Half decompositions, leading amplitudes, rational diagonals\\ and asymptotic expansions to every order}
\author{Six research reports of one external research session\\(bundle Reports 134, 135, 136, 137, 138 and 140), merged into one ProveIt report}
\date{Parts I--VI: 2 October 2026\quad merged: 5 October 2026}
\begin{document}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=false}
\maketitle
\begin{abstract}
A permutation contains exactly one classical occurrence of a pattern when exactly one of its subsequences, adjacent or not, is order-isomorphic to the pattern. This report prints six manuscripts on three such counts: $u_n$, for exactly one $1234$ (OEIS A217057); $b_n$, for exactly one $1243$ (A224179); and $U_n$, for exactly one $12345$ (A224248). They are normalized by the avoidance counts: $A_n=|\Av_n(1234)|=|\Av_n(1243)|$ (A005802) for the first two, and $|\Av_n(12345)|$ (A047889) for the third. Part~I proves that $u_n/A_n\to R$, where $R$ is an explicit series of positive terms, so that $u_n\sim(81\sqrt3/(16\pi))R\,9^nn^{-4}$; its finite rational certificate $R>0.50009$ refutes Conway and Guttmann's conjecture $R=1/2$, and an exact partial sum computed in the write raises the bound to $R>0.50153$. Part~II proves that $\sum u_nz^n$ is the diagonal of a rational power series, hence D-finite, gives an explicit rational constant term, and proves $u_n/A_n=R+S/n+S_2/n^2+(43020\sqrt3/\pi)\log n/n^3+O(n^{-3})$. Part~III extends this expansion to every fixed order, with coefficients affine in $\log n$; the next logarithmic coefficient is $-5041845\sqrt3/\pi$, and every logarithmic coefficient lies in $(\sqrt3/\pi)\mathbb Q$. Part~IV proves $b_n/A_n\to R_{1243}$, with $R_{1243}$ an explicit positive series, rational diagonality and non-algebraicity, and $b_n/A_n=R_{1243}+o(n^{-1/2})$; the value $149/160$ suggested by Conway and Guttmann remains open. Part~V proves an exact four-binomial gluing and a six-term formula for one $12345$, rational diagonality, and $U_n/|\Av_n(12345)|\to R_5>0$, so that $U_n\sim(1536R_5/\pi^{3/2})16^nn^{-15/2}$. Part~VI proves an expansion of that ratio in powers of $1/n$ to every fixed order, with no logarithms. The same method thus has opposite outcomes for $1234$ (logarithms from relative order three) and for $12345$ (none). Each Part also proves inverse-threshold statements with the rounding uncertainty kept inside the ceilings. The amplitudes and the non-logarithmic correction coefficients are given as convergent series, not in closed form, and none is certified numerically beyond lower bounds. The report is unrefereed, and nothing in it is formalized.
\end{abstract}
\hypersetup{pageanchor=true}

\section*{Guide to this report}\phantomsection\label{upo:sec:guide}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Guide to this report}
The six Parts are six manuscripts of the session bundle of Reports 1--243 (arrival commit \texttt{60f54ea06}), placed in this directory by commit \texttt{e85586b7c} (batch~105). They form two chains and one offshoot on one spine, the decomposition of a permutation with a unique occurrence into two halves glued along that occurrence:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth} l l@{}}
\toprule
Part & Report & Delivered title & Sequence & Labels\\
\midrule
I & 134 & Permutations with exactly one increasing subsequence of length four & A217057 & \texttt{upo:amp:}\\
II & 135 & Rational structure and logarithmic asymptotics for A217057 & A217057 & \texttt{upo:rat:}\\
III & 137 (base) & All orders logarithmic asymptotics for A217057 & A217057 & \texttt{upo:all:}\\
IV & 136 & Permutations with exactly one classical 1243 occurrence & A224179 & \texttt{upo:1243:}\\
V & 138 & Exact formulas and leading asymptotics for unique 12345 & A224248 & \texttt{upo:ex5:}\\
VI & 140 & All orders asymptotics and inverse thresholds for unique 12345 & A224248 & \texttt{upo:all5:}\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\emph{Arrangement.} The Parts are printed in order of dependence. Reports 134, 135 and 137 are one chain on one $1234$ occurrence: each cites the one before and was delivered with it as an unchanged companion. The base of the merge is Report~137, the most general of the three; its \file{Report137.tex} was staged as this \file{article.tex}. It is printed as Part~III, after Reports 134 and 135, because it uses their exact gluing identity, global bounds, unitary integral and first expansions as inputs and restates none of their proofs. Report~136 (one $1243$) depends on Reports 134 and 135: it reproves Report~134's tableau formula and fixed-boundary lemma word for word, and adapts Report~135's unitary-integral method; it follows the $1234$ chain as Part~IV. Reports 138 and 140 are the chain for one $12345$, with the same architecture at length five; Report~140 builds on Report~138. They are Parts V and VI. Report~139 of the same batch (one $1432$, an unrelated method) is the separate report \file{a224182-unique-1432-order}.

\emph{Numbering.} Sections are numbered continuously through the six Parts, and statements and equations within sections. The offsets of the section numbers are $0$, $10$, $22$, $29$, $43$ and $54$ for Parts I--VI; Section~\ref{upo:sec:together}, after Part~VI, is the write's synthesis. Reports 135, 137, 138 and 140 number statements within sections, so their Theorem~$k.j$ is Theorem~$(k+\text{offset}).j$ here: Report~135's Theorem~9.1 is Theorem~\ref{upo:rat:thm:secondlog}, Report~137's Theorem~1.1 is Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}, Report~138's Theorem~8.1 is Theorem~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:ratio}, Report~140's Theorem~7.1 is Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:conv}. Reports 134 and 136 number their statements consecutively through the document; here they are renumbered within sections. Report~134's Theorem~1, Lemmas 2,~3, Proposition~4, Lemma~5, Lemmas 6,~7 and Corollary~8 are Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main}, Lemmas \ref{upo:amp:lem:gluing} and~\ref{upo:amp:lem:half}, Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F}, Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed}, Lemmas \ref{upo:amp:lem:domination} and~\ref{upo:amp:lem:middle} and Corollary~\ref{upo:amp:cor:inverse}. Report~136's Theorems 1,~2, Lemmas 3--5, Lemmas 8--10, Proposition~11, Corollary~12, Lemmas 13,~14 and Theorems 15,~16 are Theorems \ref{upo:1243:thm:main} and~\ref{upo:1243:thm:marked}, Lemmas \ref{upo:1243:lem:corner}, \ref{upo:1243:lem:glue}, \ref{upo:1243:lem:half}, Lemmas \ref{upo:1243:lem:tail}, \ref{upo:1243:lem:mismatch}, \ref{upo:1243:lem:triangle}, Proposition~\ref{upo:1243:prop:J}, Corollary~\ref{upo:1243:cor:inverse}, Lemmas \ref{upo:1243:lem:global-rate}, \ref{upo:1243:lem:J-rate} and Theorems \ref{upo:1243:thm:rate}, \ref{upo:1243:thm:profile}; its Proposition~6 and Lemma~7 are Report~134's Proposition~4 and Lemma~5 word for word and are printed once, as Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F} and Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed}. Report~136's Appendix~A is Section~\ref{upo:1243:app:CT}. The manuscripts number equations consecutively through each document; here equations are numbered within sections, so a delivered equation number is not kept, but every delivered equation keeps its label name, with its Part's prefix. Statements added in the write (Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper}, Remarks \ref{upo:amp:rem:numerics}, \ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} and~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy}) are the last numbered statements of their sections, so no delivered statement number moved.

\emph{Notes.} Notes added when the manuscripts were merged are set in small type and tagged \textbf{[write]}, with their date. They give cross-references between the Parts, mark statements that a later Part supersedes, pin citations that a manuscript left implicit, and mark results proved twice. Superseded statements are printed as delivered, with a dated note pointing forward. No delivered statement was changed and no delivered symbol was renamed; where a symbol means different things in different Parts, the notation section below and the table at the head of each Part say which meaning applies. Each Part has, after its own questions, a subsection ``Further questions and research'', which, under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026, records which of that Part's questions a later Part answers and every claim of the Part that is stated without proof; Section~\ref{upo:sec:further} gathers the open problems of the whole report.

\section*{What is proved, and where}\phantomsection\label{upo:sec:status}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What is proved, and where}
\begingroup\small
\begin{longtable}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.55\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.40\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Statement & Where\\
\midrule
\endhead
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\emph{One $1234$ (A217057); $A_n=|\Av_n(1234)|$, $C_0=81\sqrt3/(16\pi)$}}\\
Twelve allowed cells; exact two-half gluing $u_n=\sum H_sH_t\binom{i+k}i\binom{j+l}j$; halves as differences $H_s(i,j)=F_{s+2}(i+1,j+1)-F_{s+1}(i,j)$; three-row tableau formula for $F_m(p,q)$ & Part~I, Lemmas \ref{upo:amp:lem:gluing}, \ref{upo:amp:lem:half}, Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F}\\
$u_n/A_n\to R$, $R$ a series of positive terms, $u_n\sim C_0R\,9^nn^{-4}$ & Part~I, Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main}\\
$\liminf u_n/A_n\ge L_{40,10}>0.50009$ without the existence proof; Conway--Guttmann's $R=1/2$ is false & Part~I, Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:certificate}\\
$\liminf u_n/A_n\ge\Pi_{44}>0.50153$ & \wtag\ Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper}\\
$\sum u_nz^n$ is a rational diagonal over $\mathbb Q$, D-finite; $(u_n)$ P-recursive; a 42-variable rational constant term & Part~II, Theorems \ref{upo:rat:thm:structure} and~\ref{upo:rat:thm:CT}; \wtag\ Remark~\ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} checks the hypotheses of the cited closure theorem\\
Not algebraic & Cited: B\'ona--Burstein \cite[Theorem 6.2]{BB}, see Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:prior}\\
$A_m=C_09^mm^{-4}(1-\frac{11}{2m}+\frac{20}{m^2}+O(m^{-3}))$; uniform boundary expansions & Part~II, Lemma~\ref{upo:rat:lem:avoidance}, \eqref{upo:rat:eq:Asecond}, Proposition~\ref{upo:rat:prop:boundary}, \eqref{upo:rat:eq:Fsecond}\\
$u_n/A_n=R+S/n+S_2/n^2+\kappa_3\log n/n^3+O(n^{-3})$, $\kappa_3=43020\sqrt3/\pi$ & Part~II, Theorems \ref{upo:rat:thm:correction} and~\ref{upo:rat:thm:secondlog}\\
Every fixed order: $u_n/A_n=\sum_{j\le J}(r_j+\kappa_j\log n)n^{-j}+O_J((1+\log n)n^{-J-1})$; $\kappa_4=-5041845\sqrt3/\pi$; $r_3$ exists & Part~III, Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}\\
A coefficient-level convolution theorem in any Banach space (integer exponent $4$, logarithmic models) & Part~III, Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:conv}\\
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\emph{One $1243$ (A224179); the same $A_n$}}\\
Marked-avoider bijection; shared decreasing spine; $b_n=\sum J_k(s)J_k(t)$ & Part~IV, Theorems \ref{upo:1243:thm:marked}, \ref{upo:1243:thm:main}, Lemmas \ref{upo:1243:lem:glue}, \ref{upo:1243:lem:half}\\
$b_n/A_n\to R_{1243}$, a positive series; rational diagonal, D-finite, not algebraic & Part~IV, Theorem~\ref{upo:1243:thm:main}, Sections \ref{upo:1243:sec:leading}--\ref{upo:1243:sec:inverse}\\
$b_n/A_n=R_{1243}+O(n^{-1/2})$, then $+o(n^{-1/2})$ (square-root cancellation); diffusive spine profile $e^{-u^2/2}$ & Part~IV, Theorems \ref{upo:1243:thm:rate} and~\ref{upo:1243:thm:profile}\\
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\emph{One $12345$ (A224248); avoiders A047889, $C_A=1536/\pi^{3/2}$}}\\
Twenty allowed cells, four-binomial gluing, nested boundary bijections, six-term inverse-Cauchy formula & Part~V, Theorems \ref{upo:ex5:thm:gluing}, \ref{upo:ex5:thm:D}, \ref{upo:ex5:thm:six}\\
Rational diagonal, P-recursive & Part~V, Theorem~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:diagonal}; the hypothesis check of Remark~\ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} applies\\
$U_n/|\Av_n(12345)|\to R_5\ge15/32768$, $U_n\sim C_AR_5\,16^nn^{-15/2}$ & Part~V, Theorems \ref{upo:ex5:thm:overview} and~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:ratio}\\
Every fixed order in powers of $1/n$, $r_1=S_5$ explicit; no logarithms & Part~VI, Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:main}\\
A coefficient-level convolution theorem for half-integer exponent $15/2$ & Part~VI, Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:conv}\\
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\emph{All three}}\\
Inverse thresholds with the rounding error inside the ceilings & Corollaries \ref{upo:amp:cor:inverse}, \ref{upo:rat:cor:inverse}, \ref{upo:all:cor:inverse}, \ref{upo:1243:cor:inverse}; Theorems \ref{upo:1243:thm:rate}, \ref{upo:1243:thm:profile}; Proposition~\ref{upo:ex5:prop:inverse}; Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:inverse}\\
The same final step, opposite outcomes for $1234$ and $12345$ & \wtag\ Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy}\\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{@{}p{0.96\textwidth}@{}}{\emph{Open in all Parts.} Closed forms and certified numerical enclosures of $R$, $S$, $S_2$, $r_3$, $R_{1243}$, $R_5$, $S_5$; whether $R_{1243}=149/160$; effective constants and onsets; usable differential equations or recurrences; the corrections for one $1243$ beyond the vanishing square-root term; algebraicity for one $12345$; longer patterns and several occurrences. See Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}.}\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup
Finite computations in all six Parts (exact counts, rational certificates, symbolic identities, mutation tests) are regression checks of formulas and implementations; no asymptotic statement rests on them. The lower bounds $L_{40,10}$, $\Pi_{44}$, $L_{35}$ and $15/32768$ are exact rational evaluations of finitely many terms of proved series of nonnegative terms; they are rigorous given the proofs. The inverse statements are instances of the transseries volume's generic inverse theorems (the front-matter section \hyperref[upo:sec:neighbours]{Relation to neighbouring reports}); no new inversion theory is claimed.

\section*{Notation across the six Parts}\phantomsection\label{upo:sec:notation}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Notation across the six Parts}
The three counts are $u_n$ (one $1234$, Parts I--III), $b_n$ (one $1243$, Part~IV) and $U_n$ (one $12345$, Parts V--VI). In the write's own text the two avoidance sequences are $A^{(4)}_n=|\Av_n(1234)|$ and $A^{(5)}_n=|\Av_n(12345)|$, and the three amplitudes are $R=R_{1234}$, $R_{1243}$ and $R_5$; the Parts themselves write $A_n$ and $R$ in each case. Common to Parts I--IV: $F_m(p,q)$, the half counts $H_s(i,j)$, $d_p=\binom{p+2}2 3^{-p}$, $C(i,j)=81d_{i+1}d_{j+1}-9d_id_j$, $C_0=81\sqrt3/(16\pi)$, $\lambda=\log 9$. Common to Parts V--VI: the boundary vector $v=(i_2,i_3;j_2,j_3)$, the kernel $K(v,w)$ and pairing $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_K$, $G_{P,R}$, the set $\Tset$ of six partitions, $h(v)$, $C_A=1536/\pi^{3/2}$, $p=15/2$, $\lambda=\log16$. The symbols below change meaning between the Parts or inside one; each Part's text fixes its meaning there.

Also integration variables $A,B,C$ in the constant terms (Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:kernel}, Section~\ref{upo:1243:app:CT}, which says they are ``unrelated to the earlier sequence''), and alphabets $A,B,C,D$ in Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:sym}.\\

Part~VI: $\bfU_j$ (bold) the corresponding arrays at length five; $U\in U(4)$. Parts I and IV: $U$ is a word in $U\,1\,V\,2\,W$; Part~IV's appendix: $U_r=c(q_r)$.\\

Part~V: $w$ a second boundary vector and $w(v)$ the domination weight (both in \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:mass}). Part~VI: $w$ a formal variable; $\omega(v)$ the weight.\\
$d$ & Parts I--IV: $d_p=\binom{p+2}23^{-p}$. Part~IV: also $d_i$ counts of allowed values (Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:marked}). Part~III: $d=\log M_0+4\log\lambda$. Part~V: $d,e$ degrees; $d_i$ in the determinant expansion. Part~VI: $d=P-t$ a degree, and in Section~\ref{upo:all5:sec:inverse} the constant $d=\log M+p\log\lambda$ (it warns).\\
$\kappa$ & Part~II: $\kappa=43020\sqrt3/\pi$. Part~III: $\kappa_j$ the logarithmic coefficients ($\kappa_3$ is Part~II's $\kappa$). Part~IV: $\kappa=\log9$ in Corollary~\ref{upo:1243:cor:inverse}. Part~VI: $\kappa_\nu$ a Casimir value.\\
$\alpha$ & Part~II: $\alpha=-11/2$, and $\alpha\in\{0,1\}$ in \eqref{upo:rat:eq:Qencoding}. Parts III and VI: $\alpha_j$ avoidance coefficients ($\alpha_1=-11/2$ for $1234$, $-135/8$ for $12345$). Part~V: partitions $\alpha$. Part~VI: $\alpha=p-1=13/2$.\\
$S$ & Parts II--III: $S=r_1$, $S_2=r_2$ for $1234$. Part~VI: $S_5=r_1$ for $12345$. Part~I: $S_k^{(I)}$ truncated transforms. Part~III: $S_K(z)$. Part~VI: $S_n$ a model sum.\\
$E$, $D$, $J$ & $E_k$, $D_k$, $J_k$, $K_k$ binomial transforms (Parts I--II); $\mathcal D(x)$, $\mathcal D_2(i,j)$, $\mathcal E$ (Part~II). Part~III: $E_r$ the Banach space, $E_j(s;p,q)$. Part~IV: $J_k(s)$ the spine half sums (not Part~II's $J_k$), $D(x)$ a dimension, $D$ a constant and a mismatch. Part~V: $D_M(P,R;Q,T)$, $E=2M-P-Q$. Part~VI: $E=E_{x,y}$ the Banach space, $D(\theta)$ the density factor, $D(F)$ a dimension.\\
$C$ & Parts I--IV: $C(i,j)$; Part~IV: $C=C_0R$ in Corollary~\ref{upo:1243:cor:inverse}. Part~V: $C_5=C_AR_5$. Constants $C,c,K$ vary within proofs.\\
$N$ & Thresholds (above); Part~I: $N=n-4$ and $N_*$, $D_*$ in the certificate; Part~V: $N=n-5$; Part~III: $N_j(p,q)$.\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\endgroup

\section*{Provenance and merge decisions}\phantomsection\label{upo:sec:provenance}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Provenance and merge decisions}
Six manuscripts of the session bundle of Reports 1--243, all dated 2~October 2026. Their title blocks read ``Report~134'', \ldots, ``Report~140''; none names an author, a tool or an addressee (each sets an empty PDF author field), and none carries ``prepared for private review'' wording. None pins a ProveIt commit or names a repository path. They arrived in commit \texttt{60f54ea06} and were placed by \texttt{e85586b7c} (batch~105).
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Part~I}, Report~134, archive \file{A217057_Unique_1234_Asymptotics_and_Conjecture_Refutation_Source.zip} (\file{Report134.tex}, 791 lines, 12 pages). Contributes the exact two-half decomposition, the tableau formula, the fixed-boundary limits, the finite certificate refuting $R=1/2$, the uniform domination, the series for $R$ and the first inverse theorem.
\item \textbf{Part~II}, Report~135, archive \file{A217057_Rational_Structure_Logarithmic_Asymptotics_and_Inversion_Source.zip} (\file{Report135.tex}, 1455 lines, 22 pages). Its archive carries Report~134's eight files under \file{companion134/} (byte copies). Contributes rational diagonality and D-finiteness, the explicit constant term, the unitary-integral expansions, $S$, $S_2$, the first logarithm and the sharper inverse.
\item \textbf{Part~III}, Report~137, archive \file{A217057_All_Orders_Logarithmic_Asymptotics_and_Inversion_Source.zip} (\file{Report137.tex}, 812 lines, 13 pages). The base. Its archive carries Report~135's twenty files, Report~134's included, under \file{companion135/} (byte copies). Contributes the expansion to every order, $\kappa_4$, the existence of $r_3$ with a regularized-series recipe, a general convolution theorem, and the inverse to every order.
\item \textbf{Part~IV}, Report~136, archive \file{A224179_Leading_Amplitude_Diffusive_Cancellation_and_Inversion_Source.zip} (\file{Report136.tex}, 1737 lines, 24 pages). Cites Reports 134 and 135 and reproves two statements of Report~134 word for word. Contributes the marked-avoider bijection, the spine decomposition, $R_{1243}$, rational diagonality, non-algebraicity, the rate and the square-root cancellation, and a 40-variable constant term.
\item \textbf{Part~V}, Report~138, archive \file{A224248_Exact_Formulas_Leading_Asymptotics_and_Inversion_Source.zip} (\file{Report138.tex}, 1159 lines, 17 pages). Cites none of Reports 134--137. Contributes the length-five gluing, the boundary bijections, the six-term formula, rational diagonality and the leading ratio $R_5$.
\item \textbf{Part~VI}, Report~140, archive \file{A224248_All_Orders_Asymptotics_and_Inverse_Thresholds_Source.zip} (\file{Report140.tex}, 1122 lines, 18 pages). Its archive carries the whole Report~138 archive as \file{dependencies/Report138_reproducible.zip}, byte-identical to the standalone delivery (SHA-256 checked at placement). Contributes the expansion to every order without logarithms, $S_5$, a half-integer convolution theorem and the inverse to every order.
\end{itemize}
Where the merge had to choose:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Order and base.} Dependency order, with the base (Report~137) third; the $1243$ Part after the $1234$ chain because it depends on it; the $12345$ chain last. Later Parts improve earlier statements; the earlier statements stay, with dated notes.
\item \emph{Verbatim repetition.} Report~136's Sections~4 and~5 (Proposition~6 and Lemma~7 with their proofs) are Report~134's Sections~4 and~5 character for character, except that Report~136 writes $B_p$ for Report~134's $c_p$ and closes Section~4 with a paragraph of its own (checked by the merge script); they are printed once, in Part~I, and Sections \ref{upo:1243:sec:tableaux} and~\ref{upo:1243:sec:fixed} keep their titles, Report~136's own closing paragraph and a pointer. Report~135's Section~2 and Report~137's Section~1 restate inputs from the earlier reports in their own words, and Report~140's Section~2 restates inputs from Report~138; they are printed in full, because they fix the statements each Part uses, with notes naming the proved statements they summarize.
\item \emph{Second routes.} Part~II's Proposition~\ref{upo:rat:prop:boundary} and Part~IV's Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:global-rate} prove boundary expansions by the same unitary-integral method (Part~IV's is weaker and global; Part~IV says it is adapted from Part~II). Part~IV's Gaussian strip bounds (Lemmas \ref{upo:1243:lem:tail}--\ref{upo:1243:lem:triangle}) refine Part~I's Cauchy--Schwarz bound \eqref{upo:amp:eq:CS} by a mismatch factor. Part~VI's Proposition~\ref{upo:all5:prop:first-ratio} reproves $r_1=S_5$ of its own Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:main} by an elementary route. All are printed.
\item \emph{Front matter, appendix and bibliography.} The six title blocks and abstracts are printed at the heads of their Parts; one table of contents replaces those of Reports 138 and 140. Report~136's Appendix~A is printed as Section~\ref{upo:1243:app:CT}, the last section of Part~IV. The six bibliographies are merged into one, keeping every detail of every entry; entries for Reports 134, 135 and 138 point to Parts I, II and V.
\item \emph{Write additions.} Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper} with its exact rational, Remarks \ref{upo:amp:rem:numerics}, \ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} and~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy}, the further-questions subsections and Section~\ref{upo:sec:together}, the dated notes, the reading tables and this front matter. Everything else is delivered text.
\end{enumerate}

\section*{Relation to neighbouring reports}\phantomsection\label{upo:sec:neighbours}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Relation to neighbouring reports}
All neighbours named here are in \file{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/}. No other report treats A217057, A224179 or A224248; the only other report on a prescribed number of pattern occurrences is the sibling below (searched 5~October 2026).

\emph{The sibling for $1432$.} \file{generating-functions-and-asymptotics/oeis-sequence-asymptotics/a224182-unique-1432-order}, placed in the same batch from Report~139, proves that exactly one $1432$ is $\Theta(9^nn^{-3})$, normalized by the same $A_n=|\Av_n(1432)|=|\Av_n(1234)|$, by a minima-skeleton argument that shares nothing with the half decompositions here; Conway and Guttmann treat $1234$, $1243$ and $1432$ in consecutive sections. Neither manuscript cites the other.

\emph{Longest increasing subsequences.} \file{generating-functions-and-asymptotics/oeis-sequence-asymptotics/a047874-long-increasing-subsequences} studies the counts of permutations by the length of a longest increasing subsequence; $A^{(4)}_n$ and $A^{(5)}_n$ are its counts for length at most $3$ and $4$, and both reports use RSK and strip tableaux. It studies a different regime (length near $N/2$); a cross-reference only.

\emph{Inverse thresholds.} Every inverse statement here is an instance of the generic theorems of the transseries volume, \file{Analysis/Transseries/docs/series-and-transseries/Transseries_And_Inversion/transseries_and_inversion.tex}: \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} (parts (1) and~(2): the integer threshold is the ceiling of any admissible interpolated inverse, and an approximation within $\eta$ fixes it when the two ceilings agree) and, for Part~I's $W_{-1}$ formula, \texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core}. The Parts prove their inverse statements directly; the notes at each say which part of these theorems it instantiates.

\emph{Formal status.} No statement of this report is formalized: no Lean or Rocq file in the repository concerns permutation patterns, A217057, A224179 or A224248 (searched 5~October 2026). Placement in the \file{SetTheory/Cardinals} report collection confers no formal status.
\clearpage
\part{Permutations with exactly one increasing subsequence of length four}\label{upo:amp:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~134, archive \file{A217057_Unique_1234_Asymptotics_and_Conjecture_Refutation_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{Report134.tex} (12 pages), dated 2~October 2026; title block ``Permutations with exactly one increasing subsequence of length four'', author line ``Report 134''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} notes. Report~134 numbers its statements consecutively; here they are numbered within sections (its Theorem~1 is Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main}, its Corollary~8 is Corollary~\ref{upo:amp:cor:inverse}). Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper}, Remark~\ref{upo:amp:rem:numerics} and Section~\ref{upo:amp:sub:further} are added in the write.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.44\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~I & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$u_n$: exactly one $1234$ (A217057); $A_n=|\Av_n(1234)|$ & Part~IV: $b_n$ (one $1243$); Parts V--VI: $U_n$ (one $12345$), and $A_n=|\Av_n(12345)|$\\
$R=\lim u_n/A_n$, the amplitude & Part~IV: $R=R_{1243}$; Parts V--VI: $R$ a boundary parameter, amplitude $R_5$\\
$H_s(i,j)$ half counts; $c_p=\binom{p+2}2$ & Part~IV: $H_h(i,j)$, $B_p$; Parts V--VI: $H_s(v)$, another object\\
$P_k$, $Q_k$ polynomials \eqref{upo:amp:eq:PQ}; $B_k^{(I)}$, $\mathcal B$ & Parts II--IV: $P=\tr U$, $Q=\sum\theta^2$; Parts II--III: $B(i,j)$\\
$b_i=(i+1)^43^{-i}$ (Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:domination}); $e_t$ \eqref{upo:amp:eq:et} & Part~III: models $b_k(n)$; Part~IV: counts $b_n$ and its own $e_t$\\
$\tau(T)$, $L=\log T$, $x(T)$, $y(T)$, $K_0=C_0R$ & Part~III: $N(T)$ with $y=\log T$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{partabstract}
Let $u_n$ count permutations of $[n]$ with exactly one classical occurrence of
$1234$, and let $A_n$ count $1234$-avoiders. We prove
$u_n\sim R A_n\sim (81\sqrt3/(16\pi))R\,9^n n^{-4}$, where $R$ is an
explicit convergent positive series of exactly computable tableau counts.
A finite rational lower bound gives $R>0.50009$, contradicting the conjecture
$R=1/2$ stated by Conway and Guttmann. The argument separates an exact
two-half decomposition, a finite lower-bound certificate, and the uniform
estimates needed for existence of the limit. It also gives the inverse
threshold consequence justified by a relative equivalent. No closed form,
certified decimal approximation to $R$, or higher-order expansion is claimed.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Later Parts go beyond the limits this Part states: Part~II proves rational diagonality, the first two corrections and the first logarithmic term (Theorems \ref{upo:rat:thm:structure}, \ref{upo:rat:thm:correction} and~\ref{upo:rat:thm:secondlog}), and Part~III the expansion to every fixed order (Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}). No closed form and no certified decimal of $R$ is known anywhere in the report; Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper} sharpens the lower bound.
\end{writenote}


\section{The problem and the main result}\label{upo:amp:sec:intro}
An occurrence of $1234$ in $\pi\in S_n$ is a choice of positions
$r_1<r_2<r_3<r_4$ with
$\pi_{r_1}<\pi_{r_2}<\pi_{r_3}<\pi_{r_4}$. Positions need not be consecutive.
We count permutations, not marked occurrences or equivalence classes.
Set $u_n=0$ for $0\le n<4$ and $A_0=1$. The sequence $u_n$ is
OEIS A217057~\cite{OEIS217057}; for $n=4,\ldots,15$ it is
\begin{gather*}
1,12,102,770,5545,39220,276144,1948212,\\
13817680,98679990,710108396,5150076076.
\end{gather*}
Regev's strip-sum theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:regev}
A_n\sim C_0\frac{9^n}{n^4},\qquad C_0=\frac{81\sqrt3}{16\pi}.
\end{equation}
The specialization is $d=1$, three rows and exponent $\alpha=2$ in
\cite[Section 3.1.4]{Regev2010}: its constant is
$(2\pi)^{-1}2^{-4}3^{9/2}2!=C_0$.
That paper confirms this ordinary strip case of the 1981 result~\cite{Regev1981};
the correction it discusses concerns $d>1$.

Here are the finite quantities defining our answer. Let $F_m(p,q)$ count
$1234$-avoiders of size $m$ whose final $p$ entries are decreasing and whose
$q$ smallest values occur in decreasing order. We use $0\le p,q\le m$;
parameters zero or one impose no restriction. For $s,i,j\in\N$, define
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:H}
H_s(i,j)=F_{s+2}(i+1,j+1)-F_{s+1}(i,j)\quad(i+j\le s),
\end{equation}
and set $H_s(i,j)=0$ outside that triangle. Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:tableaux}
gives a finite integer formula for every $F_m(p,q)$.
Put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:PQ}
P_k=k^2+15k+38,\qquad Q_k=k^2+11k+18.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}\label{upo:amp:thm:main}
The limit $R=\lim_{n\to\infty}u_n/A_n$ exists, is finite and positive, and is
given by the absolutely convergent series
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:R}
R=\frac1{10368}\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}9^{-t}
\sum_{\substack{k,l\ge0\\k+l\le t}}
H_t(k,l)\left(\frac32\right)^{k+l}
\bigl(P_kP_l-Q_kQ_l\bigr).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:main}
u_n\sim C_0R\frac{9^n}{n^4}.
\end{equation}
Moreover $R\ge L_{40,10}>50009/100000>1/2$, where
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:certificate}
L_{40,10}=
\frac{6958884420052382006187220194732220723168380665062}
{13915193059764305937984450503671774362956903094027}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

Every coefficient $H_t(k,l)$ is nonnegative, and the factor in parentheses
in~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:R} is strictly positive. Thus every nonzero summand is positive.
The dimensionless ratio $R$ must be distinguished from the leading
coefficient $C_0R$.
\begin{writenote}[The refutation on record, 5 October 2026]
The conjecture is Conway and Guttmann's \cite{CG}. In arXiv:2306.12682v2, Section~5.1 (printed page~19 of the journal version), they estimate the limit of the ratio from about 200 known terms, find it ``plausibly 0.5'', and conjecture that the ratio of one-occurrence to avoiding counts is $1/2$ for $1234$, equivalently $u_n\sim81\sqrt3\,9^n/(32\pi n^4)$ (read 5~October 2026). Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main} refutes it. The refutation uses only Lemmas \ref{upo:amp:lem:gluing}, \ref{upo:amp:lem:half} and~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed} and the nonnegativity of every summand: Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:certificate} proves $\liminf u_n/A_n\ge L_{40,10}$ without assuming that the limit exists, and \eqref{upo:amp:eq:gap} certifies $L_{40,10}>1/2$ in exact rational arithmetic. The proof is printed in full in Sections \ref{upo:amp:sec:gluing}--\ref{upo:amp:sec:certificate}. Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper} raises the bound to $0.50153$, and Remark~\ref{upo:amp:rem:numerics} explains why extrapolation from the data pointed to $1/2$. No text in the repository repeated the conjecture before this report, so none needed correction.
\end{writenote}

\paragraph{What was known and what is established here.}
B\'ona and Burstein's inequalities already imply
$u_n=\Theta(9^n/n^4)$ by symmetry and Regev's theorem
\cite[Section 6, equation (6.1)]{BB}. They also prove nonalgebraicity of the
ordinary generating function in this case. Catalytic functional-equation
algorithms of Nakamura and Zeilberger enumerate prescribed small occurrence
counts and include this sequence~\cite{NZ}. Conway and Guttmann give further
enumeration and asymptotic analysis~\cite{CG}. In the published journal
version, Section 5.1, printed page 19, they explicitly conjecture
$u_n/A_n\to1/2$, equivalently leading coefficient $81\sqrt3/(32\pi)$.
Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main} disproves that specific amplitude and establishes a
leading equivalent. The existence proof and the finite disproof are given
separately below. This report makes no worldwide priority claim and no
claim about their other pattern or occurrence-count conjectures.
\begin{writenote}[Citations pinned, 5 October 2026]
Checked against arXiv:2101.00332v3, the preprint of the published \cite{BB}: the $\Theta$ statement is their equation~(6.1); the nonalgebraicity is their Theorem~6.2, stated for even $k>2$ and proved by their Lemma~6.3; their Theorem~5.3 proves nonrationality for every $k\ge3$. Report~134 cites the arXiv version, Reports 135, 136 and 138 the published one; the merged bibliography gives both.
\end{writenote}

\section{The twelve allowed cells and exact gluing}\label{upo:amp:sec:gluing}
Fix the unique increasing four-chain, with entries $c_1<c_2<c_3<c_4$ in
their position order. For a nonchain entry, let $a$ be the number of chain
positions before it, and $b$ the number of chain values below it. Thus
$(a,b)\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}^2$. The longest increasing chain using this entry and
only distinguished-chain entries has length
\[
\min(a,b)+1+4-\max(a,b)=5-|a-b|.
\]
If $|a-b|\le1$, it contains an increasing four-chain different from the
distinguished one. All extra entries therefore lie in
\begin{align*}
\mathcal N&=\{(0,2),(0,3),(0,4),(1,3),(1,4),(2,4)\},\\
\mathcal S&=\{(2,0),(3,0),(3,1),(4,0),(4,1),(4,2)\}.
\end{align*}
The chain together with its $\mathcal N$ entries is a \emph{northern half};
the analogous restriction is a \emph{southern half}. Each half contains
exactly its distinguished four-chain. Reverse-complement, sending a point
to its reflection in both coordinates, exchanges the half types.

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.07cm,y=1.07cm,font=\small]
\fill[gray!12] (0,0) rectangle (5,5);
\foreach \a/\b in {0/2,0/3,0/4,1/3,1/4,2/4}
  {\fill[blue!14] (\a,\b) rectangle ++(1,1);}
\foreach \a/\b in {2/0,3/0,3/1,4/0,4/1,4/2}
  {\fill[orange!18] (\a,\b) rectangle ++(1,1);}
\draw[gray!55,step=1] (0,0) grid (5,5);
\foreach \a/\b in {0/3,0/4,1/3,1/4}
  {\node at (\a+.5,\b+.5) {$\mathcal N$};}
\foreach \a/\b in {3/0,3/1,4/0,4/1}
  {\node at (\a+.5,\b+.5) {$\mathcal S$};}
\node[align=center] at (.5,2.5) {$\mathcal N$\\$j$};
\node[align=center] at (2.5,4.5) {$\mathcal N$\\$i$};
\node[align=center] at (2.5,.5) {$\mathcal S$\\$k$};
\node[align=center] at (4.5,2.5) {$\mathcal S$\\$l$};
\foreach \r in {1,...,4}{
  \fill (\r,\r) circle (2pt);
  \node[anchor=south east,inner sep=3pt] at (\r,\r) {$c_{\r}$};}
\foreach \r in {0,...,4}{
  \node[anchor=north] at (\r+.5,-.07) {$\r$};
  \node[anchor=east] at (-.07,\r+.5) {$\r$};}
\node at (2.5,-.72) {position gap $a$};
\node[rotate=90] at (-.75,2.5) {value gap $b$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Only the twelve colored cells with $|a-b|\ge2$ can contain extra
entries. The four black dots are the distinguished chain. The marked
boundary cells have sizes $i,j,k,l$. A half must itself have exactly one
four-chain; the cells are not independent unrestricted avoider classes.}
\label{upo:amp:fig:cells}
\end{figure}

Let a northern half have $s$ extra points, $i$ of them in $(2,4)$ and $j$
in $(0,2)$. For a southern half with $t$ extra points, define $k,l$ by
reflection: they count $(2,0)$ and $(4,2)$, respectively.

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:amp:lem:gluing}
Writing $H_s(i,j)$ for the number of northern halves with those parameters,
the number of full permutations is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:gluing}
u_n=\sum_{s+t=n-4}\ \sum_{i,j,k,l\ge0}
H_s(i,j)H_t(k,l)\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The two halves share only position gap $a=2$ and value gap $b=2$.
In the former, merge the $i$ northern and $k$ southern positions while
preserving each half's order. There are $\binom{i+k}{i}$ choices.
In the latter, merge the $j$ northern and $l$ southern values, giving
$\binom{j+l}{j}$ choices. These involve four distinct cells and are
independent; all other cross-half comparisons are forced. Total position
and value orders determine one permutation, and both shuffles can be
recovered from it.

It remains to check uniqueness of the four-chain after gluing. Each of the
four marked cells in Figure~\ref{upo:amp:fig:cells} is decreasing: an increasing
pair there would combine with two distinguished points to make another
four-chain in its half. An increasing pair from $\mathcal N$ to $\mathcal S$
can only run from $(0,2)$ to $(4,2)$. An increasing pair in the other
direction can only run from $(2,0)$ to $(2,4)$. In either case no further
point of either half can extend the mixed pair in an increasing chain:
any additional opposite-half pair is constrained to the same two cells,
and within either cell there is no increasing pair. Thus a mixed increasing
chain has at most two extra entries.

Nor can a distinguished point join such a mixed pair. In the first case
both values are between $c_2,c_3$, while the two positions are before all
and after all chain positions. In the second case both positions are
between those of $c_2,c_3$, while their values are below all and above all
chain values. These comparisons exclude a distinguished point before,
between, or after the pair. Hence every four-chain is contained in a half,
and its distinguished one is the only possibility.

Finally the unique chain is determined by the full permutation, so
restriction recovers the two halves uniquely. There is no marking
multiplicity or division factor. Summing the shuffle counts proves the
formula.
\end{proof}

\section{A half is a difference of boundary restricted avoiders}\label{upo:amp:sec:halves}
\begin{lemma}\label{upo:amp:lem:half}
The half count in Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:gluing} is exactly~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:H}. In
particular it is nonnegative and vanishes unless $i+j\le s$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Standardize a northern half of total size $s+4$. Its first two chain
values are $1,2$, and its last two chain positions have values $c,d$.
The $j$ points in $(0,2)$ have values $3,\ldots,j+2$; hence $c=j+3$.
Its form is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:halfword}
U\,1\,V\,2\,W\,c\,d.
\end{equation}
Here $W$ is decreasing of length $i$, all its values exceed $d$, the values
$3,\ldots,j+2$ occur decreasingly in $U$, and every value in $V$ exceeds
$c$. These facts follow from the cell geometry and the decreasingness of
the two marked northern cells.

Delete $2,c$ and standardize to a permutation $\beta$ of size $s+2$.
It avoids $1234$, since the only original four-chain has been broken.
Its last $i+1$ entries, corresponding to $W,d$, are decreasing; its
$j+1$ smallest values occur decreasingly; and its final value is greater
than $j+1$.

Conversely let $\beta$ be an avoider with these three properties.
Its decreasing suffix of length $i+1$ contains none of its $j+1$ smallest
values, because its last and therefore smallest suffix value exceeds
$j+1$. In particular its value $1$ is before that suffix. Insert a new
value $2$ immediately before the specified suffix, then a new value
$c=j+3$ immediately before the last entry, increasing old values as
needed to preserve their relative order. The result has
form~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:halfword}. The low values $3,\ldots,j+2$ lie decreasingly
before $1$; all entries between $1$ and the inserted $2$ exceed $c$;
and $W$ is decreasing above $d>c$.

A four-chain using neither inserted value would have existed in $\beta$.
A chain using the inserted $2$ has at most the single smaller predecessor
$1$, and the only increasing pair to its right is $c,d$. A chain using
the inserted $c$ has at most the single successor $d$, and the only
increasing pair of smaller entries to its left is $1,2$. Thus the new
permutation has exactly the chain $1,2,c,d$. The two operations are
inverse, including when either boundary condition holds for a longer
block than required.

It remains to exclude the avoiders whose final value is at most $j+1$.
Since their $j+1$ smallest values occur decreasingly, such a final value
must be $1$. Deleting this final minimum changes the suffix condition
from $i+1$ to $i$ and the small-value condition from $j+1$ to $j$.
Appending a new minimum reverses this operation and never makes a new
increasing four-chain. The excluded number is therefore $F_{s+1}(i,j)$,
giving~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:H}.

In the retained class the last $i+1$ entries and the $j+1$ smallest values
are disjoint. Their combined size $i+j+2$ is at most $s+2$, proving
triangular support. In particular $H_0(0,0)=1$ and
$H_s(0,0)=A_{s+2}-A_{s+1}$.
\end{proof}

\section{The exact tableau formula}\label{upo:amp:sec:tableaux}
For a partition $\lambda$, let $f^\lambda$ denote the number of standard
Young tableaux of its diagram. A vertical strip has at most one box per
row; a horizontal strip has at most one box per column. Define
\[
V_p(\lambda)=\sum_{\substack{\mu\subseteq\lambda\\
\lambda/\mu\text{ a vertical }p\text{-strip}}}f^\mu.
\]
\begin{proposition}\label{upo:amp:prop:F}
For $0\le p,q\le m$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:F}
F_m(p,q)=\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash m\\\lambda_1\le3}}
V_p(\lambda)V_q(\lambda).
\end{equation}
After transposition, use three-row shapes $\rho=(a,b,c)$, with
$a\ge b\ge c\ge0$ and $a+b+c=m$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:W}
W_p(\rho)=
\sum_{\substack{a\ge u\ge b\ge v\ge c\ge w\ge0\\u+v+w=m-p}}
f^{(u,v,w)}.
\end{equation}
Then $F_m(p,q)=\sum_{\rho\vdash m,\,\ell(\rho)\le3}W_p(\rho)W_q(\rho)$,
where
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:hook}
f^{(u,v,w)}=
\frac{(u+v+w)!(u-v+1)(u-w+2)(v-w+1)}
{(u+2)!(v+1)!w!}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Ordinary row-insertion RSK is a bijection from permutations to pairs
$(P,Q)$ of standard tableaux of the same shape $\lambda$. The longest
increasing subsequence has length $\lambda_1$, so avoidance is precisely
$\lambda_1\le3$. The descent correspondences are
$\Des(\pi)=\Des(Q)$ and $\Des(\pi^{-1})=\Des(P)$, where a tableau descent
means that the next label is in a strictly lower row.

A decreasing suffix of length $p$ requires the last $p$ labels of $Q$ to
occupy strictly increasing row numbers. Their deletion leaves a vertical
$p$-strip. Conversely, any such strip has exactly one admissible filling
by these labels, in row order from top to bottom. It is standard: any
retained box has a smaller label, and the retained Young diagram has no
box to the right of a removed box in its row or below it in its column.
Comparisons between removed boxes in a column follow the row order.
Hence there are $V_p(\lambda)$ choices
for $Q$.

The $q$ smallest values of $\pi$ occurring decreasingly means that
every index $1,\ldots,q-1$ is a descent of $P$, so these $q$ labels occupy
its first column. The remaining number is the skew-tableau dimension
$f^{\lambda/(1^q)}$. For completeness, the vertical Pieri rule says that
the coefficient of $s_\lambda$ in $s_\mu e_q$ is one if
$\lambda/\mu$ is a vertical $q$-strip and zero otherwise. Taking the
adjoint gives
$s_{\lambda/(1^q)}=\sum_{\lambda/\mu\text{ vertical }q}s_\mu$.
In homogeneous degree $m-q$, the exponential specialization sends
$s_\nu$ to $f^\nu/(m-q)!$, and a skew Schur function to its skew
standard-tableau count divided by the same factorial. It follows that
$f^{\lambda/(1^q)}=V_q(\lambda)$. The cases $p=0$ or $q=0$ have empty
strips and the same interpretation.

Conditional on the common shape, $P,Q$ are independent choices. This
proves~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:F}. Transposition changes vertical strips to horizontal
ones and preserves tableau dimensions. For three-row shapes, the
horizontal-strip condition is exactly the interlacing in~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:W}.
Finally~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:hook} is the three-row hook-length formula, valid also
with zero parts and for the empty shape.
\end{proof}

These formulas are finite and exact. They also show
$A_m=F_m(0,0)=\sum_{\ell(\rho)\le3}(f^\rho)^2$.
The remaining argument requires more than coefficient agreement: first a
fixed-boundary limit, then a bound uniform in every boundary parameter.

\section{Fixed boundary asymptotics}\label{upo:amp:sec:fixed}
Put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:d}
c_p=\binom{p+2}{2},\qquad d_p=c_p3^{-p}.
\end{equation}
\begin{lemma}\label{upo:amp:lem:fixed}
For every fixed $p,q\in\N$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:fixed}
\frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_m}\longrightarrow d_pd_q.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for fixed $i,j$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Climit}
\frac{H_s(i,j)}{A_s}\longrightarrow
C(i,j):=81d_{i+1}d_{j+1}-9d_id_j>0.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Give the three-row partitions $\rho=(a,b,c)$ of $m$ probability
$(f^\rho)^2/A_m$. Fix $\eta=1/20$. We show that with probability tending
to one,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:good}
\max\{|a-m/3|,|b-m/3|,|c-m/3|\}\le m^{1/2+\eta},
\quad a-b>m^\eta,\quad b-c>m^\eta.
\end{equation}
Assigning the row of every tableau label gives
$f^\rho\le\binom m{a,b,c}$. For any triple outside the balance window,
the multinomial coefficient is at most
$3^m\exp(-c_*m^{2\eta})$ for some fixed $c_*>0$ and sufficiently large
$m$. One elementary justification is to regard
$3^{-m}\binom m{a,b,c}$ as a multinomial probability; some coordinate is
outside its $m/3$ mean by more than $m^{1/2+\eta}$, and the binomial
Chernoff bound applies. There are $O(m^2)$ triples. Their squared
dimensions have total
$O(m^2 9^m e^{-2c_*m^{2\eta}})=o(A_m)$ by~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:regev}.

Inside the window, all row lengths are of order $m$ and the multinomial
coefficient is uniformly $O(3^m/m)$, for example by the factorial bounds
in Stirling's formula. Rewrite~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:hook} as
\[
f^{(a,b,c)}=\binom m{a,b,c}
\frac{(a-b+1)(a-c+2)(b-c+1)}{(a+2)(a+1)(b+1)}.
\]
If either adjacent gap is at most $m^\eta$, the squared product of the
three numerator factors is $O(m^{2+6\eta})$. There are
$O(m^{1/2+2\eta})$ such shapes: choose the small gap in $O(m^\eta)$
ways and one row in a window of size $O(m^{1/2+\eta})$, with the third
row fixed by the total. Their total squared weight is therefore
\[
O\left(9^m m^{-2}m^{-6}m^{2+6\eta}
m^{1/2+2\eta}\right)
=O(9^m m^{-11/2+8\eta})=o(A_m).
\]
This proves~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:good}.

For fixed $p$, every weak composition
$\delta_1+\delta_2+\delta_3=p$ gives a legal horizontal strip removal
$\rho/(\rho-\delta)$ on~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:good}, once $m$ is large. The adjacent
gaps and last row eventually exceed $p$, which proves all interlacing
inequalities. These are all possible removals, numbering $c_p$.
The exact ratio of dimensions is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:ratiohook}
\frac{f^{\rho-\delta}}{f^\rho}=
\frac{(m-p)!}{m!}
(a+2)_{\underline{\delta_1}}
(b+1)_{\underline{\delta_2}}
c_{\underline{\delta_3}}
\frac{\Delta(\rho-\delta)}{\Delta(\rho)},
\end{equation}
where $\Delta(a,b,c)=(a-b+1)(a-c+2)(b-c+1)$ and underlining denotes
a falling factorial. Uniformly on the good shapes, these three factors
are respectively asymptotic to $m^{-p}$, $(m/3)^p$, and $1$.
Thus $W_p(\rho)/f^\rho\to d_p$ uniformly there.

For all shapes, $0\le W_p(\rho)/f^\rho\le1$, since the untransposed
strip sum counts a subset of standard tableaux with a specified suffix
descent condition. Express~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:F} divided by $A_m$ as the
expectation of the product of the two ratios. Its difference from
$d_pd_q$ is bounded by a good-event supremum error tending to zero plus
a constant times the probability of the bad event. This proves
\eqref{upo:amp:eq:fixed} without assuming a fixed probability space.

Finally $A_{s+h}/A_s\to9^h$ for fixed $h$ by~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:regev}, which
proves~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Climit}. The strict positivity follows from
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Cpositive}
C(i,j)=9d_id_j
\left(\frac{(i+3)(j+3)}{(i+1)(j+1)}-1\right)>0.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\section{A finite certificate already rules out one half}\label{upo:amp:sec:certificate}
This section does not assume existence of $R$. Fix nonnegative integers
$T,I$. In~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:gluing}, retain only terms with one half size $t\le T$
and the other half's two boundary parameters at most $I$. For
$n>2T+4$ the two endpoint ranges are disjoint. They contribute equally
by exchanging the halves. All terms are nonnegative. For each retained
fixed $t,k,l,i,j$, Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed} gives
\[
\frac{H_{n-4-t}(i,j)}{A_n}\longrightarrow9^{-t-4}C(i,j).
\]
Taking a limit in this finite sum proves
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:finiteL}
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\frac{u_n}{A_n}\ge L_{T,I}:=
2\sum_{t=0}^{T}9^{-t-4}
\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)
\sum_{i,j=0}^{I}C(i,j)\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}.
\end{equation}
No infinite interchange or boundary uniformity is needed for this step.

For exact computation define
\[
S_k^{(I)}=\sum_{i=0}^{I}\binom{i+k}{i}d_i,
\qquad B_k^{(I)}=\sum_{i=0}^{I}\binom{i+k}{i}d_{i+1}.
\]
The innermost sum in~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:finiteL} is exactly
$81B_k^{(I)}B_l^{(I)}-9S_k^{(I)}S_l^{(I)}$.
Integer dimensions and rational arithmetic now give~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:certificate}
at $T=40,I=10$. Write its numerator and denominator as $N_*,D_*$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:gap}
L_{40,10}-\frac12=
\frac{2575780340458074389989885792667083379858236097}
{27830386119528611875968901007343548725913806188054}>0.
\end{equation}
The stronger displayed decimal inequality means the exact rational
comparison $100000N_*>50009D_*$, not a floating-point comparison.
This proves the finite disproof independently of the next section.

\paragraph{Independent reconstruction.}
The accompanying code computes the complete certificate in two ways.
One uses~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:W}--\eqref{upo:amp:eq:hook}. The other generates tableau
dimensions by Young-lattice branching, starting with dimension one for
the empty shape and adding one legal corner at a time. For each contained
inner and outer three-row shape, it explicitly lists removed-box columns
and accepts the skew diagram exactly when no column is repeated.
Thus the second method uses neither the hook product nor the interlacing
loop to construct the needed $F$ and $H$ arrays. Both paths evaluate the
entire finite rational sum, rather than checking a supplied decimal or
only its final inequality. Reproduction details are in
Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:reproduction}.

\begin{proposition}[\wtag]\label{upo:amp:prop:sharper}
For an integer $T\ge0$ let $\Pi_T$ be the partial sum of the series \eqref{upo:amp:eq:R} over $0\le t\le T$:
\[
\Pi_T=\frac1{10368}\sum_{t=0}^{T}9^{-t}\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)\left(\frac32\right)^{k+l}\bigl(P_kP_l-Q_kQ_l\bigr).
\]
Then $\liminf_{n\to\infty}u_n/A_n\ge\Pi_T$ for every $T$; this does not use Lemmas \ref{upo:amp:lem:domination} or~\ref{upo:amp:lem:middle}. In exact arithmetic,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Pi44}
\Pi_{44}=\frac{616064898260691226056814615415016451341424247360270300969}{1228351671029471449207812122938224643885861920422359990272}>\frac{50153}{100000},
\end{equation}
so $R\ge\Pi_{44}>0.50153$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Fix $T$. For every $I$, \eqref{upo:amp:eq:finiteL} gives $\liminf u_n/A_n\ge L_{T,I}$. In $L_{T,I}$ the outer sums over $t\le T$ and $k+l\le t$ are finite, and the coefficients $H_t(k,l)$ are nonnegative (Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:half}). For fixed $k,l$ the inner sum $\sum_{i,j\le I}C(i,j)\binom{i+k}i\binom{j+l}j$ has positive terms by \eqref{upo:amp:eq:Cpositive}, so it increases with $I$; it converges as $I\to\infty$ because $d_i\le(i+1)^23^{-i}$ and $\binom{i+k}i\le(i+1)^k$. Its limit is $81E_kE_l-9D_kD_l$ with the absolutely convergent transforms $D_k,E_k$ of Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:sums}, and by \eqref{upo:amp:eq:DE} this equals $\frac{81}{256}(\frac32)^{k+l}(P_kP_l-Q_kQ_l)$. Hence, as $I\to\infty$,
\[
L_{T,I}\longrightarrow2\sum_{t=0}^T9^{-t-4}\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)\frac{81}{256}\left(\frac32\right)^{k+l}(P_kP_l-Q_kQ_l)=\Pi_T,
\]
since $2\cdot81/(256\cdot9^4)=1/10368$. Taking the supremum over $I$ gives $\liminf u_n/A_n\ge\Pi_T$. With Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main}, $R\ge\Pi_T$.

The value \eqref{upo:amp:eq:Pi44} was computed in the write (5~October 2026) by a program written for the purpose, independent of the delivered code: it evaluates $F_m(p,q)$ for $m\le46$ from Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F} (hook formula \eqref{upo:amp:eq:hook} and the interlacing sum \eqref{upo:amp:eq:W}), then $H_t(k,l)$ by \eqref{upo:amp:eq:H}, and sums $\Pi_{44}$ in exact rational arithmetic. As checks, the same program reproduces $u_4,\ldots,u_{15}$ of Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:intro} through \eqref{upo:amp:eq:gluing}, gives $H_t(k,l)\ge0$ for all $t\le44$, gives $\Pi_0=35/324$, and reproduces $L_{40,10}$ of \eqref{upo:amp:eq:certificate} as the same fraction. The intermediate partial sums are $\Pi_{10}=0.457116$, $\Pi_{20}=0.490148$, $\Pi_{30}=0.498089$, $\Pi_{40}=0.500923$ and $\Pi_{44}=0.501538$ (rounded); the inequality in \eqref{upo:amp:eq:Pi44} is the exact comparison $100000\,N>50153\,D$ of numerator and denominator.
\end{proof}
The bound is one-sided. The tail of \eqref{upo:amp:eq:R} beyond $T$ is $O((T+1)^{-3})$ with an unspecified constant (Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:uniform}), so $\Pi_{44}$ is not an approximation with a certified error, and no upper bound for $R$ is known.

\begin{remark}[\wtag\ Why the numerics pointed to $1/2$]\label{upo:amp:rem:numerics}
Conway and Guttmann's estimate rests on ratio and quadratic estimators plotted against $1/n^2$ \cite[Section~5.1]{CG}, which presuppose corrections in powers of $1/n$. Parts II and III prove that $u_n/A_n$ has the term $\kappa_3\log n/n^3$ with $\kappa_3=43020\sqrt3/\pi\approx2.37\times10^4$, followed by $\kappa_4\log n/n^4$ with $\kappa_4=-5041845\sqrt3/\pi\approx-2.78\times10^6$ (Theorems \ref{upo:rat:thm:secondlog} and~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}). At $n=200$ these two terms are about $0.0157$ and $-0.0092$; at $n=50$ they are about $0.74$ and $-1.74$. At the end of the available data they are therefore about ten and six times the proved margin $0.0015$ (the actual value of $R-1/2$ is not known). The $\kappa_4$ term is larger in size than the $\kappa_3$ term for every $n<|\kappa_4|/\kappa_3\approx117$ (at $n=70$ they are about $0.29$ and $-0.49$), so over much of the range of the data the logarithmic part of the expansion is not yet led by its first term. An extrapolation that omits these terms cannot be expected to decide whether $R$ exceeds $1/2$. \textup{(Wording corrected after the independent check of 5 October 2026; the first wording read ``an order of magnitude larger than the margin $R-1/2>0.0015$ proved above, and below $n\approx70$ the asymptotic series is not yet in its useful range''.)} The exact ratios computed in the write with the program of Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper} make the slow approach visible: $u_n/A_n=0.70858$, $0.73021$, $0.71055$, $0.69538$, $0.69035$ at $n=20$, $30$, $40$, $46$, $48$. The intake dossier for this batch reports least-squares fits of OEIS b-file ratios for $40\le n\le70$, with $\kappa_3$ included, that return $0.474$--$0.483$, below the proved lower bound. These fits are diagnostics, not estimates, and nothing in the report rests on them. The refutation of $R=1/2$ rests on the proof of Sections \ref{upo:amp:sec:gluing}--\ref{upo:amp:sec:certificate} alone.
\end{remark}
\section{Uniform domination and existence of the amplitude}\label{upo:amp:sec:uniform}
Fixed boundary limits alone do not justify~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:R}. We give uniform
control of the unbounded parameters and of the middle of the convolution.

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:amp:lem:domination}
There is an absolute constant $K$ such that for every $s,i,j\in\N$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Hbound}
0\le \frac{H_s(i,j)}{A_s}\le Kb_ib_j,
\qquad b_i=\frac{(i+1)^4}{3^i}.
\end{equation}
Also
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Bfinite}
\mathcal B:=\sum_{i,k\ge0}b_ib_k\binom{i+k}{i}<\infty.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The horizontal $p$-strip incidence matrix between three-row shapes of
sizes $m-p$ and $m$ has at most $c_p$ ones in any row or any column:
the numbers of removed or added boxes in the three rows form a weak
composition of $p$. Cauchy--Schwarz first inside the strip sum and then
between the two boundary vectors gives
\begin{align}
F_m(p,p)&\le c_p\sum_\rho\sum_{\rho/\mu\text{ horizontal }p}
(f^\mu)^2\le c_p^2 A_{m-p},\label{upo:amp:eq:diag}\\
F_m(p,q)&\le c_pc_q\sqrt{A_{m-p}A_{m-q}}.\label{upo:amp:eq:CS}
\end{align}
All indices here satisfy $0\le p,q\le m$.
Equation~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:regev}, adjusted over finitely many small values,
provides constants $0<c<C<\infty$ with
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:globalA}
c\frac{9^m}{(m+1)^4}\le A_m\le C\frac{9^m}{(m+1)^4}
\quad(m\ge0).
\end{equation}
Consequently
\[
\frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_m}\le K_1c_pc_q3^{-p-q}
\frac{(m+1)^4}{(m-p+1)^2(m-q+1)^2}.
\]
Since $(m+1)/(m-p+1)\le p+1$ for $0\le p\le m$
(the difference after cross multiplication is $p(m-p)\ge0$), and
$c_p\le(p+1)^2$, this yields
\[
\frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_m}\le K_1(p+1)^4(q+1)^4 3^{-p-q}.
\]
Use $H_s(i,j)\le F_{s+2}(i+1,j+1)$, the globally bounded ratio
$A_{s+2}/A_s$, and $(i+2)^4\le16(i+1)^4$ to obtain~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Hbound}.
Outside the support the same assertion is immediate.

For the second assertion, set $r=i+k$. Then
\[
\sum_{i+k=r}b_ib_k\binom ri
\le (r+1)^8\left(\frac23\right)^r.
\]
The right side is summable in $r$. The complete four-index coupling
therefore has summable majorant with total $K^2\mathcal B^2$.
\end{proof}

For clarity let the aggregate contribution of half sizes $s,t$ be
\[
G_{s,t}=\sum_{i,j,k,l\ge0}H_s(i,j)H_t(k,l)
\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}.
\]
It is symmetric in $s,t$ and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Gbound}
0\le G_{s,t}\le K^2\mathcal B^2 A_sA_t.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:amp:lem:middle}
There is a constant $K_2$ such that, with $N=n-4$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:middle}
\frac1{A_n}\sum_{\substack{s+t=N\\s,t>T}}G_{s,t}
\le K_2(T+1)^{-3}
\end{equation}
whenever the sum is nonempty.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:globalA} and~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Gbound}, the left side is bounded
by a constant times
\[
9^{-4}(n+1)^4\sum_{T<s<N-T}
\frac1{(s+1)^4(N-s+1)^4}.
\]
For $s\le N/2$, $(n+1)/(N-s+1)\le2(N+5)/(N+2)\le5$.
Use the symmetric estimate on the other half of the range. The result
is at most a constant times
$\sum_{s>T}(s+1)^{-4}\le (T+1)^{-3}/3$, with an inessential adjustment
if needed at the endpoint. This is uniform in $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of existence and the endpoint representation]
For fixed $t$, the boundary support of $H_t(k,l)$ is finite. For each
fixed $k,l$, Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:domination} dominates the sum over $i,j$ by
a constant times
$b_ib_j\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}$, which is summable by
\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Bfinite}. The additional ratio $A_{n-4-t}/A_n$ is uniformly
bounded for sufficiently large $n$ and has limit $9^{-4-t}$.
Dominated summation and Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed} therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:et}
\frac{G_{n-4-t,t}}{A_n}\longrightarrow e_t:=
9^{-4-t}\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)
\sum_{i,j\ge0}C(i,j)\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}.
\end{equation}
All terms are nonnegative. Independently, taking the limit
in~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Gbound} yields
\[
0\le e_t\le K^2\mathcal B^2\,9^{-4-t}A_t
=O((t+1)^{-4}).
\]
Thus $\sum_te_t$ converges and its tail is $O((T+1)^{-3})$.

For fixed $T$ and $n>2T+4$, the two endpoint ranges of the exact
convolution are disjoint and sum to $2\sum_{t=0}^TG_{n-4-t,t}$.
The remaining middle is bounded by~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:middle}.
Taking $n\to\infty$ at fixed $T$, then $T\to\infty$, proves
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:Rend}
\frac{u_n}{A_n}\longrightarrow R=2\sum_{t\ge0}e_t.
\end{equation}
In particular there is neither unaccounted mass in large boundary
parameters nor mass remaining in the middle half-size range.
\end{proof}

\section{Evaluation of the boundary sums}\label{upo:amp:sec:sums}
Define convergent sums
\[
D_k=\sum_{i\ge0}\binom{i+k}{i}d_i,
\qquad E_k=\sum_{i\ge0}\binom{i+k}{i}d_{i+1}.
\]
Since $\sum_{i\ge0}d_iw^i=(1-w/3)^{-3}$ and
$\sum_{k\ge0}\binom{i+k}{i}z^k=(1-z)^{-i-1}$, summation gives,
initially for sufficiently small $|z|$,
\begin{align*}
\sum_{k\ge0}D_kz^k&=\frac{27(1-z)^2}{(2-3z)^3},\\
\sum_{k\ge0}E_kz^k&=\frac{27(1-z)^3}{(2-3z)^3}-1.
\end{align*}
Extracting coefficients using
$[z^k](1-3z/2)^{-3}=\binom{k+2}{2}(3/2)^k$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:DE}
D_k=\frac3{16}Q_k\left(\frac32\right)^k,
\qquad E_k=\frac1{16}P_k\left(\frac32\right)^k.
\end{equation}
The subtraction of $1$ in the second generating function is essential
at $k=0$: $D_0=27/8$ and $E_0=19/8$.

Substitution of~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Climit} in~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:et} and
\eqref{upo:amp:eq:Rend} now yields
\begin{align*}
R&=2\sum_{t\ge0}9^{-4-t}\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)
\bigl(81E_kE_l-9D_kD_l\bigr)\\
&=\frac2{81}\sum_{t\ge0}9^{-t}\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)
\bigl(E_kE_l-D_kD_l/9\bigr).
\end{align*}
Equation~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:DE} proves precisely~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:R}, including its
factor $1/10368$. Since $P_k-Q_k=4k+20>0$ and both polynomials are
positive for $k\ge0$, all nonzero summands are positive. The $t=0$
summand is $35/324$. Existence, positivity, and convergence have already
been proved, and~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:finiteL} gives the lower bound for $R$.
Together with~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:regev}, this completes the proof of
Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main}.

The $O(T^{-3})$ tail statements here have unspecified constants. They
prove convergence and passage to the limit; they do not certify digits
of $R$. In particular, a positive partial sum of~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:R} is a lower
bound, not a two-sided approximation with a certified decimal error.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
The partial sums $\Pi_T$ of Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper} are such lower bounds. Effective tail constants remain open (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, item~1).
\end{writenote}

\section{What the equivalent implies for inverse thresholds}\label{upo:amp:sec:inverse}
Let $\tau(T)=\min\{n\in\N:u_n\ge T\}$ for real $T>0$. Write
\[
\lambda=\log9,\quad L=\log T,\quad K_0=C_0R,
\qquad
x(T)=\frac{L+4\log L-4\log\lambda-\log K_0}{\lambda}.
\]
Here $x(T)$ is used only for sufficiently large $T$.

\begin{corollary}\label{upo:amp:cor:inverse}
There is a positive function $\epsilon(T)\to0$ such that for all
sufficiently large $T$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:inverse}
\left\lceil x(T)-\epsilon(T)\right\rceil
\le\tau(T)\le
\left\lceil x(T)+\epsilon(T)\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
No effective rate for $\epsilon(T)$ is asserted.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Prepending a new largest value to a permutation cannot use that value in
an increasing subsequence of length at least two: it has no predecessor
and no larger successor. All old occurrences are unchanged. This is an
injection from the permutations counted by $u_n$ to those counted by
$u_{n+1}$, so $u_n$ is nondecreasing. It is unbounded by
Theorem~\ref{upo:amp:thm:main}, hence $\tau(T)$ exists.

Put $h(y)=\lambda y-4\log y+\log K_0$. For large $y$, it is strictly
increasing with $h'(y)\to\lambda$. The unique large solution $y(T)$ of
$h(y)=L$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{upo:amp:eq:modelroot}
y(T)=x(T)+O(\log L/L)=x(T)+o(1).
\end{equation}
Indeed, inserting $x(T)=L/\lambda+O(\log L)$ gives
$h(x(T))-L=O(\log L/L)$, and $h'$ is bounded away from zero.

The relative equivalent says
$\log u_n=h(n)+r_n$ with $r_n\to0$ for $n\ge4$.
Let $a(T)=\sup_{n\ge\lfloor y(T)/2\rfloor}|r_n|$, which tends to zero.
Choose $\delta(T)=3a(T)/\lambda+1/y(T)$, and take $T$ large enough
that $h'(v)\ge\lambda/2$ near and above $y(T)/2$.
Then $h(n)+r_n<L$ for integers $y(T)/2\le n\le y(T)-\delta(T)$,
and $h(n)+r_n>L$ for integers $n\ge y(T)+\delta(T)$.
For the first assertion one uses
$(\lambda/2)\delta(T)>a(T)$; the same margin proves the second.
Earlier integers also have $u_n<T$: for instance the equivalent gives
$\log u_{\lceil y(T)/2\rceil}-L\to-\infty$, and monotonicity applies.

Thus
$\lceil y(T)-\delta(T)\rceil\le\tau(T)
\le\lceil y(T)+\delta(T)\rceil$.
Take $\epsilon(T)=|y(T)-x(T)|+\delta(T)$ and
use~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:modelroot} to obtain~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:inverse}.
\end{proof}

The rounding uncertainty must remain \emph{inside} the ceilings. A
relative $o(1)$ equivalent does not decide on which side of a nearby
integer the threshold lies. It also does not supply first or higher
correction terms. As an optional exact expression for the continuous
model only,
\[
y(T)=-\frac4\lambda
W_{-1}\!\left(-\frac\lambda4(K_0/T)^{1/4}\right),
\]
where $W_{-1}$ is the real branch at negative arguments tending to zero.
This is not an exact formula for the discrete threshold. Finite threshold
checks must use exact $u_n$ values from~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:gluing}, not a partial
sum substituted for $R$.
\begin{writenote}[Instances of the generic inverse theorems, 5 October 2026]
The ceiling sandwich \eqref{upo:amp:eq:inverse} is the form that parts (1) and~(2) of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} in the transseries volume (the front-matter section \hyperref[upo:sec:neighbours]{Relation to neighbouring reports}) take once a real inverse is located within $\epsilon(T)$ of $x(T)$; the proof above establishes this directly. The $W_{-1}$ expression for $y(T)$ is \texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core} for $aX+b\log X=L'$ with $a=\lambda$, $b=-4$ and $L'=\log T-\log K_0$, on the branch $W_{-1}$, whose values tend to $-\infty$ as its argument increases to $0$, which gives the large root. Part~II narrows the width to $O((\log T)^{-3})$ (Corollary~\ref{upo:rat:cor:inverse}) and Part~III to every fixed order (Corollary~\ref{upo:all:cor:inverse}).
\end{writenote}

\section{Reproduction and limits of the computation}\label{upo:amp:sec:reproduction}
The accompanying reproducibility archive contains this TeX source and
PDF, exact source fixtures, two certificate implementations, and a
standard-library Python driver. Its README specifies the complete commands
and dependency requirements for checking, deterministic PDF rebuilding, and
deterministic archive creation. The programs use integer and rational
arithmetic for every certificate decision.

The checks cover exact agreement with the A217057 fixture, direct
permutation enumeration at manageable sizes, independently constructed
tableau dimensions and horizontal strips, the complete $T=40,I=10$
certificate, and the strict rational inequalities above. Selected
mutations test that failures are detected rather than silently ignored.
The driver has explicit validation active under Python's optimized
mode. Its closed inventory rejects unexpected entries, symbolic links,
and nonregular objects. Normal commands create outputs exclusively
outside the bundle and reject symbolic-link ancestors before computation.
Intentional maintainer sealing is a separately documented operation.

These tests address transcription, normalization, implementation, and
reproducibility. They do not turn finite checks into a proof of the
analytic theorem. The latter rests on the bijections, the fixed-boundary
concentration argument, the uniform Cauchy--Schwarz majorant, and the
convolution-tail estimate proved above.

\paragraph{Questions left open here.}
The series~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:R} is an exact answer for the leading amplitude,
but no simpler closed form is obtained. Making the domination constants
effective enough for efficient certified numerical enclosures would be
useful. First and higher asymptotic corrections for $u_n$, and the
corresponding effective or all-orders inverse expansions, remain open
in this report. The two-half geometry suggests comparisons with other
single-pattern classes, but does not by itself settle any of them.
\subsection{Further questions and research}\label{upo:amp:sub:further}
\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions above, 5 October 2026]
(a) A closed form for $R$: open. (b) Effective domination constants and certified enclosures of $R$: open (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, item~1); Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper} gives only a lower bound. (c) First and higher corrections: \emph{answered} by Part~II ($S$, $S_2$, $\kappa_3$; Theorems \ref{upo:rat:thm:correction} and~\ref{upo:rat:thm:secondlog}) and Part~III (every fixed order, Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}). (d) Effective or all-orders inverse expansions: the all-orders inverse is Part~III's Corollary~\ref{upo:all:cor:inverse}; effective constants remain open (item~4). (e) Other single-pattern classes: advanced by Part~IV (one $1243$), Parts V--VI (one $12345$) and the sibling report for one $1432$; Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy} compares the outcomes, and longer patterns remain open (item~9). This Part states no claim without proof: its open points are the questions above and the limitations it prints (unspecified tail constants, no rate for $\epsilon(T)$). Nothing in it was found to be wrong; the one wrong claim it addresses is external, Conway and Guttmann's $R=1/2$.
\end{writenote}

\clearpage
\part{Rational structure and logarithmic asymptotics for A217057}\label{upo:rat:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~135, archive \file{A217057_Rational_Structure_Logarithmic_Asymptotics_and_Inversion_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{Report135.tex} (22 pages), dated 2~October 2026; title block ``Rational structure and logarithmic asymptotics for A217057'', author line ``Report 135''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} notes; a section number is Report~135's plus~$10$ (its Theorem~$k.j$ is Theorem~$(k+10).j$), and equations are renumbered within sections. Remark~\ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} and Section~\ref{upo:rat:sub:further} are added in the write.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.44\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~II & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule

$U(z)=\sum u_nz^n$; $U\in U(3)$ & Parts V--VI: $U_n$ the $12345$ count; Part~III: arrays $U_j$\\
$P,Q$ shuffle weights (Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:kernel}); $P=\tr U$; $Q=\sum\theta_j^2$ & Part~I: polynomials $P_k,Q_k$; Parts V--VI: boundary parameters\\
$\mathsf B(a,b)=[v^b](1+v)^a$; $B(i,j)$ \eqref{upo:rat:eq:B}; $B$ a constant-term variable & Part~IV: $\mathcal B(a,b)$, $B(z)$, $B_p$\\
$D_k,E_k,J_k,K_k,L_k,M_k$ transforms; $\mathcal D(x)$; $\mathcal D_2(i,j)$ & Part~IV: $J_k(s)$ spine sums, $D(x)$\\
$\alpha=-11/2$; $\alpha\in\{0,1\}$ in \eqref{upo:rat:eq:Qencoding} & Part~VI: $\alpha=13/2$\\

\end{center}
\begin{partabstract}
Let $u_n$ count permutations with exactly one classical increasing
subsequence of length four, and $A_n$ count the corresponding avoiders.
Continuing Report 134, we prove that $\sum u_nz^n$ is a rational diagonal
and hence D-finite, and give an explicit 42-variable rational constant
term with convergent contours. A uniform unitary-integral expansion and
weighted convolution analysis also prove
\[
\frac{u_n}{A_n}=R+\frac Sn+\frac{S_2}{n^2}
+\frac{43020\sqrt3}{\pi}\frac{\log n}{n^3}+O(n^{-3}).
\]
The constants $S,S_2$ are explicit absolutely convergent signed series;
$R>0$ is the amplitude from Report 134. The positive logarithmic term
rules out a pure power expansion at the third relative correction.
The full corrected model gives an eventual $O((\log T)^{-3})$ uncertainty
inside the ceilings for inverse thresholds. No explicit recurrence,
certified numerical values for the series constants, effective onset,
finite third coefficient, or all-orders expansion is asserted.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
In this report ``Report~134'' \cite{R134} is Part~I; the companion it calls unchanged is Part~I's text and the \file{134-amplitude-} files. The finite $n^{-3}$ coefficient and the further logarithms that this Part does not reach are proved in Part~III (Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}: $r_3$ exists, $\kappa_4=-5041845\sqrt3/\pi$).
\end{writenote}


\section{Scope and the new conclusions}
An occurrence of the classical pattern $1234$ is a choice of four
positions $r_1<r_2<r_3<r_4$ with increasing values. The positions need not
be consecutive. Let $u_n$ be the number of permutations of $[n]$ with
exactly one such choice, with $u_0=u_1=u_2=u_3=0$. This is
OEIS A217057~\cite{OEIS217057}. Let $A_n$ count the $1234$-avoiders and set
$A_0=1$.

Report 134~\cite{R134} proves an exact two-half decomposition and
\begin{equation}\label{upo:rat:eq:oldlimit}
u_n\sim R A_n\sim C_0R\,9^n n^{-4},
\qquad C_0=\frac{81\sqrt3}{16\pi},\qquad R>0.50009.
\end{equation}
Its finite rational certificate disproves the particular conjecture
$R=1/2$ of Conway and Guttmann~\cite[Section 5.1]{CG}.
The present report is a continuation, with that report included unchanged
as a companion in the archive. Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:inputs} defines all the
finite counts and records the exact identities and bounds used here.
The proofs of those inherited combinatorial facts remain in the companion;
all the new algebraic and asymptotic steps are proved below.

\begin{theorem}\label{upo:rat:thm:structure}
The ordinary generating function $U(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}u_nz^n$ is the
diagonal of a rational power series over $\mathbb Q$. In particular it
is D-finite, and $(u_n)$ is P-recursive. Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:kernel}
constructs an explicit rational constant-term representation of $U$.
\end{theorem}
For precision, a rational diagonal means that for some finite $d$ and
rational function $Q$ regular at the origin,
\[
U(z)=\Diag Q(z):=\sum_{n\ge0}
[x_1^n\cdots x_d^n]Q(x_1,\ldots,x_d)z^n.
\]
P-recursiveness means existence of a nonzero polynomial-coefficient
recurrence of finite order. D-finiteness means existence of a nonzero
linear differential equation with polynomial coefficients.
Neither an explicit recurrence nor an explicit origin-regular diagonal
function $Q$ is computed here. The rational constant term is a separate,
fully displayed constructive representation with specified contours.

To state the refinement of~\eqref{upo:rat:eq:oldlimit}, put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:rat:eq:da}
d_p=\binom{p+2}{2}3^{-p},\qquad a_p=\frac{p(p-1)}2,
\end{equation}
and define
\begin{align}
C(i,j)&=81d_{i+1}d_{j+1}-9d_id_j,\label{upo:rat:eq:C}\\
B(i,j)&=-81d_{i+1}d_{j+1}(8+a_{i+1}+a_{j+1})
       +9d_id_j(4+a_i+a_j).\label{upo:rat:eq:B}
\end{align}
The half counts $H_t(k,l)$ are defined in~\eqref{upo:rat:eq:H}.

\begin{theorem}\label{upo:rat:thm:correction}
The following signed series is absolutely convergent:
\begin{align}\label{upo:rat:eq:Sraw}
S={}&2\sum_{t\ge0}9^{-t-4}\sum_{k+l\le t}H_t(k,l)
\sum_{i,j\ge0}\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}\\[-2pt]
&\hspace{32mm}\cdot\bigl(4(t+4)C(i,j)+B(i,j)\bigr).\notag
\end{align}
It satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{upo:rat:eq:ratioexp}
\frac{u_n}{A_n}=R+\frac Sn+O(n^{-2}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, with $M_0=C_0R$ and $\beta=S/R-11/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:rat:eq:uexp}
u_n=M_0\,9^n n^{-4}\left(1+\frac\beta n+O(n^{-2})\right).
\end{equation}
The infinite $i,j$ sums in~\eqref{upo:rat:eq:Sraw} have exact polynomial
binomial transforms, given in Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:transforms}.
\end{theorem}
The implied constants are not made effective. Absolute convergence
specifies $S$ exactly, but does not certify its decimal value, its sign,
or its nonvanishing. The same qualifications apply to $\beta$.
Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:secondlog} goes farther: Theorem~\ref{upo:rat:thm:secondlog}
adds an absolutely convergent second coefficient $S_2$ and evaluates the
first logarithmic term, with coefficient $43020\sqrt3/\pi>0$.
Section~\ref{upo:rat:sec:inverse} derives the resulting $O((\log T)^{-3})$
inside-ceiling uncertainty. The first-correction proof is retained as a
useful intermediate result and supplies the endpoint estimate needed
to identify the logarithm.

\section{Exact reduction and inherited estimates}\label{upo:rat:sec:inputs}
For $0\le p,q\le m$, let $F_m(p,q)$ count the $1234$-avoiders whose
last $p$ entries are decreasing and whose $q$ smallest values occur in
decreasing order. Conditions with parameter zero or one are vacuous.
For $s,i,j\in\N$ put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:rat:eq:H}
H_s(i,j)=F_{s+2}(i+1,j+1)-F_{s+1}(i,j)\quad(i+j\le s),
\end{equation}
and set $H_s(i,j)=0$ otherwise. These differences are nonnegative integers,
not an inclusion-exclusion approximation.
\begin{writenote}[A summary of Part~I, 5 October 2026]
This section restates, in its own words and citing Part~I, results proved there: \eqref{upo:rat:eq:gluing} is Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:gluing}; the interpretation of \eqref{upo:rat:eq:H} is Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:half}; \eqref{upo:rat:eq:W}--\eqref{upo:rat:eq:hook} are Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F}; \eqref{upo:rat:eq:globalA} is \eqref{upo:amp:eq:globalA}; \eqref{upo:rat:eq:Hbound} is Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:domination}; \eqref{upo:rat:eq:Rraw} is \eqref{upo:amp:eq:Rend} with \eqref{upo:amp:eq:et}. It is printed in full because its displays are the ones this Part uses; it adds no claim.
\end{writenote}

\subsection{Why the two halves and the two shuffles appear}
Fix the unique increasing four-chain. For any extra point, let $a$ count
chain positions before it and $b$ count chain values below it. A chain
using that point and distinguished-chain points has maximal length
$5-|a-b|$. Thus $|a-b|\le1$ is forbidden. The twelve allowed cells split as
\begin{align*}
\mathcal N&=\{(0,2),(0,3),(0,4),(1,3),(1,4),(2,4)\},\\
\mathcal S&=\{(2,0),(3,0),(3,1),(4,0),(4,1),(4,2)\}.
\end{align*}
The northern and southern halves share the four-chain. They have $s,t$
extra points. In the northern half let $i,j$ count the cells $(2,4),(0,2)$;
in the southern half let $k,l$ count $(2,0),(4,2)$.
Each of these four boundary cells is decreasing, since an increasing
pair would create another four-chain inside its half.

The only position-order ambiguity is the shuffle of the $i$ and $k$
points in position gap 2. Independently, the $j$ and $l$ points in value
gap 2 may be shuffled in value order. Cross-half increasing pairs cannot
extend to a four-chain: they can only connect the two marked cells in
one of these shared gaps, and no distinguished point can extend such a
pair. Thus gluing valid halves produces exactly one four-chain and
recovers the two shuffles uniquely. The result is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:rat:eq:gluing}
u_n=\sum_{s+t=n-4}\sum_{i,j,k,l\ge0}
H_s(i,j)H_t(k,l)\binom{i+k}{i}\binom{j+l}{j}.
\end{equation}
There is no marking multiplicity or division factor.

For the interpretation of~\eqref{upo:rat:eq:H}, standardize a northern half.
Its distinguished values are $1,2,c,d$ with $c=j+3$, and it has form
$U\,1\,V\,2\,W\,c\,d$, where $W$ is decreasing of size $i$ above $d$.
Deleting $2,c$ gives an avoider counted by $F_{s+2}(i+1,j+1)$ with final
value greater than $j+1$. The excluded avoiders end in their minimum;
deleting that minimum gives $F_{s+1}(i,j)$. The two insertions reverse
the construction. The suffix and low-value blocks of a retained avoider
are disjoint, which also proves the triangular support $i+j\le s$.
The full inverse-bijection checks are in~\cite[Sections 2--3]{R134}.

\subsection{The fixed-row tableau formula}
For a partition $\lambda=(\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)$, including zero
parts, let $|\lambda|$ be its size and $f^\lambda$ its standard-tableau
