regularized constants and is not permitted.

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:all:lem:models}
There are unique vectors $V_k\in E$, $k\ge3$, such that for every
$K\ge3$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:rdecay}
r^{(K)}_n=a_n-\sum_{k=3}^KV_kb_k(n)=O_E((n+1)^{-K-2}).
\end{equation}
If $h_d(1,\ldots,k)$ denotes the complete homogeneous polynomial of
degree $d$ in $1,\ldots,k$, they are computed by
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:Vrec}
V_k=\frac{U_{k-3}-\sum_{\ell=3}^{k-1}
 (-1)^{\ell+1}\ell!h_{k-\ell}(1,\ldots,\ell)V_\ell}
 {(-1)^{k+1}k!}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Expand the rational function~\eqref{upo:all:eq:bexact} as
$(-1)^{k+1}k!n^{-k-1}\prod_{j=1}^k(1-j/n)^{-1}$ to any fixed order.
Its leading coefficient is nonzero. Triangular matching against the
assumed Banach expansion gives~\eqref{upo:all:eq:Vrec} and~\eqref{upo:all:eq:rdecay}.
\end{proof}

For $j\ge0$ define a regularized Taylor coefficient by
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:Tj}
T_j=\frac{(-1)^j}{j!}\sum_{n\ge0}\fall nj
 \left(a_n-\sum_{k=3}^{j}V_kb_k(n)\right).
\end{equation}
An empty inner sum means zero. These series converge absolutely in $E$:
for $j\le2$, use $a_n=O_E(n^{-4})$; for $j\ge3$, apply
\eqref{upo:all:eq:rdecay} with $K=j$. In the latter case the weighted summand is
$O_E(n^{-2})$.

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:all:lem:remainder}
For a fixed $K\ge3$ set
\[
S_K(z)=\sum_{k=3}^KV_k(1-z)^k\log(1-z),\qquad
P_K(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}T_j(1-z)^j.
\]
Then, as $n\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:finitecalc}
v_n=[z^n]\left(2\B(P_K,S_K)+\B(S_K,S_K)\right)
 +O(n^{-K-2}).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $R_K(z)=\sum r_n^{(K)}z^n$. Its derivatives through order $K$ at
$z=1$ are absolutely convergent coefficient sums. For $k>j$, the
$j$th derivative of $(1-z)^k\log(1-z)$ tends to zero at $z=1$ and its
coefficient derivative series is absolutely convergent. Therefore the
Taylor polynomial of $R_K$ through degree $K-1$, expressed in powers
of $1-z$, is exactly $P_K$ from~\eqref{upo:all:eq:Tj}.
Let $q_n=[z^n](R_K-P_K)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:q}
\|q_n\|=O((n+1)^{-K-2}),\quad
\sum_{n\ge0}\fall njq_n=0\ (0\le j\le K-1),\quad
\sum_{n\ge0}n^K\|q_n\|<\infty.
\end{equation}
The zero falling-factorial moments also imply zero ordinary moments
through $K-1$.

All generating functions converge absolutely for $|z|<1$, so their
products equal coefficient convolutions. Terms involving $P_K$ twice
are polynomial and vanish for sufficiently large $n$. Convolution of
$q$ with $P_K$ is $O(n^{-K-2})$. Convolution of $q$ with itself has the
same bound: split at $n/2$, bound the larger index by $O(n^{-K-2})$,
and sum the other sequence absolutely.

It remains to bound $q*b_k$, for fixed $3\le k\le K$. In the part
$t>n/2$, $\|q_t\|=O(n^{-K-2})$ and $\sum_s|b_k(s)|<\infty$.
In the part $t\le n/2$, use the rational extension in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:bexact}:
its $h$th derivative on $[n/2,n]$ is $O(n^{-k-1-h})$ for all large $n$.
Taylor expansion of $b_k(n-t)$ through degree $K-1$ has remainder
$O(t^Kn^{-k-1-K})$. Its convolution with $q$ is bounded using the last
condition in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:q}. Each retained moment, after extending the
sum to all $t$, vanishes. The truncation tail for its degree $h$ is
\[
O(n^{-k-1-h})\sum_{t>n/2}t^h\|q_t\|
 =O(n^{-k-K-2}).
\]
Thus the entire convolution is $O(n^{-K-2})$. Continuity of $\B$ handles
its vector coefficients. Expansion of
$\B(P_K+S_K+(R_K-P_K),P_K+S_K+(R_K-P_K))$ proves the lemma.
No continuation of $R_K$ across $|z|=1$ has been used.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{upo:all:thm:conv}
The convolution has a complete expansion
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:convexp}
v_n=\sum_{j=0}^J(c_j+\ell_j\log n)n^{-4-j}
 +O_J(n^{-5-J}(1+\log n))
\end{equation}
for every fixed $J$, with $\ell_0=\ell_1=\ell_2=0$.
Every coefficient is obtained by expanding the finite expression
in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:finitecalc}, with any $K\ge J+4$.
In particular, each $\ell_j$ depends only on the local tail vectors
$U_h$, never on the regularized moments $T_h$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Products $P_KS_K$ are finite sums of the single-logarithm models
in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:bk}. Products $S_K^2$ are finite sums of
$(1-z)^m\log^2(1-z)$ with $m\ge6$. For $n>m$, differentiation twice
in $\alpha$ of the finite polynomial
$[z^n](1-z)^\alpha=(-1)^n\fall\alpha n/n!$ gives the exact formula
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:log2}
[z^n](1-z)^m\log^2(1-z)
 =2b_m(n)(H_m-H_{n-m-1}).
\end{equation}
Here $H_m=\sum_{h=1}^m1/h$ and $H_0=0$; these scalar harmonic
numbers are distinct from the earlier half arrays $H_s(i,j)$.
For a fixed integer $p\ge0$, Euler--Maclaurin gives
\[
H_N=\log N+\gamma+\frac1{2N}
 -\sum_{h=1}^p\frac{B_{2h}}{2hN^{2h}}+O_p(N^{-2p-2}),
\]
where $B_{2h}$ are Bernoulli numbers. The finite remainder follows from
the integral remainder in Euler--Maclaurin applied to $1/x$; the
periodic Bernoulli function is bounded and the appropriate derivatives
are integrable on $[N,\infty)$. Equivalently, see the exact
harmonic--digamma identity and its fixed-order expansion in
DLMF~\cite{DLMFharm,DLMFEM}. Expanding $N=n-m-1$ with fixed $m$ and
using~\eqref{upo:all:eq:bexact} gives full expansions involving only a constant
and a single $\log n$. The earliest log-squared model has $m=6$, so its
coefficient logarithm first occurs at $n^{-7}$. No higher logarithmic
powers occur. Taking $K\ge J+4$ makes the error of
\eqref{upo:all:eq:finitecalc} smaller than the error in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:convexp}.
\end{proof}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Part~VI's Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:conv} is the counterpart of Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:conv} for the half-integer exponent $15/2$, with fractional-binomial models whose products are polynomials, so that no logarithm arises there. Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy} compares the two.
\end{writenote}

\section{The constants and the first two logarithms}\label{upo:all:sec:third}
Apply Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:conv} to $E_r$, $\B=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{
\mathcal K}$ and $a_n=9^{-n}H_n$. By~\eqref{upo:all:eq:glue},
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:unshift}
u_n=9^{n-4}v_{n-4}.
\end{equation}
The fixed shift, the pure-power avoidance expansion, and ordinary
formal division yield~\eqref{upo:all:eq:full}, with its claimed error.
The coefficients are unique: at the first differing order, multiplying
the difference by that power of $n$ would give a nonzero affine
function of $\log n$ tending to zero. Thus the first three constants
agree with $R,S,S_2$ from Report 135.

Here is an explicit recipe for the finite third coefficient. The first
four singular vectors are
\begin{align}
V_3&=U_0/6,&V_4&=(6U_0-U_1)/24,\notag\\
V_5&=(35U_0-10U_1+U_2)/120,&
V_6&=(225U_0-85U_1+15U_2-U_3)/720.\label{upo:all:eq:Vfirst}
\end{align}
Compute $U_2,U_3$ by the finite character/Gaussian algorithm in
Section~\ref{upo:all:sec:algorithm}. Form the absolutely convergent series
\begin{align}
T_0&=\sum_{n\ge0}a_n,&T_1&=-\sum_{n\ge0}na_n,\notag\\
T_2&=\frac12\sum_{n\ge0}n(n-1)a_n,&
T_3&=-\frac16\sum_{n\ge0}n(n-1)(n-2)(a_n-V_3b_3(n)).
\label{upo:all:eq:Tfirst}
\end{align}
For $m=3,4,5,6$, put
\[
\Phi_m=2\sum_{k=3}^{m}\pa{T_{m-k}}{V_k}.
\]
The finite coefficients of $v_n$ through the first log are
\begin{align}
c_0&=6\Phi_3,\notag\\
c_1&=36\Phi_3-24\Phi_4,\notag\\
c_2&=150\Phi_3-240\Phi_4+120\Phi_5,\notag\\
\ell_3&=1440\pa{V_3}{V_3}=40\pa{U_0}{U_0},\notag\\
c_3&=540\Phi_3-1560\Phi_4+1800\Phi_5-720\Phi_6
 +\ell_3(\gamma-H_6),\label{upo:all:eq:cfirst}
\end{align}
where $\gamma$ is Euler's constant. These formulas use the finite
coefficients of $b_k$ at small $n$ exactly as in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:bk}.
The inverse avoidance series starts
\[
(1-11/(2n)+20/n^2-965/(16n^3)+\cdots)^{-1}
 =1+\frac{11}{2n}+\frac{41}{4n^2}+\frac{107}{16n^3}+\cdots.
\]
Expanding the shift $n-4$ in~\eqref{upo:all:eq:unshift} now gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:r3}
r_3=\frac{9^{-4}}{C_0}
 \left(c_3+\frac{59}{2}c_2+\frac{1441}{4}c_1
 +\frac{37291}{16}c_0\right).
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{upo:all:eq:Tfirst}--\eqref{upo:all:eq:r3}, together with finite local
polynomial calculations, are the promised absolutely convergent
regularized-series prescription. Existence and convergence, not a
numerically certified evaluation, are claimed here.

The next logarithmic coefficient of the unshifted convolution is
\begin{align*}
\ell_4&=30240\pa{V_3}{V_3}-20160\pa{V_3}{V_4}
       =140\pa{U_0}{U_1}.
\end{align*}
The shift and division therefore give
\begin{align}
\kappa_3&=\frac{9^{-4}}{C_0}\ell_3,
\notag\\
\kappa_4&=\frac{9^{-4}}{C_0}\left(\ell_4+\frac{67}{2}\ell_3\right)
 =C_0 9^{-4}\left(570\pa CC+140\pa CB\right).\label{upo:all:eq:kappa4}
\end{align}
For transparency, both needed pairings are exact rational calculations:
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:pairvals}
\pa CC=1393848,\qquad \pa CB=-52348032.
\end{equation}
A convenient general evaluation rule is this: if
$f_i=3^{-i}f(i)$ and $g_i=3^{-i}g(i)$ for rational polynomials, write
$f(i)=\sum_p f_p\fall ip$ and $g(i)=\sum_q g_q\fall iq$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:fallpair}
\sum_{i,k\ge0} f_i g_k\binom{i+k}{i}
 =3\sum_{p,q}f_pg_q(p+q)!.
\end{equation}
It follows by differentiating $(1-x-y)^{-1}$ and putting $x=y=1/3$.
Absolute convergence licenses every differentiation. Rank-one
factorizations of $C,B$ reduce~\eqref{upo:all:eq:pairvals} to this rule; the companion replay carries out the rational arithmetic. Substitution proves~\eqref{upo:all:eq:kappas}.

More generally, every $V_k$ is $C_0$ times an array of the form
$3^{-i-j}$ times a rational polynomial. Thus all pairings of singular
vectors are $C_0^2$ times rationals by~\eqref{upo:all:eq:fallpair}.
The log coefficient of~\eqref{upo:all:eq:log2} is the rational function
$-2b_m(n)$. Shift and division use rational coefficients. Consequently
every $\kappa_j$ is a rational multiple of $C_0$, proving the final
algebraic assertion of Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}.

\section{All orders inverse thresholds}\label{upo:all:sec:inverse}
Let $M_0=C_0R>0$, $\lambda=\log9$, and
\[
N(T)=\min\{n\ge0:u_n\ge T\}.
\]
The ratio $u_{n+1}/u_n\to9$ makes the sequence eventually strictly
increasing. For sufficiently large thresholds, this controls the
inverse irrespective of finitely many early values.

The direct expansion implies, to every fixed order,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:logu}
\log u_n=\lambda n-4\log n+\log M_0
 +\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{L_j(\log n)}{n^j}
 +O_J\left(\frac{(1+\log n)^{J+1}}{n^{J+1}}\right),
\end{equation}
where $L_j$ is a polynomial with $\deg L_j\le\lfloor j/3\rfloor$.
Indeed every logarithm in the normalized direct expansion costs at
least three powers of $1/n$; expand the ordinary scalar logarithm by
its finite Taylor formula. This degree bound concerns $\log u_n$,
where higher powers of logarithms may occur; it does not contradict the
affine-log structure of $u_n/A_n$.

\begin{corollary}\label{upo:all:cor:inverse}
For every fixed $J\ge0$ there are explicitly recursive polynomials
$Q_j$, $1\le j\le J$, of degree at most $j$, such that, with
$y=\log T$ and $d=\log M_0+4\log\lambda$,
\[
x_J(T)=\frac1\lambda\left[y+4\log y-d
 +\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{Q_j(\log y)}{y^j}\right]
\]
satisfies the eventual sandwich
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all:eq:inverse}
\left\lceil x_J(T)-C_J\frac{(1+\log y)^{J+1}}{y^{J+1}}\right\rceil
 \le N(T)\le
\left\lceil x_J(T)+C_J\frac{(1+\log y)^{J+1}}{y^{J+1}}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
The constants and onset are not asserted effective. No convention of
rounding a truncated asymptotic expansion to a single exact integer is
implied when its error interval crosses an integer.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Set $w=\lambda x$ and formally substitute
$w=y+4\log y-d+\sum_{j=1}^JQ_j(\log y)y^{-j}$ in
\eqref{upo:all:eq:logu}. At each new order $y^{-j}$ the new polynomial $Q_j$
appears with coefficient one; all other terms have already been
computed. This determines $Q_j$ successively. Expansion of
$\log(1+(w-y)/y)$ and $(1+(w-y)/y)^{-h}$ shows inductively that its
degree is at most $j$. Finite Taylor remainders on $(w-y)/y=o(1)$ give
residual $O_J((1+\log y)^{J+1}y^{-J-1})$.

For a rigorous threshold conclusion, regard the right side of the
finite expansion~\eqref{upo:all:eq:logu} as a smooth function of a positive
real variable $x$. Its derivative is $\lambda+o(1)$, bounded below by
$\lambda/2$ for all large $x$. The discrete remainder and the
substitution residual have the stated bound uniformly for integers
within a bounded distance of $x_J$. Increasing the constant $C_J$ makes
the lower and upper shifts dominate both errors. For full precision about rounding, put
$\epsilon=C_J(1+\log y)^{J+1}y^{-J-1}$. The lower comparison is at
$k_-=\lceil x_J-\epsilon\rceil-1<x_J-\epsilon$, and the upper one
is at $k_+=\lceil x_J+\epsilon\rceil\ge x_J+\epsilon$.
The derivative bound and the chosen error margin give
$\log u_{k_-}<\log T$ and $\log u_{k_+}\ge\log T$.
All these integers lie within a bounded distance of $x_J$.
Eventual monotonicity, with the finite early maximum below $T$, gives
$k_-<N(T)\le k_+$, exactly~\eqref{upo:all:eq:inverse}.
\end{proof}
For example, write
$u_n=M_0 9^n n^{-4}(1+\beta/n+\delta/n^2+\cdots)$, where
\[
\beta=-\frac{11}{2}+\frac SR,
\qquad \delta=20-\frac{11S}{2R}+\frac{S_2}{R}.
\]
If
$a=4z-d$, the first two inverse polynomials are
\[
Q_1(z)=4a-\lambda\beta,
\qquad Q_2(z)=4Q_1(z)-2a^2+\lambda\beta a
 -\lambda^2(\delta-\beta^2/2).
\]
At higher orders the same recursion includes the logarithmic
coefficients and the regularized constants from the direct expansion.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Corollary~\ref{upo:all:cor:inverse} is an instance of parts (1)--(2) of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} (the front-matter section \hyperref[upo:sec:neighbours]{Relation to neighbouring reports}), with $x_J(T)$ as approximate inverse; the recursion for the $Q_j$ is a finite reversion. It contains Corollaries \ref{upo:amp:cor:inverse} and~\ref{upo:rat:cor:inverse}.
\end{writenote}

\section{Reproducibility and the scope of the computations}\label{upo:all:sec:repro}
The companion archive contains this article in editable \LaTeX\ and
PDF, the exact arithmetic replay, typed reference fixtures, integrity
checks, and documented clean-build and deterministic-pack commands.
It also includes the unchanged final Report 135 companion and its
Report 134 materials. The latter retain their own verifiers and seals;
the new replay checks their byte integrity before relying on them.
No third-party paper PDFs are redistributed.

The new finite checks cover the single-log coefficient identities and
squared-log coefficient products, triangular singular-vector matching,
the Gaussian coefficients through $\alpha_3$, the two exact kernel
pairings and $\kappa_4$, the finite $r_3$ shift weights, and the first
inverse-polynomial cancellations. Additional regularization checks use
explicit synthetic scalar sequences with exactly known model tails.
They check the implementation of subtraction and convergent weighted
sums. They are not numerical evaluations or error certificates for the
actual A217057 constants.

The verifier rejects undeclared or ill-typed fixture data and checks the
sealed source inventory. Normal and optimized Python runs are required
to produce the same result; the tests do not rely on removable
\texttt{assert} statements. Separate output locations and explicit
path guards protect the frozen package. Fresh clean builds and
repacking are checked for deterministic output. These finite
computations support the displayed algebra and reproducibility.
The uniform estimates, infinite-series convergence, and all-orders
claims are established by the proofs in this article, not by testing
finitely many coefficients.

\section{Further questions}\label{upo:all:sec:questions}
The all-orders statement, existence of the finite third constant,
convergent regularization algorithm, next exact logarithmic coefficient,
and arbitrary fixed-order inverse sandwich are proved here from the
exact inputs of Report 135. The following questions remain separate.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Can the regularized series be evaluated with explicit tail bounds
to certify useful decimal enclosures for $R,S,S_2,r_3$ and later
nonlogarithmic constants? Absolute convergence alone supplies no
numerical certificate at a stated truncation.
\item Can the local and off-chart estimates be made effective enough
to give practical constants and onset thresholds in the direct and
inverse remainders? A shrinking asymptotic error interval still does
not resolve exact integer rounding if it crosses an integer.
\item What is the growth of the coefficients as their order increases?
Convergence, Gevrey bounds, or summability of the infinite formal series
would require additional analysis. No exponentially small sector
expansion is established here.
\item Can the exact rational-diagonal description from Report 135 be
turned into a usable differential equation or recurrence that supports
certified large-index computation of the regularized constants?
\item Which other exact-pattern decompositions admit a comparable
weighted Banach expansion and a bilinear convolution reduction? The
coefficient theorem applies whenever its explicit hypotheses hold;
verifying those hypotheses for another pattern is a new problem.
\end{enumerate}
\subsection{Further questions and research}\label{upo:all:sub:further}
\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions above, 5 October 2026]
Questions 1--4: open (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, items 1, 4, 6 and~5). Question~5: advanced. Part~VI verifies the hypotheses of the analogous convolution theorem for one $12345$ (Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:half}), with a half-integer exponent and the opposite outcome (Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy}); Part~IV's spine convolution for one $1243$ has not been brought into this form (item~7). Report~137's \file{137-allorders-SOURCES.md} says that the underlying proof source was ``frozen and independently reviewed'' (SHA-256 \texttt{0070de0a\ldots}); that source and its review were not delivered, so the repository cannot check them, and nothing here rests on them. This Part states no claim without proof, and nothing in it was found to be wrong.
\end{writenote}

\clearpage
\part{Permutations with exactly one classical 1243 occurrence}\label{upo:1243:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~136, archive \file{A224179_Leading_Amplitude_Diffusive_Cancellation_and_Inversion_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{Report136.tex} (24 pages), dated 2~October 2026; title block ``Permutations with exactly one classical 1243 occurrence'', author line ``Report 136''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} notes, except that its Proposition~6 and Lemma~7, which are Report~134's Proposition~4 and Lemma~5 word for word, are printed once, in Part~I (Sections \ref{upo:1243:sec:tableaux} and~\ref{upo:1243:sec:fixed} keep their titles and point there), and its Appendix~A is printed as Section~\ref{upo:1243:app:CT}. Report~136 numbers its statements consecutively; here they are numbered within sections, with section numbers Report~136's plus~$29$. Section~\ref{upo:1243:sub:further} is added in the write.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.44\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~IV & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$b_n$: exactly one $1243$ (A224179); $B(z)=\sum b_nz^n$ & Parts I--III: $u_n$; Parts V--VI: $U_n$\\

\begin{partabstract}
Let $b_n$ count permutations with exactly one classical $1243$ occurrence,
and let $A_n$ count $1234$-avoiders. An exact marked-avoider bijection and a
shared decreasing spine give finite three-row tableau formulas for $b_n$.
A uniform Gaussian estimate for mismatched horizontal strips makes the
spine sum summable and proves
$b_n\sim R A_n\sim (81\sqrt3/(16\pi))R\,9^n n^{-4}$, where $R$ is an
explicit convergent positive series. An everywhere-defined 23-index
binomial encoding proves that the generating function is a rational
diagonal and hence D-finite; its coefficient scale implies
nonalgebraicity. A diffusive spine profile gives an exact endpoint-tail equivalent and
proves cancellation of the square-root correction, improving the relative
error to $o(n^{-1/2})$ and the inside-ceiling inverse uncertainty to
$o((\log x)^{-1/2})$. The value $R=149/160$ suggested in the literature remains
unresolved. No certified decimal enclosure, first nonzero correction,
or claim of worldwide historical priority is made.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
``Report~134'' and ``Report~135'' \cite{R134,R135} are Parts I and II of this report. Conway and Guttmann's value $149/160$ is still open; see the note after \eqref{upo:1243:eq:lower} and Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, item~2.
\end{writenote}


\section{The sequence and the conclusions}\label{upo:1243:sec:intro}
An occurrence of $1243$ in $\pi\in S_n$ is a quadruple of positions
$i<j<k<l$ with $\pi_i<\pi_j<\pi_l<\pi_k$. The positions need not be
consecutive. We count permutations with exactly one such quadruple;
additional $1234$ occurrences are allowed. Set $b_n=0$ for $0\le n<4$.
This is OEIS A224179~\cite{OEIS224179}; for $n=4,\ldots,15$ its values are
\begin{gather*}
1,11,88,638,4478,31199,218033,1535207,\\
10910759,78310579,567588264,4152765025.
\end{gather*}
Write $A_n=|\Av_n(1234)|=|\Av_n(1243)|$ and $A_0=1$.
West's classical bijection gives the equality of these avoidance counts;
we recall it, with attribution, in Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:marked}.
Regev's three-row, exponent-two strip theorem gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:regev}
 A_n\sim C_0\frac{9^n}{n^4},\qquad C_0=\frac{81\sqrt3}{16\pi}.
\end{equation}
This is the $d=1$, three-row, $\alpha=2$ specialization explicitly
recorded in~\cite[Section 3.1.4]{Regev2010}, confirming the ordinary strip
case of~\cite{Regev1981}. In particular there are positive constants
$a_0,a_1$ with
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Aglobal}
 a_0\frac{9^n}{(n+1)^4}\le A_n\le a_1\frac{9^n}{(n+1)^4}
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}

Here are the exact quantities in the answer. Let $F_m(p,q)$ count
$1234$-avoiders of size $m$ whose final $p$ entries decrease and whose
$q$ smallest values occur decreasingly. Empty and one-element conditions
are vacuous. Put
\begin{align}
 H_h(i,j)&=F_{h+2}(i+1,j+1)-F_{h+1}(i,j)
       &&(i+j\le h),\label{upo:1243:eq:H}\\
 J_k(s)&=\sum_{j=0}^s H_{s+k}(k,j),\qquad
 \gamma_k=\frac{27}{8}(5k+24)(k+2)3^{-k}.\label{upo:1243:eq:Jdef}
\end{align}
Set $H_h(i,j)=0$ when $i+j>h$. All indices are nonnegative unless stated
otherwise. Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:tableaux} gives finite tableau evaluations of
$F$ and proves the nonnegativity of the relevant $H$ values.

\begin{theorem}\label{upo:1243:thm:main}
The exact enumeration is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:spine}
 b_n=\sum_{\substack{s,t,k\ge0\\s+t+k=n-4}}J_k(s)J_k(t)
 \qquad(n\ge4).
\end{equation}
The amplitude ratio exists and has the positive convergent representation
\begin{align}
 R:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{b_n}{A_n}
 &=\frac2{9^4}\sum_{t,k\ge0}9^{-t}\gamma_kJ_k(t)\label{upo:1243:eq:amplitude}\\
 &=\frac1{972}\sum_{t,k\ge0}9^{-t}3^{-k}(5k+24)(k+2)J_k(t),
 \qquad 0<R<\infty.\notag
\end{align}
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:main-equivalent}
 b_n\sim C_0R\,\frac{9^n}{n^4}.
\end{equation}
The ordinary generating function $B(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}b_nz^n$ is the
diagonal of a rational power series over $\mathbb Q$. It is D-finite
but not algebraic over $\mathbb Q(z)$.
\end{theorem}

No scalar differential equation, recurrence, order bound, or efficient
elimination algorithm is computed here. The rational-diagonal assertion
is a structural consequence of the exact finite formula, not a guess
from initial coefficients. The equivalent alone gives the inverse statement in
Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:inverse}. The additional uniform argument of
Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:rate} gives
\[
 b_n=C_0R\,9^n n^{-4}(1+O(n^{-1/2}))
\]
and an inside-ceiling width $O((\log x)^{-1/2})$. The diffusive analysis
of Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:profile} improves both remainders to little-$o$ at
these scales, proving a zero square-root coefficient. It does not
identify the first nonzero correction or an all-orders expansion.

\subsection*{What is inherited and what is established here}
The rank-at-most-two skeleton bijection is West's prior work; the
exposition in B\'ona~\cite[Section 2, Lemma 2.2]{WestExposition} supplies
an accessible source. Regev's avoidance equivalent is also prior work.
The tableau formula for $F$ and its fixed-boundary limit were used in the
companion Report 134 on one $1234$ occurrence~\cite{R134}; their
proofs are reproduced here so that this report does not require a reader
to obtain that companion. The marked extension, shared-spine extraction,
23-index encoding, and uniform growing-spine analysis are the steps
proved for the present target. Standard RSK, Pieri, and hook-length
identities are used explicitly. The nonalgebraicity deduction uses the
general analytic lemma of B\'ona--Burstein~\cite[Lemma 6.3]{BB}; their
monotone-pattern combinatorial injection is not being applied to $1243$.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
In this report Report~134's tableau formula and fixed-boundary lemma are printed once, as Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F} and Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed}; Sections \ref{upo:1243:sec:tableaux} and~\ref{upo:1243:sec:fixed} below point there (Report~136 writes $B_p$ for Part~I's $c_p$). Report~135 is Part~II; Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:global-rate} below is a weaker, global second route to Part~II's Proposition~\ref{upo:rat:prop:boundary}. \cite[Lemma 6.3]{BB} was checked against arXiv:2101.00332v3: it is as quoted in Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:inverse}.
\end{writenote}

\section{From one occurrence to a marked avoider}\label{upo:1243:sec:marked}
For an entry of a permutation, its \emph{rank} is the length of a longest
increasing subsequence ending there. Call ranks at most two \emph{low}
and the remaining entries \emph{high}. Fix the positions and values of
the low entries; this is the low skeleton. List the remaining positions
as $h_1<\cdots<h_m$ and remaining values as $v_1<\cdots<v_m$.
A cell $(h_i,y)$ is allowed if some two low entries at positions $a<b<h_i$
have values $\pi_a<\pi_b<y$.

The allowed board is northeast closed: increasing position or value
preserves allowance. Every high entry has a supporting low pair. Indeed,
a chain ending there can be started at a rank-one point and continued to
a rank-two point before its high part. Conversely, any full matching of
high positions with high values using allowed cells preserves the low
skeleton. A filled high point has rank at least three. It cannot precede
and lie below a low point, since then that low point would also have a
low supporting pair and rank at least three. Thus the ranks of low
points remain unchanged.

Let $d_i$ be the number of high values globally allowed at $h_i$, with
$d_0=0$. These numbers are nondecreasing. Every value placed in an earlier
high position is allowed at $h_i$, so the number of remaining choices is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:choices}
 a_i=d_i-i+1\ge1.
\end{equation}
The inequality follows from the existence of the original matching and
is independent of earlier choices. Choosing the $(e_i+1)$st smallest
remaining allowed value, where $0\le e_i<a_i$, is therefore an exact code
for all matchings of the skeleton.

An \emph{active inversion} consists of high positions $h_i<h_j$ with
values $x>y$, where $y$ is already allowed at $h_i$. Exactly $e_i$ active
inversions start at $h_i$: the $e_i$ smaller allowed values skipped there
all appear later. Thus their number is $\sum_i e_i$. An active inversion
with smaller value $y$ has exactly
\[
 s_\pi(h_i,y)=\#\{a<b<h_i:\pi_a<\pi_b<y\}
\]
supporting pairs, and this positive integer is its number of $1243$
occurrences. Every $1243$ occurrence arises in this way: its last two entries are
high, and its supporting pair either is low or has an earlier low
supporting pair below it. Thus the smaller top value is already allowed
at the earlier high row. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:active}
 N_{1243}(\pi)=\sum_{\text{active inversions }(h_i,h_j)}
                    s_\pi(h_i,\pi_{h_j}).
\end{equation}

The all-zero code is the unique $1243$-avoiding matching: fill each high
position from left to right with the smallest remaining allowed value.
Call it the \emph{greedy avoider} $\sigma$. There is also a unique
$1234$-avoiding matching, obtained by filling the high values in
decreasing order. It is allowed because the largest remaining value is
allowed whenever any remaining value is. It avoids $1234$ because the
last two entries of an increasing four-chain would be increasing high
points; conversely every increasing pair of high points extends the low
support of its first point to such a chain. This is West's classical
skeleton-preserving bijection~\cite{WestExposition}.

At step $i$ of the greedy filling, write $y$ for the smallest remaining
allowed value. If a second choice exists, write $z$ for it. Call row $i$
\emph{eligible} when $a_i\ge2$ and $s_\sigma(h_i,y)=1$.

\begin{theorem}[The marked extension]\label{upo:1243:thm:marked}
Swapping $y$ and $z$ in a greedy avoider at an eligible row is a bijection
from eligible marked $1243$-avoiders to permutations containing exactly
one $1243$. In particular, if $M(\sigma)$ is the number of eligible rows,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:marked}
 b_n=\sum_{\sigma\in\Av_n(1243)}M(\sigma).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:active}, a matching with one occurrence has one active
inversion of support one. Its code has one digit equal to one and every
other digit zero. Compare it with the greedy matching. At its unique
exceptional row it selects $z$ rather than $y$. Until the greedy matching
selects $z$, both matchings then choose the same entries: newly admitted
values are below $y$, and no value already admitted at the exceptional
row lies strictly between $y$ and $z$. At the row where the greedy
matching selects $z$, the exceptional one selects $y$. Thereafter their
remaining sets agree. They differ exactly by that transposition.
Their prefixes before the exceptional row agree, so the unique active
inversion has the same support count $s_\sigma(h_i,y)$. Hence it gives
exactly one occurrence precisely at an eligible row. Conversely this
transposition is valid, since $y$ remains allowed later, and its code
has the required single exceptional digit. The unique occurrence
identifies the active inversion; swapping it back recovers the greedy
avoider and the mark. This proves both directions and uniqueness.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A local corner criterion]\label{upo:1243:lem:corner}
Let $y_i$ be the smallest high value globally allowed at row $h_i$.
A row is eligible if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:corner}
 d_i\ge i+1,\qquad d_i>d_{i-1},\qquad
 \text{exactly one low increasing pair lies southwest of }(h_i,y_i).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
After the preceding greedy step, the minimum high value used so far is
the smallest globally allowed value at that preceding row. This follows
inductively: a newly admitted minimum is immediately chosen, and without
a new minimum the old minimum stays unchanged. If $d_i>d_{i-1}$, the new
global minimum $y_i$ is unused and below every earlier high value. It is
the greedy choice. Thus no earlier high point lies southwest of
$(h_i,y_i)$ and its supporting pairs are all low. The second-choice
condition $a_i\ge2$ is exactly $d_i\ge i+1$.

If $d_i=d_{i-1}$, the global minimum was used earlier. The present greedy
candidate $y$ lies above that earlier high value $x$. A low supporting
pair $a<b<x$ for $x$ precedes the present row and lies below $y$; the
three increasing pairs $(a,b),(a,x),(b,x)$ then support $y$. Such a row
cannot be eligible. This proves the criterion, including the initial row
with $d_0=0$.
\end{proof}

For clarity, the southwest condition is genuinely local in the low
skeleton. Rank-one points decrease, and rank-two points decrease. Let
$b$ be the last rank-two point before $h_i$, and $b'$ the preceding such
point if it exists. Let $a$ be the last rank-one point preceding $b$, and
$a'$ its predecessor. The unique-low-pair condition says
$b<y_i<b'$ and $a<b<a'$, where an absent predecessor has value $+\infty$.
Every increasing low pair ends at a rank-two point and starts at a
rank-one point, which proves these equivalences. At an eligible row,
$b$ lies after the preceding high position; otherwise the board threshold
could not fall.

This mark is not uniformly bounded on individual permutations. For
example, the avoider
$(1,2q,2q+1,2q-2,2q-1,\ldots,2,3,2q+2)$ has $q$ eligible rows.
A limiting average therefore requires a uniform estimate; fixed-boundary
limits by themselves do not suffice.

\section{The shared decreasing spine}\label{upo:1243:sec:spine}
Transport an eligible mark to the $1234$-avoider with the same skeleton.
In that avoider, all high values decrease. At row $h_i$ write $z$ for its
value and $y=y_i$ for the newly allowed minimum. The smallest among the
$d_i$ globally allowed high values occurs at high position $h_{d_i}$.
Since $d_i\ge i+1$, it occurs after $z$. Write $a,b$ for the unique low
support in increasing position and value order. The marked core is
$a,b,z,y$ in position order and $a<b<y<z$ in value order.

Equivalently: the rectangle before $z$ and below $y$ contains exactly one
increasing pair, namely $a,b$; $a$ has rank one and $b$ rank two; $z$ is
the first high point after $b$; and $y$ is the smallest high value above
$b$. The corner criterion proves these assertions. In particular the
last rank-two point before $z$ is $b$, which appears after the previous
high row; the allowed values at $z$ are precisely the high values above
$b$. Conversely these four assertions and a later high point $y<z$
recover~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:corner}.

Use cell coordinates relative to the four marked positions $a,b,z,y$
and the four marked values $a,b,y,z$: coordinate $(u,v)$ means $u$ marked
positions lie to the left and $v$ marked values lie below. The only cells
for extra points are
\begin{align}
 \mathcal N&=\{(0,2),(0,3),(0,4),(1,3),(1,4)\},\notag\\
 \mathcal T&=\{(2,0),(3,0),(3,1),(4,0),(4,1)\},\label{upo:1243:eq:cells}\\
 &\hspace{25mm}\text{and the central cell }(3,3).\notag
\end{align}
Here is the necessity check. Before $a$, values below $b$ contradict
rank one of $a$ or uniqueness of the southwest support. Between $a,b$,
values below $y$ create another support. Between $b,z$, values in $(a,y)$
do likewise, while values above $y$ would be earlier high points after
$b$. Between $z,y$, values in $(b,y)$ contradict minimality of the high
value $y$, and values above $z$ complete an increasing four-chain. After
$y$, values in $(b,y)$ again contradict minimality, and values above $y$
complete the four-chain $a,b,y,$ followed by that point. These exclusions
leave exactly~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:cells}.

The points of cell $(0,2)$ decrease, as do those of $(2,0)$; otherwise
each increasing pair there would be another southwest support. The
central points, together with $z,y$, form a decreasing spine $W$ of
length $r\ge2$. They all have rank three, since they are above and after
$a,b$ and no increasing four-chain is allowed.

Every $\mathcal N$ point is before $b$ and above $b$; every $\mathcal T$
point is after $b$ and below $b$. Thus these two sets are mutually
inverted, and no increasing chain uses both types. The whole permutation
avoids $1234$ if and only if both restrictions
\[
 \mathcal N\cup\{a,b\}\cup W,
 \qquad \mathcal T\cup\{a,b\}\cup W
\]
avoid it. Call these the northern and southern halves. They share the
entire core $12\oplus\delta_r$, where $\delta_r$ is a decreasing word of
length $r$. Inversion exchanges the two types and fixes the standardized
core.

\begin{lemma}[Unique amalgamation]\label{upo:1243:lem:glue}
Two valid halves with the same spine length $r$ amalgamate uniquely into
an eligible marked $1234$-avoider. No shuffle factor is needed.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The north half supplies all comparisons involving northern positions
and spine values; the south half supplies all comparisons involving
southern values and spine positions. Northern versus southern
comparisons are forced by their mutual inversion. The interleavings of
southern points among spine positions and northern values among spine
values already belong to the two half words. Consequently there is one
amalgamation.

No four-chain can use both types, so the two avoidance conditions ensure
global avoidance. The other points of the southwest support rectangle form a decreasing
group before $a$ with values between $b,y$, and a decreasing group
after $b$ but before $z$ with values below $a$. Neither group supplies
an increasing pair internally, with the other group, or with $a,b$.
Thus $a,b$ is its sole increasing pair. Points between
$b,z$ are decreasing new left-to-right minima; thus $z$ is the first high
point after $b$. Points whose values are between $b,y$ lie decreasingly
before $a$ and have rank one; hence $y$ is the smallest high value above
$b$. The reconstructed mark satisfies the equivalent corner assertions
above. This proves sufficiency as well as uniqueness. Necessity follows
by taking restrictions of the original marked avoider.
\end{proof}

Let $N_r(s)$ count northern halves with $s$ extra points outside the
spine and $a,b$. The preceding bijections yield
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Nproduct}
 b_n=\sum_{r\ge2}\ \sum_{s+t=n-r-2}N_r(s)N_r(t).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Identifying the half count]\label{upo:1243:lem:half}
For $r\ge2$ and $s\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Nhalf}
 N_r(s)=\sum_{q=1}^{s+1}
 \bigl(F_{s+r}(r-1,q)-F_{s+r-1}(r-2,q-1)\bigr)
 =J_{r-2}(s).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A standardized northern half has $a=1,b=2$ and word $U1V2W$, where $W$
is a decreasing suffix of length $r$, ending at $y$. All $V$ values
exceed $y$, while the $j$ values $3,\ldots,j+2$ below $y=j+3$ occur
decreasingly in $U$. Put $q=j+1$, so $1\le q\le s+1$. Delete $b=2$ and
the final point $y$, then standardize. The result has size $s+r$, avoids
$1234$, has a decreasing suffix of length $r-1$, and has its $q$ smallest
values decreasing. That suffix is disjoint from those $q$ values.

Conversely, take any avoider with these properties. Append a new final
value of rank $q+1$, shifting larger values up. It cannot complete a
four-chain because the $q$ smaller values decrease. Insert a new value
$2$ immediately before the designated suffix (now of length $r$),
keeping the old minimum $1$ and shifting the other values. This insertion
cannot create a four-chain: there is only one smaller value and the
later spine decreases. The decreasing $q$ smallest entries end at the
old minimum and occur before the suffix, so the result has precisely the
required word $U1V2W$, geometry, and boundary decrease. The operations
are inverse.

For a nonempty decreasing suffix and a decreasing set of $q$ smallest
values, the two sets intersect if and only if the final value is $1$.
Indeed an intersection puts the minimum later in the decreasing suffix,
where it must be final. Deleting that final $1$ is a bijection to
$F_{s+r-1}(r-2,q-1)$. Subtracting the intersecting cases gives the
summand in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Nhalf}. With $k=r-2$ and $j=q-1$, it is
$H_{s+k}(k,j)$. Summing proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

Substituting~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Nhalf} into~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Nproduct} proves the exact
formula~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:spine}. In particular, as an identity of formal series,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:formal}
 B(z)=z^4\sum_{k\ge0}z^kJ_k(z)^2,
 \qquad J_k(z)=\sum_{s\ge0}J_k(s)z^s.
\end{equation}
Every coefficient here uses finitely many terms. No analytic convergence
is needed for this exact identity.

\section{The exact tableau formula}\label{upo:1243:sec:tableaux}
\begin{writenote}[Printed in Part~I]
Report~136's Section~4 states and proves its Proposition~6, which is Report~134's Proposition~4 word for word, with $B_p$ in place of $c_p$. It is printed as Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:F}, with its displays \eqref{upo:amp:eq:F}, \eqref{upo:amp:eq:W} and \eqref{upo:amp:eq:hook}; references to them below point there. Report~136 closes the section with the following paragraph, printed as delivered.
\end{writenote}
These formulas are finite and exact. They also show
$A_m=F_m(0,0)=\sum_{\ell(\rho)\le3}(f^\rho)^2$.
These inherited finite and fixed-boundary formulas are reproduced from
Report 134~\cite{R134}. For $i+j\le h$, the difference in
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:H} counts exactly the size-$h+2$ avoiders with decreasing
suffix of length $i+1$ and decreasing $j+1$ smallest values for which
those two sets are disjoint. The final-minimum deletion argument in
Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:half} proves this assertion, hence $H_h(i,j)\ge0$.
The remaining argument requires a bound uniform in every boundary parameter.

\section{Fixed boundary asymptotics}\label{upo:1243:sec:fixed}
\begin{writenote}[Printed in Part~I]
Report~136's Section~5, with its definition $B_p=\binom{p+2}2$, $d_p=B_p3^{-p}$ and its Lemma~7 and proof, is Report~134's Section~5 word for word, with $B_p$ in place of $c_p$. It is printed as \eqref{upo:amp:eq:d} and Lemma~\ref{upo:amp:lem:fixed}; the limit $C(i,j)$ used below is \eqref{upo:amp:eq:Climit}.
\end{writenote}
\section{Uniform Gaussian estimates}\label{upo:1243:sec:gaussian}
For a three-row partition $\mu$ of $r$, put
\[M_r(\mu)=\max_i|\mu_i-r/3|,\qquad B_p=\binom{p+2}{2}.\]
All constants $K,c>0$ below are absolute; their values may change from
line to line. Their existence, rather than numerical optimization, is
what the limit argument needs.

\begin{lemma}[Global three-row Gaussian tail]\label{upo:1243:lem:tail}
There are constants $K,c>0$ such that, for all integers $r\ge0$ and all
real $x\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:tail}
 \frac1{A_r}\sum_{M_r(\mu)\ge x}(f^\mu)^2
 \le K\exp\left(-\frac{cx^2}{r+1}\right).
\end{equation}
In particular, this statement is uniform at empty and small shapes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We first record the uniform local multinomial bound
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:multinomial}
 \binom{N}{x_1,x_2,x_3}
 \le\frac{K3^N}{N+1}
 \exp\left(-c\frac{\sum_i(x_i-N/3)^2}{N}\right)
 \quad(N\ge1).
\end{equation}
Here $x_i$ are nonnegative integers summing to $N$.
For completeness, put $q_i=x_i/N$ and
$D(q)=\sum_iq_i\log(3q_i)$. Entropy convexity gives
$D(q)\ge\frac12\sum_i(q_i-1/3)^2$: on the interior of the simplex the
Hessian of $\sum_iq_i\log q_i$ is diagonal with entries $1/q_i\ge1$,
and the boundary follows by continuity. Also
$3^{-N}\binom{N}{x_1,x_2,x_3}\le e^{-ND(q)}$, since the multinomial
probability at parameter $q$ of that count vector is at most one.
If all $x_i\ge N/6$, uniform Stirling bounds supply the additional factor
$K/N$, proving~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:multinomial}. Otherwise
$S:=\sum_i(x_i-N/3)^2\ge N^2/36$, and
\[
 e^{-S/(2N)}\le\frac K N e^{-S/(4N)},
\]
because $N e^{-N/144}$ is bounded. This proves~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:multinomial}
throughout the simplex.

For the hook formula set
\[
 (x_1,x_2,x_3)=(a+2,b+1,c),\qquad N=r+3.
\]
Then
\[
 f^{(a,b,c)}=
 \binom{N}{x_1,x_2,x_3}
 \frac{(x_1-x_2)(x_1-x_3)(x_2-x_3)}{(r+1)(r+2)(r+3)}.
\]
The shifted deviations satisfy
$x_i-N/3=\mu_i-r/3+(2-i)$. Thus, with $M=M_r(\mu)$,
\[
 \max_i|x_i-N/3|\ge(M-1)_+,
 \qquad \prod_{i<j}|x_i-x_j|\le8(M+1)^3.
\]
Since $N\le3(r+1)$ and $(M-1)_+^2\ge M^2/2-1$, equation
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:multinomial} gives
\[
 f^\mu\le
 K3^r(r+1)^{-4}(M+1)^3
 e^{-cM^2/(r+1)}.
\]
Squaring, writing $T=M/\sqrt{r+1}$, and absorbing the factor
$(1+T)^6$ into a weaker Gaussian yields the pointwise bound
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:localdimension}
 (f^\mu)^2\le K9^r(r+1)^{-5}
 e^{-cM_r(\mu)^2/(r+1)}.
\end{equation}
All these inequalities apply also to $r=0$, where $N=3$.

For $M\ge x$, split the exponential in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:localdimension}
into two equal factors and extract $e^{-cx^2/(2(r+1))}$.
The sum of the remaining factor over partitions of $r$ is $O(r+1)$.
Indeed, extend the first two row coordinates to all integers and use
\[
 M^2\ge\tfrac12\bigl((a-r/3)^2+(b-r/3)^2\bigr),
 \qquad
 \sup_{u\in\mathbb R}\sum_{h\in\mathbb Z}
 e^{-c(h-u)^2/(r+1)}\le K\sqrt{r+1}.
\]
The tail sum is therefore at most
$K9^r(r+1)^{-4}e^{-cx^2/(r+1)}$.
Dividing by the lower bound in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Aglobal} proves the result.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Gaussian mismatch for strip sums]\label{upo:1243:lem:mismatch}
For all $m\ge0$ and $0\le p,q\le m$, put
$r=m-p$, $u=m-q$, and $D=|p-q|=|r-u|$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:mismatch}
 F_m(p,q)\le
 K B_pB_q\sqrt{A_rA_u}\,
 \exp\left(-\frac{cD^2}{\max(r,u)+1}\right).
\end{equation}
Replacing the denominator by $m+1$ is also valid. For $D=0$, one may
instead use the sharp elementary prefactor bound
$F_m(p,p)\le B_p^2A_{m-p}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $T_p$ be the zero-one horizontal-strip incidence matrix, with rows
indexed by outer shapes of size $m$ and columns by inner shapes of size
$r=m-p$. Every row and every column has at most $B_p$ ones: the numbers
of boxes removed from or added to the three rows form a weak composition
of $p$. Its Euclidean operator norm is at most $B_p$, either by the
row/column Schur bound or by two applications of Cauchy--Schwarz.
If $v_r$ is the vector with entries $f^\mu$, then
$W_p=T_pv_r$ and $\|v_r\|_2^2=A_r$.

Suppose that $\mu,\nu$ are inner shapes of sizes $r,u$ admitting the same
outer shape $\lambda$. By symmetry assume $u\ge r$. Interlacing implies
$\mu_1\ge\lambda_2\ge\nu_2$, and consequently
\[
 \frac{u-r}{3}
 \le (u/3-\nu_2)+(\mu_1-r/3)
 \le M_u(\nu)+M_r(\mu).
\]
Hence $M_r(\mu)\ge D/6$ or $M_u(\nu)\ge D/6$.
Let $v_r^{\rm tail},v_u^{\rm tail}$ be the vectors restricted to these
respective tail sets. Positivity and the preceding support implication give
\begin{align*}
 F_m(p,q)
 &\le \langle T_pv_r^{\rm tail},T_qv_u\rangle
       +\langle T_pv_r,T_qv_u^{\rm tail}\rangle\\
 &\le B_pB_q\sqrt{A_rA_u}
 \left(\frac{\|v_r^{\rm tail}\|_2}{\sqrt{A_r}}
       +\frac{\|v_u^{\rm tail}\|_2}{\sqrt{A_u}}\right).
\end{align*}
Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:tail}, followed by square roots, bounds the parentheses by
\[
 K\left(e^{-cD^2/(r+1)}+e^{-cD^2/(u+1)}\right)
 \le K e^{-cD^2/(\max(r,u)+1)}.
\]
This also covers $D=0$ and empty inner shapes.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Triangular normalized bound]\label{upo:1243:lem:triangle}
There are constants $K,c>0$ such that, whenever $p,q\ge0$ and $p+q\le m$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:triangle}
 \frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_m}
 \le K B_pB_q3^{-p-q}
 \exp\left(-\frac{c(p-q)^2}{m+1}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Aglobal} and Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:mismatch}, the right side before
simplification has the additional rational factor
\[
 R_{m,p,q}=\frac{(m+1)^4}{(m-p+1)^2(m-q+1)^2}.
\]
Write $r=m-p$, $u=m-q$; the support assumption gives $r+u\ge m$.
If $\min(r,u)\ge m/4$, then $R_{m,p,q}\le4^4$.
Otherwise $D=|u-r|\ge m/2$ and $R_{m,p,q}\le(m+1)^4$.
For $m\ge1$, a half of the available Gaussian exponent absorbs this
polynomial, since
\[
 \sup_{m\ge1}(m+1)^4
 \exp\left(-\frac{c m^2}{8(m+1)}\right)<\infty.
\]
The case $m=0$ is immediate. Weakening $c$ proves the assertion.
\end{proof}


\section{Summability along the shared spine}\label{upo:1243:sec:endpoints}
Recall
\[
 H_h(i,j)=F_{h+2}(i+1,j+1)-F_{h+1}(i,j)
 \quad\text{for }i+j\le h,
\]
and zero otherwise. By Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:half} and the discussion after the tableau formula, $H$ is nonnegative, so
$0\le H_h(i,j)\le F_{h+2}(i+1,j+1)$. Define
\[
 J_k(s)=\sum_{j=0}^s H_{s+k}(k,j),\qquad
 d_p=B_p3^{-p},\qquad
 C(k,j)=81d_{k+1}d_{j+1}-9d_kd_j,
\]
and
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:gamma}
 \gamma_k=\sum_{j\ge0}C(k,j)
 =\frac{27}{8}(5k+24)(k+2)3^{-k}.
\end{equation}
The formula follows from $\sum_jd_j=27/8$ and
$\sum_jd_{j+1}=19/8$. In particular
$\gamma_k\asymp(k+1)^2 3^{-k}$, uniformly for $k\ge0$.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform $J$ bounds and endpoint summability]\label{upo:1243:prop:J}
For every $s,k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Jdom}
 J_k(s)\le K A_{s+k}\gamma_k.
\end{equation}
For every fixed $k$, the known fixed-boundary limits of $H$ imply
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Jlimit}
 \frac{J_k(s)}{A_{s+k}}\longrightarrow\gamma_k
 \qquad(s\longrightarrow\infty).
\end{equation}
Finally, for
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:e}
 e_t=9^{-t}\sum_{k\ge0}\gamma_kJ_k(t),
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:esummable}
 0\le e_t\le K(t+1)^{-3/2}.
\end{equation}
In particular the positive endpoint series is finite, and its tail above
$T$ is $O((T+1)^{-1/2})$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
In the $j$th summand of $J_k(s)$, take
$m=s+k+2$, $p=k+1$, $q=j+1$. Since $j\le s$, one has $p+q\le m$.
Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:triangle}, the bounded ratio $A_{h+2}/A_h$, and the
summability of $B_{j+1}3^{-j}$ show
\[
 \frac{H_{s+k}(k,j)}{A_{s+k}}
 \le K B_{k+1}B_{j+1}3^{-k-j},
 \qquad
 \frac{J_k(s)}{A_{s+k}}
 \le K(k+1)^2 3^{-k}\le K\gamma_k.
\]
The same summable majorant in $j$ permits dominated summation of the
fixed-boundary limits
$H_{s+k}(k,j)/A_{s+k}\longrightarrow C(k,j)$, proving~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jlimit}.

For endpoint summability it is important to retain the original,
un-normalized denominator factors rather than only~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jdom}.
Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:mismatch} and~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Aglobal} give, for $j\le t$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:endpointterm}
 9^{-t}\gamma_k H_{t+k}(k,j)
 \le K\frac{(k+1)^4(j+1)^2 3^{-j}}
              {(t+2)^2(t+k-j+2)^2}
 \exp\left(-\frac{c(k-j)^2}{t+k+3}\right).
\end{equation}
Indeed the two inner sizes are $t+1$ and $t+k+1-j$, and their exponential
factor $3^{2t+k+2-j}$ cancels $9^t3^k$, up to an absolute constant.

First sum terms with $j\ge k/2$. Discard the exponential and the second
inverse-square denominator. Then $k\le2j$, and
\[
 \sum_{j\ge0}\sum_{0\le k\le2j}
 (k+1)^4(j+1)^2 3^{-j}<\infty.
\]
This region contributes $O((t+2)^{-2})$.

For $j<k/2$, one has $k-j>k/2$ and
$t+k-j+2\ge(t+k+3)/2$. Sum the geometrically weighted $j$ factor to bound
this region by
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:ksum}
 \frac K{(t+2)^2}\sum_{k\ge0}
 \frac{(k+1)^4}{(t+k+3)^2}
 \exp\left(-\frac{c k^2}{t+k+3}\right).
\end{equation}
For $k\le t+1$, the two inverse-square denominators together are
$O((t+1)^{-4})$, and the exponential is at most
$e^{-c'k^2/(t+1)}$. The elementary Gaussian-moment estimate
\[
 \sum_{k\ge0}(k+1)^4 e^{-c'k^2/(t+1)}
 \le K(t+1)^{5/2}
\]
therefore gives $O((t+1)^{-3/2})$.
For $k>t+1$, one has $t+k+3\le3k$, so this portion of the sum
in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:ksum} is at most
\[
 \frac K{(t+2)^2}\sum_{k\ge1}(k+1)^2e^{-c'k}
 =O((t+1)^{-2}).
\]
Combining the three bounds proves~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:esummable}.
\end{proof}

\section{Proof of the leading equivalent}\label{upo:1243:sec:leading}
The exact identity~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:spine} has now been proved, and all uniform
estimates needed to pass to its limit are available. Orient the sum by
requiring $s\ge t$. Equivalently, sum over $(t,k)\in\N^2$, set
$s=n-k-t-4$, keep the term only when $s\ge t$, and give it the factor
$2-\mathbf1_{s=t}$. This counts all triples exactly, including the
diagonal. On its support,
\[
 \frac{n+1}{s+k+1}\le5.
\]
The global bound~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Aglobal} and~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jdom} therefore give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:convdom}
 (2-\mathbf1_{s=t})\frac{J_k(s)J_k(t)}{A_n}
 \le K9^{-t}\gamma_kJ_k(t).
\end{equation}
The right side is summable over all $t,k$ by~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:esummable}.
For each fixed $t,k$, eventually $s>t$ and $s\to\infty$. The fixed-spine
limit~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jlimit} and Regev's ratio
$A_{n-t-4}/A_n\to9^{-t-4}$ give
\[
 (2-\mathbf1_{s=t})\frac{J_k(n-k-t-4)J_k(t)}{A_n}
 \longrightarrow2\cdot9^{-t-4}\gamma_kJ_k(t).
\]
Extend summands by zero off the oriented support. Dominated convergence
proves~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:amplitude}, including every diagonal and middle term.
The limit is finite by endpoint summability. It is positive because
$J_0(0)=H_0(0,0)=1$ and $\gamma_0=162$. Multiplying by
Regev's equivalent proves~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:main-equivalent}.

\subsection*{What the series says about the amplitude}
Conway--Guttmann~\cite[Section 5.2, printed pages 20--23]{CG} numerically
estimate a ratio near $0.93125$ and tentatively suggest $149/160$.
The positive series here establishes a ratio's existence and finiteness,
but does not determine whether it equals that rational number.
For every integer $T\ge0$ the rational sum
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:lower}
 L_T=\frac1{972}\sum_{k+t\le T}
 9^{-t}3^{-k}(5k+24)(k+2)J_k(t)
\end{equation}
is a rigorous lower bound for $R$. The included exact certificate
computes
\[
 L_{35}=\frac{55999844944982350869187286355724963}
                 {90113598179756697647360591499090564},
\]
whose numerical rendering is about $0.621436121475$.
This is a rendering of a lower bound, not a certified approximation to
$R$. Equation~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:esummable} gives
$\sum_{t>T}e_t=O((T+1)^{-1/2})$ with an unspecified absolute constant;
it gives no convenient certified numerical error bar for~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:lower}.
It also does not by itself yield an asymptotic correction to $b_n$.
Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:profile} subsequently proves a tail equivalent and,
by a separate matched-interior argument, cancellation at the square-root
scale.
\begin{writenote}[On $149/160$, 5 October 2026]
Conway and Guttmann (arXiv:2306.12682v2, Section~5.2) report that the ratio tends to an apparent limit $0.93125\pm0.00015$ and write that $149/160=0.93125$ would be a likely candidate if the limit were a simple fraction; they prove nothing about it. The lower bound $L_{35}\approx0.6214$ is far below, and the tail equivalent of Theorem~\ref{upo:1243:thm:profile}, of order $T^{-1/2}$, explains the slow convergence of finite sums (it concerns the tail in $t$ only, not the triangle $k+t>T$ omitted by $L_T$). The intake dossier for this batch extrapolated the exact ratios $b_n/A_n=0.7548$, $0.8106$, $0.8299$ at $n=40$, $60$, $72$ naively in powers of $1/n$ and obtained $0.93157$--$0.93177$, consistent with $149/160$. That is not a certificate: the correction structure beyond the vanishing $n^{-1/2}$ term is unknown. The question is open (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, item~2).
\end{writenote}

\section{An everywhere defined binomial encoding}\label{upo:1243:sec:BLS}
We now derive the rational-diagonal claim directly from the exact
formula. The result used is Bostan--Lairez--Salvy~\cite{BLS},
Definition 1.1, Theorem 3.5 and Corollary 3.6 in arXiv version 2:
their algebra of binomial sums is closed under products, integral affine
substitutions, and directed partial summation. A univariate binomial sum
supported on $\N$ has a rational-diagonal generating function and a
nonzero polynomial-coefficient recurrence. It is important to exhibit
this closure construction, rather than invoke an unrestricted claim
about finite hypergeometric sums.

For all integers $a,b$, define
\[
 \mathcal B(a,b)=[z^b](1+z)^a.
\]
The formal expansion at zero is intended. The value is zero for $b<0$;
for $b\ge0$ it is the falling-factorial polynomial in $a$, divided by
$b!$. Negative upper arguments are allowed. Define
\[
 h(r)=\mathcal B(r,r),\qquad \delta(r)=\mathcal B(0,r).
\]
These are the indicators of $r\ge0$ and $r=0$, respectively. All
expressions below are therefore defined at every integer tuple.

For an arbitrary integer triple $x=(x_1,x_2,x_3)$ write $M=|x|$ and
$e_i=x_i-i+\sigma(i)$ for $\sigma\in S_3$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:binomialD}
 D(x)=\sum_{\sigma\in S_3}\sgn(\sigma)
 \mathcal B(M,e_1)\mathcal B(M-e_1,e_2)\mathcal B(e_3,e_3).
\end{equation}
For a nonnegative partition $x$ this equals $f^x$. To check this,
$e_1+e_2+e_3=M$; a negative $e_i$ makes the corresponding product zero,
and otherwise the product is $M!/(e_1!e_2!e_3!)$. Thus the signed sum is
$M!\det(1/(x_i-i+j)!)$, with negative reciprocal factorials interpreted
as zero only in this verification. Factoring with $X_i=x_i+3-i$ leaves
falling-factorial columns of degrees $2,1,0$, hence a Vandermonde and
exactly the hook formula~\eqref{upo:amp:eq:hook}. Formula~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:binomialD}
itself uses no potentially undefined factorials.

For integer triples $\lambda,\mu$ define
\begin{align*}
 I(\lambda,\mu)={}&h(\lambda_1-\mu_1)h(\mu_1-\lambda_2)
 h(\lambda_2-\mu_2)\\
 &\quad\cdot h(\mu_2-\lambda_3)h(\lambda_3-\mu_3)h(\mu_3).
\end{align*}
It is the indicator of
$\lambda_1\ge\mu_1\ge\lambda_2\ge\mu_2\ge\lambda_3\ge\mu_3\ge0$,
which forces both triples to be partitions and is precisely
horizontal-strip interlacing. Set
\begin{align}
 E(m,p,q;\lambda,\mu,\nu)={}&
 \delta(|\lambda|-m)\delta(|\mu|-m+p)\delta(|\nu|-m+q)
 \notag\\[-2pt]
 &\quad\cdot I(\lambda,\mu)I(\lambda,\nu)D(\mu)D(\nu).
 \label{upo:1243:eq:E}
\end{align}
For $0\le p,q\le m$ and every integer $N\ge m$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:boxF}
 F_m(p,q)=\sum_{\lambda,\mu,\nu\in\{0,\ldots,N\}^3}
 E(m,p,q;\lambda,\mu,\nu).
\end{equation}
Indeed the size indicators and interlacings reproduce the tableau
formula and every surviving coordinate is at most $m$.

Use 23 summation variables, grouped as
\[
 z=(k,s,t,j,l,\lambda,\mu,\nu,\Lambda,\eta,\theta),
\]
where the final six symbols denote triples. For
$\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}$, define
\begin{align}
 Q_{\alpha,\beta}(n,z)={}&
 \delta(s+t+k-n+4)h(s-j)h(t-l)\notag\\
 &\cdot E(s+k+2-\alpha,k+1-\alpha,j+1-\alpha;
                  \lambda,\mu,\nu)\notag\\
 &\cdot E(t+k+2-\beta,k+1-\beta,l+1-\beta;
                  \Lambda,\eta,\theta).\label{upo:1243:eq:Q}
\end{align}
The complete formula is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:master}
 b_n=\sum_{\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{\alpha+\beta}
 \sum_{z\in\{0,\ldots,n+2\}^{23}}Q_{\alpha,\beta}(n,z)
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
The first indicator enforces $s+t+k=n-4$, and the next two enforce
$j\le s,l\le t$. The binary choices expand the two differences defining
$H$, giving exactly~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:spine}. For $n<4$ the size condition is
impossible. For $n\ge4$, all five outer indices are at most $n-4$, the
outer tableau sizes are at most $n-2$, and the bound $n+2$ contains every
surviving coordinate. All surviving $F$ calls have $0\le p,q\le m$.

Expanding the four $D$ factors leaves at most $4\cdot6^4=5184$ signed
products of $\mathcal B$ at integral affine forms. This number and the
23 summation indices are fixed independently of $n$. Implement the
23 box sums by directed partial sums with fresh upper-bound variables,
then set every upper bound to $n+2$. This defines a binomial sum for all
integer $n$; multiply by $h(n)$ to give support on $\N$. For $n\ge0$
the directed sums are the ordinary finite sums in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:master}.
The cited Bostan--Lairez--Salvy theorem now gives a rational diagonal and
P-recursiveness. Equivalently $B(z)$ is D-finite. No statement about
arbitrary patterns or higher prescribed occurrence counts follows.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
The check of Remark~\ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} (Definition~1.1, Theorem~3.5 and Corollary~3.6 of the cited arXiv version) applies to this encoding without change.
\end{writenote}

\section{Nonalgebraicity and inverse thresholds}\label{upo:1243:sec:inverse}
The positive equivalent gives $b_n\asymp9^n/n^4$ for large $n$.
B\'ona--Burstein~\cite[Lemma 6.3]{BB} prove the following general analytic
obstruction: a nonnegative power series whose coefficients lie between
positive multiples of $K^n/n^m$ for every positive index, where integer
$m>1$, is not algebraic. The initial zeros of $b_n$ require care.
Apply their lemma to
\[
 \widetilde B(z)=B(z)+z+z^2+z^3.
\]
For $n\ge4$, $b_n>0$ because prepending a new maximum to $1243$ repeatedly
preserves its unique occurrence. After adjusting constants for finitely
many indices, the coefficients of $\widetilde B$ satisfy the displayed
bounds for every $n\ge1$, with $m=4,K=9$. Thus $\widetilde B$ is
nonalgebraic. Adding a polynomial preserves algebraicity, so $B$ is
nonalgebraic as well. This argument uses the general analytic lemma,
not that paper's injection for one decreasing pattern.

Prepending a new maximum to any permutation cannot create a $1243$
occurrence containing that new point: its first position cannot have
any role except the smallest value in such a pattern. This is an
injection preserving the occurrence count, so $b_n$ is nondecreasing.
For $x>0$, define the inverse threshold
\[
 N(x)=\min\{n\ge0:b_n\ge x\},\qquad C=C_0R,\quad\kappa=\log9.
\]
The minimum is finite, by~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:main-equivalent}.

\begin{corollary}[Rounding-safe inversion]\label{upo:1243:cor:inverse}
Let $L=\log x$ and, for sufficiently large $x$, put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:g}
 g(x)=\frac{L+4\log L-4\log\kappa-\log C}{\kappa}.
\end{equation}
There exists $\varepsilon(x)\to0$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:inverse}
 \left\lceil g(x)-\varepsilon(x)\right\rceil
 \le N(x)\le
 \left\lceil g(x)+\varepsilon(x)\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
In particular $N(x)=g(x)+O(1)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Taking logarithms of the equivalent gives
$\log b_n=f(n)+o(1)$, where
$f(u)=\kappa u-4\log u+\log C$. For sufficiently large $u$,
$f'(u)=\kappa-4/u>\kappa/2$, so $f$ is increasing and its inverse is
locally Lipschitz with constant $2/\kappa$.
Direct substitution gives $f(g(x))=L+O(\log L/L)=L+o(1)$; consequently
the real solution $r(x)$ of $f(r)=L$ satisfies $r(x)=g(x)+o(1)$.

Choose a decreasing envelope $\eta(m)\to0$ such that
$|\log b_n-f(n)|\le\eta(m)$ for every $n\ge m$.
Let $m(x)=\lfloor r(x)/2\rfloor$; then $m(x)\to\infty$.
Since $f(m(x))+\eta(m(x))<L$ eventually, monotonicity implies
$N(x)>m(x)$. For all indices in this relevant range, the two inverse
solutions of $f(u)=L\pm\eta(m(x))$ bracket the threshold after taking
ceilings: below the lower solution one has $b_n<x$, and at or above
the upper solution one has $b_n\ge x$. Both real solutions differ from
$r(x)$ by $o(1)$, giving~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:inverse} after enlarging a positive
$\varepsilon(x)$ tending to zero.
\end{proof}

The equivalent supplies no explicit rate for $\varepsilon$. In
particular, removing the ceilings and replacing $O(1)$ by $o(1)$ would
lose the integer staircase and is not justified. A rate or higher
asymptotic correction requires an additional theorem.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Corollary~\ref{upo:1243:cor:inverse} and the inverse parts of Theorems \ref{upo:1243:thm:rate} and~\ref{upo:1243:thm:profile} are instances of parts (1)--(2) of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} (the front-matter section \hyperref[upo:sec:neighbours]{Relation to neighbouring reports}), with $g(x)$ as approximate inverse.
\end{writenote}
\section{A quantitative error bound}\label{upo:1243:sec:rate}
The leading-equivalent argument above uses fixed-boundary limits together
with global bounds.
A modest uniform rate can also be proved. We include the required
unitary-integral argument, adapted from the boundary method in the
companion Report 135~\cite{R135}, rather than use a fixed-parameter
expansion uniformly without justification.

\begin{lemma}[A global boundary approximation]\label{upo:1243:lem:global-rate}
For $p,q\ge0$ with $p+q\le m$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:global-F-rate}
 \left|\frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_m}-d_pd_q\right|
 \le Kd_pd_q\min\left\{1,\frac{(1+p+q)^2}{m+1}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Also
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:A-rate}
 A_m=C_0 9^m m^{-4}(1+O(m^{-1})),
 \qquad \frac{A_{h+a}}{A_h}=9^a(1+O((h+1)^{-1}))\quad(a=1,2).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $P(U)=\operatorname{tr}U$ on $U(3)$ and let $h_p=s_{(p)}$ be the
complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial of degree $p$ in the three
eigenvalues. Schur--Weyl decomposition and horizontal Pieri give
$P^{m-p}h_p=\sum_\lambda W_p(\lambda)s_\lambda$. Orthogonality of unitary
characters, with Haar probability measure, gives the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:unitary}
 F_m(p,q)=\int_{U(3)}P^{m-p}h_p\,
                    \overline{P^{m-q}h_q}\,dU.
\end{equation}
For $p=q=0$, this is $A_m=\int|P|^{2m}dU$; the trace-moment identity is
also recorded by Rains~\cite{Rains}.

The common eigenvalue phase cancels because each factor has total degree
$m$. After quotienting by this phase, $|P|=3$ has a unique maximum.
In a fixed small chart write the centered angles as
$\theta_1+\theta_2+\theta_3=0$ and $Q=\sum\theta_i^2$. Weyl's density,
up to a constant Jacobian, is
\[
 \prod_{a<b}|e^{i\theta_a}-e^{i\theta_b}|^2
   =\Delta(\theta)^2(1+O(Q)),\qquad
 \log(|P|^2/9)=-Q/3+O(Q^2).
\]
The remainders are uniform also on $\Delta=0$, by factoring out the
analytic sinc factors. Off this neighborhood, compactness gives
$|P|/3\le\rho<1$. In the local chart $|P|^{2m}/9^m\le e^{-cmQ}$.
Scaling $\theta=y/\sqrt m$, the two-dimensional volume contributes
$m^{-1}$ and the degree-six Vandermonde square contributes $m^{-3}$.
The errors $Q$ and $mQ^2$ integrate to relative $O(m^{-1})$: shrinking the
chart absorbs their exponential remainder into a weaker Gaussian.
Off-chart contributions are exponentially small. Thus
$A_m=D9^m m^{-4}(1+O(m^{-1}))$; comparison with~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:regev}
identifies $D=C_0$. The fixed-shift assertion follows, with finitely small
indices absorbed into the constant. The same Gaussian scaling gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:tilted-moment}
 \mathbb E_m[Qe^{c_1LQ};\mathrm{local}]=O(m^{-1})
 \quad(0\le L\le\epsilon m),
\end{equation}
for each fixed $c_1$, some $\epsilon>0$, and density
$|P|^{2m}dU/A_m$. This uses~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Aglobal} for normalization.

In the chart define
\[
 r_p(\theta)=\frac{h_p(e^{i\theta})}{B_p(P(\theta)/3)^p},
 \qquad h_p/P^p=d_pr_p.
\]
Fix a centered unit vector $v$, put $\theta=rv$, and let
$z(r)=P(rv)/3$, $g_p(r)=h_p(e^{irv})/B_p$. Averaging over weak
compositions of $p$ shows $|g_p^{(j)}(r)|\le p^j$ for $j=1,2$, and
$|g_p|\le1$. In a fixed small interval $|z(r)|\ge e^{-C r^2}$;
its first two derivatives are bounded, and the finite chain rule gives
\[
 |(z^{-p})^{(j)}(r)|\le K_j(p+1)^j e^{C'pr^2}
 \qquad(0\le j\le2).
\]
Extra inverse powers of $z$ are uniformly bounded. The real part of
$r_p(rv)\overline{r_q(rv)}$ is even in $r$, has value one at zero,
and has second derivative bounded by
$K(1+p+q)^2e^{C''(p+q)r^2}$. Taylor's theorem gives
\[
 |\Re(r_p\overline{r_q})-1|
 \le K(1+p+q)^2Qe^{C''(p+q)Q}.
\]
Conjugation invariance allows taking the real part under the integral.
If $1+p+q\le\sqrt{m+1}$, equation~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:tilted-moment} integrates
this bound to $O((1+p+q)^2/m)$ for sufficiently large $m$.

No division by $P$ is used off the chart. The original integrand
in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:unitary}, since $|h_p|\le B_p$, is bounded by
$9^m d_pd_q(|P|/3)^{2m-p-q}$. Its normalized off-chart contribution is
$O(d_pd_qm^4\rho^{2m-p-q})$, exponentially small in the range in question;
the constant approximant has the same negligible complement.
This proves~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:global-F-rate} in the small-boundary range.
Otherwise the minimum on its right is one, and the triangular
bound~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:triangle} supplies $F_m(p,q)/A_m\le Kd_pd_q$.
Enlarging $K$ handles the finitely many small $m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Uniform spine approximation]\label{upo:1243:lem:J-rate}
For every $s,k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:J-rate}
 \left|\frac{J_k(s)}{A_{s+k}}-\gamma_k\right|
 \le K\gamma_k\min\left\{1,\frac{(k+1)^2}{s+k+1}\right\}.
\end{equation}
For each fixed nonnegative integer $r$, the endpoint moments satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Mr}
 M_r(t):=9^{-t}\sum_{k\ge0}(k+1)^{2r}\gamma_kJ_k(t)
       \le K_r(t+1)^{r-3/2}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Put $h=s+k$. Apply~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:global-F-rate} to both terms of $H_h(k,j)$,
using the fixed-shift ratio~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:A-rate}. Since $d_{k+1}\asymp d_k$
uniformly, the result is
\[
 \left|\frac{H_h(k,j)}{A_h}-C(k,j)\right|
 \le\frac{Kd_kd_j(k+j+1)^2}{h+1}\qquad(k+j\le h).
\]
Summing $0\le j\le s$ and using geometric moments gives
$Kd_k(k+1)^2/(h+1)$. The missing tail of $\gamma_k$ is at most
$Kd_k(s+1)^2 3^{-s}$, which is bounded by the same expression, since
$(s+k+1)/(k+1)^2\le s+1$ and $(s+1)^3 3^{-s}$ is bounded.
Now $\gamma_k\asymp d_k$, so this gives the second bound in the minimum.
The first follows from~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jdom} and positivity.

For the moment claim, multiply~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:endpointterm} by $(k+1)^{2r}$.
The part $j\ge k/2$ is still $O_r((t+1)^{-2})$, because its
geometric-polynomial double sum converges. The part $j<k/2$ is at most
\[
 \frac{K_r}{(t+2)^2}\sum_{k\ge0}
 \frac{(k+1)^{4+2r}}{(t+k+3)^2}
       e^{-ck^2/(t+k+3)}.
\]
For $k\le t+1$, the denominators supply $O((t+1)^{-4})$ and the
Gaussian moment is $O_r((t+1)^{(5+2r)/2})$. For $k>t+1$, exponential
decay in $k$ gives $O_r((t+1)^{-2})$. Both are bounded as
in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Mr}. In particular $M_0(t)=e_t=O((t+1)^{-3/2})$ and
$M_1(t)=O((t+1)^{-1/2})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Quantitative leading estimate]\label{upo:1243:thm:rate}
As $n\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:count-rate}
 \frac{b_n}{A_n}=R+O(n^{-1/2}),\qquad
 b_n=C_0R\,9^n n^{-4}(1+O(n^{-1/2})).
\end{equation}
With $g$ as in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:g}, there is $K<\infty$ such that, for all
sufficiently large $x$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:inverse-rate}
 \left\lceil g(x)-K(\log x)^{-1/2}\right\rceil
 \le N(x)\le
 \left\lceil g(x)+K(\log x)^{-1/2}\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
The constants are not numerically certified. No nonzero coefficient at
the relative $n^{-1/2}$ scale is asserted.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $n\ge32$ put
\[
 D_n=\{(t,k)\in\N^2:2t+k\le n-4\},\quad
 s=n-4-t-k,\quad h=s+k=n-4-t,\quad
 w(t,k)=9^{-t}\gamma_kJ_k(t).
\]
On $D_n$, $h\ge(n-4)/2$. From~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:A-rate},
\[
 \frac{A_h}{A_n}=9^{-t-4}\left(1+O\left(\frac{t+1}{n}\right)\right).
\]
Indeed $(n/h)^4$ stays bounded and differs from one by $O((t+1)/n)$;
the relative avoidance errors contribute $O(1/n)$.
Combining this with~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:J-rate} gives
\[
 \left|\frac{J_k(s)J_k(t)}{A_n}-9^{-4}w(t,k)\right|
 \le\frac K n w(t,k)\bigl(t+1+(k+1)^2\bigr).
\]
Summing over $D_n$, equation~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Mr} bounds the total by
\[
 \frac K n\sum_{0\le t\le n/2}
       \bigl((t+1)e_t+M_1(t)\bigr)=O(n^{-1/2}).
\]
Both the complement of $D_n$ and its diagonal $2t+k=n-4$ are contained
in
$\{t>n/8\}\cup\{t\le n/8,\ k>n/2\}$ for $n\ge32$.
The $w$ mass of this union is at most
\[
 \sum_{t>n/8}e_t+\frac4{n^2}\sum_{t\le n/8}M_1(t)
 =O(n^{-1/2})+O(n^{-3/2}).
\]
The normalized convolution diagonal is bounded by $K$ times its $w$
mass by~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:convdom}. Thus replacing the oriented factor
$2-\mathbf1_{s=t}$ by two and extending to all $t,k$ changes the answer
by $O(n^{-1/2})$. The complete endpoint sum is $R$, proving the first
assertion. Multiplication by~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:A-rate} gives the second.

Finally, taking logarithms gives
$\log b_n=\kappa n-4\log n+\log(C_0R)+O(n^{-1/2})$.
Its smooth main term has derivative tending to $\kappa>0$, so its real
inverse has an uncertainty $O((\log x)^{-1/2})$. The monotonicity and
ceiling argument of Corollary~\ref{upo:1243:cor:inverse} applies with this explicit
asymptotic error envelope. Substitution in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:g} shows that the
smooth inverse differs from $g(x)$ by
$O(\log\log x/\log x)=o((\log x)^{-1/2})$, proving~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:inverse-rate}.
\end{proof}

The bound strengthens the existence theorem and its rounding width.
It is not an expansion with a known next term: cancellation could make
the true error smaller. It supplies no certified decimal digits of $R$.

\section{The diffusive spine profile and cancellation}\label{upo:1243:sec:profile}
The $O(n^{-1/2})$ estimate supplies uniform control, but its leading
half-power coefficient actually vanishes. We now prove this from a
profile at spine lengths of order $\sqrt n$. Write
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Dconstant}
 a=\frac{135}{8},\qquad
 D=C_0a^2\int_0^\infty u^4e^{-u^2/2}\,du
   =\frac{4428675}{2048}\sqrt{\frac6\pi}.
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Profile and improved remainder]\label{upo:1243:thm:profile}
If $h=s+k\to\infty$ and $k/\sqrt h\to u\in[0,\infty)$, then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Jprofile}
 \frac{J_k(s)}{A_h\gamma_k}\longrightarrow e^{-u^2/2}.
\end{equation}
The endpoint density and its amplitude tail satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:etail-equivalent}
 e_t\sim Dt^{-3/2},\qquad
 \frac2{9^4}\sum_{t>T}e_t
 \sim\frac{675}{512}\sqrt{\frac6\pi}\,T^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:little-rate}
 \frac{b_n}{A_n}=R+o(n^{-1/2}),\qquad
 b_n=C_0R\,9^n n^{-4}(1+o(n^{-1/2})).
\end{equation}
Consequently the width in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:inverse-rate} can be replaced by some
$\varepsilon(x)=o((\log x)^{-1/2})$ inside both ceilings.
\end{theorem}

The last assertion identifies a zero coefficient at the relative
$n^{-1/2}$ scale. It neither proves an $O(n^{-1})$ error nor identifies
the first nonzero correction. The tail equivalent is asymptotic and has
no certified finite-$T$ error bound. Each $e_t$ includes the full sum
over $k$; this $t$-only tail is distinct from the omitted triangular
region $k+t>T$ in $L_T$.

\subsection{A character limit at a growing boundary}
We use the unitary formula~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:unitary} and the local coordinates
in Lemma~\ref{upo:1243:lem:global-rate}. Under $y=\sqrt m\,\theta$, the normalized
trace-weighted probability measure converges on the centered plane
$y_1+y_2+y_3=0$ to density
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:saddlelaw}
 Z^{-1}e^{-\sum_i y_i^2/3}\Delta(y)^2\,dy.
\end{equation}
The same Gaussian domination used in that lemma justifies convergence
against bounded functions with a small Gaussian tilt.
Suppose $p/\sqrt m\to u\ge0$ and $q$ is fixed. Locally the normalized
integrand is $r_p(\theta)\overline{r_q(\theta)}$.
On compact $y$ sets, $(P(y/\sqrt m)/3)^{-p}\to1$, since
$\log(P/3)=O(|y|^2/m)$; also $r_q\to1$.
The factor $h_p(e^{iy/\sqrt m})/B_p$ is the characteristic function of a
uniform weak composition $\delta_1+\delta_2+\delta_3=p$, evaluated at
$y/\sqrt m$. If $u>0$, triangular-grid Riemann sums show that
$\delta/p$ tends to the uniform probability law on the simplex,
$X\sim\operatorname{Dirichlet}(1,1,1)$. Hence
\[
 \frac{h_p(e^{iy/\sqrt m})}{B_p}
 \longrightarrow\mathbb E_X e^{iu X\cdot y}.
\]
For $u=0$, the same limit follows from
$|\delta\cdot y|/\sqrt m\le p|y|/\sqrt m\to0$, including bounded $p$.

This passage is global. On the small chart,
$|h_p|/B_p\le1$ and $|(P/3)^{-p-q}|\le e^{C(p+q)Q}$;
as $p+q=O(\sqrt m)$, a fixed part of the saddle Gaussian absorbs this
tilt. Off the chart the original polynomial integrand has normalized
bound $Km^4\rho^{2m-p-q}$ and is exponentially negligible.
Thus dominated convergence gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:charactertransform}
 \frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_md_pd_q}
 \longrightarrow\mathbb E_Y\mathbb E_X e^{iu X\cdot Y},
\end{equation}
where $Y$ has density~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:saddlelaw}.

To evaluate the transform, let $G$ be a traceless Hermitian $3$ by $3$
matrix with density proportional to $e^{-\operatorname{tr}(G^2)/3}$
on its eight-dimensional real vector space, equipped with inner product
$\operatorname{tr}(AB)$. The Weyl change of variables gives precisely
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:saddlelaw} as its eigenvalue density. For an independent
Haar-uniform complex unit vector $v$, the squared coordinates in an
orthonormal basis have the Dirichlet law above. This follows by
normalizing three independent complex Gaussian coordinates: their
squared moduli are independent exponentials, and division by their sum
gives the uniform simplex law. Conditional on the eigenvalues $Y$,
$v^*Gv$ therefore has the law of $X\cdot Y$.

Unitary invariance makes its averaged law the law of $G_{11}$. The
orthonormal real coordinates of $G$ are centered Gaussians of variance
$3/2$, so
\[
 \operatorname{Var}(\operatorname{tr}(AG))
 =\frac32\operatorname{tr}(A_0^2),\qquad
 A_0=A-\frac{\operatorname{tr}A}{3}I.
\]
For $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,0,0)$, the final trace is $2/3$.
Thus $G_{11}$ is a real centered Gaussian of variance one, and
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:Fprofile}
 \frac{F_m(p,q)}{A_md_pd_q}\longrightarrow e^{-u^2/2}
 \qquad(p/\sqrt m\to u,\ q\text{ fixed}).
\end{equation}
This convergence is uniform when $p/\sqrt m$ ranges over a fixed compact
interval: a contrary sequence would have a subsequence with convergent
ratios, contradicting the sequential limit and continuity of the target.

Apply~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Fprofile} to the two terms defining $H_h(k,j)$ for
fixed $j$. The fixed-shift avoidance ratios give
\[
 \frac{H_h(k,j)}{A_h}
   =C(k,j)e^{-u^2/2}+o(\gamma_k).
\]
Here $d_{k+1},d_k,\gamma_k$ are uniformly comparable. The triangular
bound gives the uniform summable majorants
\[
 \frac{H_h(k,j)}{A_h\gamma_k}\le K(j+1)^2 3^{-j},\qquad
 \frac{|C(k,j)|}{\gamma_k}\le K(j+1)^2 3^{-j}.
\]
Since $k=O(\sqrt h)$, $s=h-k\to\infty$. Truncate at a fixed $j$, pass
to the limit, then remove the truncation by these bounds. This proves
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jprofile}, again compact-uniformly in $k/\sqrt h$.

\subsection{The endpoint density}
Recall $w(t,k)=9^{-t}\gamma_kJ_k(t)$. If $k/\sqrt t\to u>0$, the
profile, Regev's equivalent, and $\gamma_k\sim ak^2 3^{-k}$ give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:wprofile}
 t^2w(t,k)\longrightarrow C_0a^2u^4e^{-u^2/2}.
\end{equation}
For $u=0$, the global bound
$w(t,k)\le K(k+1)^4/(t+k+1)^4$ gives the same zero limit.
The convergence is compact-uniform by sequential compactness.

Splitting the $j$ sum of~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:endpointterm} at $j=k/2$ yields
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:wtail}
 w(t,k)\le
 \frac{K(k+1)^4}{(t+2)^2(t+k+3)^2}
          e^{-ck^2/(t+k+3)}
 +\frac{K(k+1)^6 3^{-k/2}}{(t+2)^2}.
\end{equation}
Indeed the first region uses the geometrically summable $j$ factor;
in the other, the tail of $(j+1)^2 3^{-j}$ supplies the displayed
geometric factor in $k$. The second term sums to $O(t^{-2})$.
For $k\le t+1$, the first is at most
\[
 Kt^{-2}\left(\frac{k+1}{\sqrt t}\right)^4e^{-c'k^2/t},
\]
with bounded small $t$ treated separately. For $k>t+1$, its
$t^{3/2}$-scaled total tends to zero exponentially.
These estimates justify the full Riemann sum, giving
$t^{3/2}e_t\to C_0a^2\int_0^\infty u^4e^{-u^2/2}du=D$.
The integral is $3\sqrt{\pi/2}$. Summing the positive power tail gives
$\sum_{t>T}e_t\sim2DT^{-1/2}$ and the coefficient in
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:etail-equivalent}.

\subsection{Matching the interior to the endpoints}
Fix $\delta\in(0,1/4)$ and orient the convolution by $t\le s$ as before.
For its interior part take $t\ge\delta n$.
If $t/n\to x\in[\delta,1/2]$ and $k/\sqrt n\to u$, both half indices
$h_1=s+k=n-t-4$ and $h_2=t+k$ tend to infinity linearly, and
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:Jprofile} gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:interiorprofile}
 n^2\frac{J_k(s)J_k(t)}{A_n}
 \longrightarrow C_0a^2 9^{-4}u^4[x(1-x)]^{-4}
 e^{-u^2/(2x(1-x))}.
\end{equation}
For $u=0$, the global bound gives
$n^2J_k(s)J_k(t)/A_n\le K_\delta(k+1)^4/n^2\to0$,
so this boundary case has the same limit. The convergence is uniform on
compact $x,u$ ranges. The exponential size
factor leaves $9^{-4}9^k\gamma_k^2$, explaining both the constant and
the scale in this display.

By~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:convdom}, the summand is bounded by $Kw(t,k)$.
For $\delta n\le t\le n/2$, the first term of~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:wtail} with
$k\le t+1$ is at most
$K_\delta n^{-2}(1+k/\sqrt n)^4e^{-c_\delta k^2/n}$.
The sum over $k>M\sqrt n$ and this $t$ range, multiplied by $\sqrt n$,
tends uniformly to zero as $M\to\infty$. The geometric term of
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:wtail} has total $O_\delta(n^{-1})$, and $k>t+1$ is
exponentially negligible. Thus the two-dimensional Riemann sum is
