justified with its entire $k$ range. Its diagonal $s=t$ has only
$O(M\sqrt n)$ terms for $k\le M\sqrt n$, each $O_{\delta,M}(n^{-2})$;
the same tail argument removes the rest. The upper bound
$t\le(n-4-k)/2$ shifts the scaled endpoint only by $o(1)$ for bounded
$k/\sqrt n$.

Let $B_{\mathrm{mid}}(n,\delta)$ denote the normalized oriented sum over
$t\ge\delta n$, with multiplicity $2-\mathbf1_{s=t}$.
Integration in $u$ in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:interiorprofile} gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:midlimit}
 \sqrt n\,B_{\mathrm{mid}}(n,\delta)
 \longrightarrow 2D9^{-4}
       \int_\delta^{1/2}[x(1-x)]^{-3/2}\,dx.
\end{equation}
For the endpoint part, the quantitative proof of Theorem~\ref{upo:1243:thm:rate},
now summed only to $t<\delta n$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:endmatch}
 \left|B_{\mathrm{end}}(n,\delta)
       -2\cdot9^{-4}\sum_{t<\delta n}e_t\right|
 \le K\frac{\sqrt\delta}{\sqrt n}+O(n^{-1}),
\end{equation}
with $K$ independent of $\delta\in(0,1/4)$.
To see every omitted part explicitly, the local approximation error is
at most
$(K/n)\sum_{t<\delta n}((t+1)e_t+M_1(t))$,
which has the displayed bound. Missing $k$ values satisfy $k\ge n/3$
for large $n$ and have $w$ mass at most
$Kn^{-2}\sum_{t<\delta n}M_1(t)
=O(\sqrt\delta\,n^{-3/2})+O(n^{-2})$.
The endpoint diagonal satisfies the same estimate.

The endpoint equivalent gives
$\sqrt n\sum_{t\ge\delta n}e_t\to2D\delta^{-1/2}$.
Subtract $R=2\cdot9^{-4}\sum_t e_t$ and combine this with
\eqref{upo:1243:eq:midlimit}--\eqref{upo:1243:eq:endmatch}. The possible scaled discrepancy
is bounded by the absolute value of
\[
 2D9^{-4}\left(
 \int_\delta^{1/2}[x(1-x)]^{-3/2}\,dx-2\delta^{-1/2}
 \right)
\]
plus $O(\sqrt\delta)$.
An antiderivative is $2(2x-1)/\sqrt{x(1-x)}$, so the parenthesis equals
\[
 \frac{2(1-2\delta)}{\sqrt{\delta(1-\delta)}}
       -2\delta^{-1/2}=O(\sqrt\delta).
\]
Letting $\delta\downarrow0$ proves
$\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sqrt n\,|b_n/A_n-R|=0$.
The avoidance rate~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:A-rate} gives the coefficient statement
in~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:little-rate}. Finally the logarithmic error is
$o(n^{-1/2})$, and the inverse argument gives an inside-ceiling width
$o((\log x)^{-1/2})$; the smooth-center discrepancy
$O(\log\log x/\log x)$ is smaller still.
This proves Theorem~\ref{upo:1243:thm:profile} without assigning a value to a
divergent beta integral or assuming an unproved correction expansion.

\section{Source status and reproducibility}\label{upo:1243:sec:scope}
Nakamura~\cite[Section 2.2, Theorem 2]{Nakamura} gives exact catalytic
functional equations and truncation algorithms for fixed $1243$
occurrence counts. The first 15 values agree with the finite formula
here. Conway--Guttmann~\cite[Section 5.2]{CG} conjecture the leading scale
and estimate the amplitude numerically. Their differential approximants
and extended series are numerical methods, not exact annihilating
equations. No finite-dimensional scalar representation, present spine
formula, or leading-amplitude proof was located in those reviewed
sources. This is a scoped source assessment, not an exhaustive
historical-priority claim.

A nearby target requires particular care. A224292 additionally avoids
$1234$ while having one $1243$. Hou--Mansour~\cite[Example 6 and Theorem 7]{HM}
already proved an algebraic generating function and an orientation swap
for that class in 2008. It is not the unrestricted sequence treated
here, and its generating function is not claimed as a new result.
Likewise the general Noonan--Zeilberger P-recursiveness conjecture cannot
be substituted for the concrete binomial proof above.

The source review covered the exact OEIS identifier, exactly-one-$1243$
queries, D-finiteness and amplitude queries, the cited primary papers,
and the nearby joint-pattern result. It did not include author
correspondence, a complete MathSciNet review, or all unpublished theses.
An absent OEIS formula alone does not establish novelty.

The reader bundle contains this PDF and its editable LaTeX source,
a standard-library Python certificate, typed exact fixtures, and a
manifest. The certificate independently enumerates small permutation
objects and compares their marked images, reconstructed halves, and
product counts; it evaluates the tableau sums, the support-reduced
23-index algebra, and the exact positive partial~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:lower}.
It also checks finite strip interlacing and Gaussian-support diagnostics.
The bundled README states the exact finite ranges and replay commands.
These checks detect implementation and indexing errors. They do not
prove an infinite limit, universal Gaussian constants, or historical
priority; those roles belong to the proofs and primary-source assessment.
Normal and optimized Python replay must give the same exact results.
No third-party PDF or private research note is required to reproduce the
bundle's checks.

\subsection*{Further questions}
Four concrete tasks remain open. First, resolve whether $R=149/160$, or
obtain a rigorous numerical enclosure that distinguishes it. Second,
make the finite-$T$ endpoint tails effective with explicit constants and
onset. Third, determine the first nonzero correction after the proved
square-root cancellation; neither an $O(n^{-1})$ rate nor its coefficient
has been established here. Fourth, compute an annihilating equation of
usable order from the rational structure. The present formulas provide
inputs to these questions, not their solutions.
\subsection{Further questions and research}\label{upo:1243:sub:further}
\begin{writenote}[Status, 5 October 2026]
The four questions above are open: Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, items 2, 4, 7 and~5. Two further points of this Part are stated without proof or were not checked. The constant term of Section~\ref{upo:1243:app:CT} is not asserted to be the rational diagonal whose existence Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:BLS} proves; whether it is one is open. The attribution of the algebraic generating function of A224292 to Hou and Mansour \cite[Example 6 and Theorem 7]{HM} is the manuscript's own and was not checked in the write; the OEIS entry for A224292 cites Nakamura only (intake). Nothing in this Part was found to be wrong.
\end{writenote}

\section{An explicit rational constant term}\label{upo:1243:app:CT}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Delivered as Report~136's Appendix~A; printed here as the last section of Part~IV.
\end{writenote}
The BLS argument proves existence of an origin-regular rational diagonal.
For a concrete rational integrand, we give a separate 40-variable constant
term with explicit contour semantics. This Laurent-period integrand is
not asserted to be that particular rational diagonal.

For a triple $X$ put
\[
 \Sigma(X)=X_1+X_2+X_3,\quad
 \Delta(X)=(X_1-X_2)(X_1-X_3)(X_2-X_3),\quad
 V(X)=\frac{\Delta(X)}{X_1^2X_2},\quad c(v)=\frac{1+v}{v}.
\]
One block uses the 20 variables
\[
 A,B,C;\ b;\ p_1,\ldots,p_5;\ q_1,\ldots,q_5; X_1,X_2,X_3;\ Y_1,Y_2,Y_3.
\]
Take also an independent primed copy of these variables. In this appendix
$A,B,C$ are integration variables, unrelated to the earlier sequence or
amplitude notation. Set $H=c(b)$, $T_r=c(p_r)$ and $U_r=c(q_r)$. Define
11 rational weights by
\begin{align*}
 R_s&=\frac{zH}{ABC},& R_j&=\frac C H,\\
 R_{\lambda_1}&=AT_1U_1,&
 R_{\lambda_2}&=\frac{AT_3U_3}{T_2U_2},&
 R_{\lambda_3}&=\frac{AT_5U_5}{T_4U_4},\\
 R_{\mu_1}&=\frac{B\Sigma(X)T_2}{X_1T_1},&
 R_{\mu_2}&=\frac{B\Sigma(X)T_4}{X_2T_3},&
 R_{\mu_3}&=\frac{B\Sigma(X)}{X_3T_5},\\
 R_{\nu_1}&=\frac{C\Sigma(Y)U_2}{Y_1U_1},&
 R_{\nu_2}&=\frac{C\Sigma(Y)U_4}{Y_2U_3},&
 R_{\nu_3}&=\frac{C\Sigma(Y)}{Y_3U_5}.
\end{align*}
Let $\mathcal R$ be this list and put
\[
 K(z)=\frac{(1-A)V(X)V(Y)}{A^2BC}
       \prod_{R\in\mathcal R}\frac1{1-R}.
\]
Define $K'(z)$ with the primed variables, and put $R_k=z/(ACA'C')$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:1243:eq:CT}
 B(z)=z^4\operatorname{CT}
       \frac{K(z)K'(z)}{1-R_k}.
\end{equation}
The constant term is over all 40 internal variables; $z$ is not
integrated. There are 23 geometric factors, eleven per block and one
for the shared spine.

\subsection*{Extraction}
Expand all geometric factors in nonnegative powers. The first block
indices are $s,j$ and its nine tableau coordinates; the shared factor
has index $k$. Expand $1-A$ using $\alpha\in\{0,1\}$ with sign
$(-1)^\alpha$. The powers of the equality variables are
\[
 A:\ |\lambda|-s-k-2+\alpha,\qquad
 B:\ |\mu|-s-1,\qquad
 C:\ |\nu|-s-k+j-1.
\]
Their constant terms enforce the three sizes in
$F_{s+k+2}(k+1,j+1)-F_{s+k+1}(k,j)$.
For every integer $d$, on any circle $|v|<1$,
\[
 \operatorname{CT}_v c(v)^d=[v^d](1+v)^d=\mathbf1_{d\ge0}.
\]
Thus $H^{s-j}$ imposes $s\ge j$. The five $T$ powers have exponents
\[
 \lambda_1-\mu_1,\quad\mu_1-\lambda_2,\quad
 \lambda_2-\mu_2,\quad\mu_2-\lambda_3,\quad\lambda_3-\mu_3,
\]
and the $U$ powers give the corresponding exponents with $\nu$.
They impose both interlacings; $\mu_3,\nu_3\ge0$ already hold for
geometric-series indices. Finally,
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{CT}_X V(X)\prod_{r=1}^3
       \left(\frac{\Sigma(X)}{X_r}\right)^{\mu_r}
 &= [X_1^{\mu_1+2}X_2^{\mu_2+1}X_3^{\mu_3}]
      \Delta(X)\Sigma(X)^{|\mu|}\\
 &=f^\mu
\end{align*}
on partition inputs, by the six-term determinant calculation in
Section~\ref{upo:1243:sec:BLS}. The strip indicators ensure such inputs.
The $Y$ extraction gives $f^\nu$. The primed block provides the second
$H$ factor with indices $t,l$ and the same $k$. The power of $z$ is
$4+s+t+k$, proving~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:CT} from~\eqref{upo:1243:eq:spine}.

\subsection*{Convergent contours}
Give all dimension variables $X_r,Y_r,X'_r,Y'_r$ radius one; every
boundary or strip variable radius $1/2$; and each equality variable
$A,B,C,A',B',C'$ radius $\epsilon=1/100$. Take $|z|<\epsilon^4/6$.
On these circles $1\le|c(v)|\le3$ and $|\Sigma(X)/X_r|\le3$. Hence
\[
 |R_s|\le3|z|\epsilon^{-3}<\epsilon/2,\quad
 |R_j|\le\epsilon,\quad
 |R_{\lambda_r}|,|R_{\mu_r}|,|R_{\nu_r}|\le9\epsilon<1,
 \quad |R_k|<1/6.
\]
The primed estimates agree. All 23 geometric series converge absolutely
and uniformly on this product torus. The remaining numerator is bounded
there. The constant term means the normalized integral with measure
$\prod_v dv/(2\pi iv)$; sum--integral interchange is justified.
The integral is analytic near $z=0$. At any fixed power $z^n$, the size
conditions and $s+t+k=n-4$ leave finitely many surviving indices, so
analytic and formal interpretations agree. No arbitrary expansion at the
simultaneous origin of the 40 variables is intended. This gives an
explicit elimination input, without a recurrence-order or complexity
claim and without evaluating $R$.

\clearpage
\part{Exact formulas and leading asymptotics for unique 12345}\label{upo:ex5:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~138, archive \file{A224248_Exact_Formulas_Leading_Asymptotics_and_Inversion_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{Report138.tex} (17 pages), dated 2~October 2026; title block ``Exact formulas and leading asymptotics for unique 12345'', author line ``Report 138''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} notes; a section number is Report~138's plus~$43$, and equations are renumbered within sections. Its table of contents is replaced by the report's. Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sub:further} is added in the write.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.44\textwidth} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.50\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Reading conventions of Part~V & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$U_n$: exactly one $12345$ (A224248); $A_n=|\Av_n(12345)|$ (A047889) & Parts I--IV: $A_n=|\Av_n(1234)|$\\
$R_5$ the amplitude; $C_5=C_AR_5$; $C_A=1536/\pi^{3/2}$ & Parts I--III: $R$; Part~IV: $R_{1243}$\\
$v=(i_2,i_3;j_2,j_3)$; $P,R,Q,T$ \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Pdef} & Part~I: $P_k,Q_k$; $R$ the amplitude elsewhere\\
$H_s(v)$ four-index half counts & Parts I--IV: $H_s(i,j)$\\
$K(v,w)$, $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_K$; $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_4$ & Parts II--III: $\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal K}$ (two binomials)\\
$h(v)$; $w(v)$ a weight, $w$ a vector & Part~VI: $B(v)$, $\omega(v)$\\
$p=15/2$ from Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:bounds}; $p=i_2+i_3+2$ in Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:boundary} & Parts I--IV: a boundary parameter\\
alphabets $A,B,C,D$ (Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:sym}); $E=2M-P-Q$ & \\
$n_*(x)$, $F(x)$, $L=\log x$ & other inverses\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{partabstract}
Let $U_n$ count permutations with exactly one classical increasing
subsequence of length five, OEIS A224248, and let $A_n$ count permutations
avoiding that pattern. We give an exact decomposition into two halves
with a four-binomial gluing kernel. Explicit mutually inverse maps turn
one half into a permutation with disjoint nested boundary restrictions.
A six-term inverse-Cauchy formula counts this class; its proof includes
the tableau-saturation argument needed to impose the empty corner.
The resulting fixed-dimensional binomial sum proves that the ordinary
generating function is a rational diagonal and that $(U_n)$ is P-recursive.
A uniform anisotropic boundary estimate and an endpoint convolution
argument prove $U_n/A_n\to R_5$, where $R_5$ has an absolutely convergent
series of nonnegative terms and is strictly positive. Consequently
$U_n\sim (1536R_5/\pi^{3/2})16^n n^{-15/2}$. We also justify leading
inverse thresholds with an $o(1)$ real error before integer rounding.
No numerical value of $R_5$, correction expansion, minimal recurrence,
or worldwide novelty claim is made.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Part~VI proves the correction expansion that this Part does not assert (Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:main}); the ``frozen leading-only baseline'' of Part~VI is this Part.
\end{writenote}


\section{The sequence and the conclusions}\label{upo:ex5:sec:intro}
An occurrence of the \emph{classical} pattern $12345$ is a choice of
five positions whose values increase; the positions need not be adjacent.
Let $U_n$ be the number of permutations of $[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ with
exactly one occurrence, and set $U_0=\cdots=U_4=0$. This is
OEIS A224248~\cite{OEISU}. Its first nonzero terms are
\[
1,\ 20,\ 270,\ 3142,\ 34291,\ 364462,\ 3844051,\ 40632886
\qquad(n=5,\ldots,12).
\]
Let $A_n$ count the avoiders, with $A_0=1$; these are OEIS
A047889~\cite{OEISA}. Reversing positions interchanges increasing and
decreasing patterns and does not change either count.

Nakamura and Zeilberger~\cite{NZ} gave functional-equation algorithms
and many exact terms. Polynomial-time enumeration for a fixed pattern
and occurrence count does not by itself imply a polynomial-coefficient
recurrence. B\'ona and Burstein~\cite{BB}, equation (6.1), already prove
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:known-theta}
 U_n=\Theta(16^n n^{-15/2}).
\end{equation}
Waite~\cite{Waite}, Corollary 4.4, extends the corresponding order
comparison to every fixed occurrence count. These cited order bounds
are distinguished here from a leading equivalent: a two-sided
$\Theta$ bound does not establish convergence of $U_n/A_n$.
The known nonrationality result for this generating function is also
consistent with rational diagonality. The cited even-length
nonalgebraicity theorem does not decide algebraicity for length five.
\begin{writenote}[Citations pinned, 5 October 2026]
Report~138 gives no citation for the two preceding sentences. They are \cite[Theorem 5.3]{BB}: for every $k\ge3$ the generating function of the permutations with exactly one copy of $k\cdots21$ is not rational, which by reversal covers one $12345$; and \cite[Theorem 6.2]{BB}, which proves nonalgebraicity for even $k>2$ only. \cite[Corollary 4.4]{Waite} extends both to every fixed number of copies (not rational for odd $k$, not algebraic for even $k$). All three were checked against the arXiv texts (2101.00332v3 and 2506.05712v2). The obstruction of \cite[Lemma 6.3]{BB} needs an integer exponent $m>1$, so it does not apply to the exponent $15/2$ of \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:known-theta}; algebraicity for length five is open (question~4 of Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:questions}).
\end{writenote}

Here is an overview of the quantities that occur in our conclusions.
Their combinatorial and algebraic definitions are proved below.
A boundary vector is $v=(i_2,i_3;j_2,j_3)\in\NN^4$, and
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Pdef}
 P=i_2+i_3+1,\quad R=i_3+1,\quad
 Q=j_2+j_3+1,\quad T=j_2+1.
\end{equation}
The integer $H_s(v)$ counts northern halves with $s$ extra points.
For $w=(k_2,k_3;l_2,l_3)$ put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:kernel}
 K(v,w)=\binom{i_2+l_2}{i_2}\binom{i_3+l_3}{i_3}
         \binom{j_2+k_2}{j_2}\binom{j_3+k_3}{j_3},
 \qquad \pair XY=\sum_{v,w\in\NN^4}X(v)Y(w)K(v,w).
\end{equation}
In particular $K(v,w)=K(w,v)$. The convention is that a southern half
is obtained by \emph{transposing} a northern half. It involves no
reverse-complement or reversal of boundary indices.

For a partition $\alpha$, $s_\alpha$ is its Schur function, and
$f^{\alpha/\rho}$ counts standard tableaux of skew shape $\alpha/\rho$.
It is zero unless $\rho\subseteq\alpha$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Gdef}
 G_{P,R}=\sum_{\substack{\alpha\vdash P\\\ell(\alpha)\le2}}
          f^{\alpha/(R)}s_\alpha,
 \qquad
 \Tset=\{\varnothing,(1),(2),(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}.
\end{equation}
The adjoint $s_\tau^\perp$ and truncated inner product are defined in
Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:sym}. Evaluation at $1^4=(1,1,1,1)$ is ordinary
four-variable character dimension. Put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:hdef}
 h(v)=256\,4^{-P-Q}
 \sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^{|\tau|}
 (s_\tau^\perp G_{P,R})(1^4)
 (s_{\tau'}^\perp G_{Q,T})(1^4).
\end{equation}
Although this finite formula has signs, the function $h$ is nonnegative.

\Needspace{20\baselineskip}
\begin{theorem}\label{upo:ex5:thm:overview}
The sequence $(U_n)$ is a multiple binomial sum over $\mathbb Q$.
Its ordinary generating function is the diagonal of a rational power
series, and $(U_n)$ is P-recursive. Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Rdef}
 R_5=2\,16^{-5}\sum_{t\ge0}16^{-t}\pair h{H_t}
 \quad\hbox{converges absolutely,}\qquad
 R_5\ge\frac{15}{32768}>0,
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:main}
 \frac{U_n}{A_n}\longrightarrow R_5,
 \qquad
 U_n\sim C_5\,16^n n^{-15/2},\qquad
 C_5=\frac{1536R_5}{\pi^{3/2}}.
\end{equation}
For $x>0$ define the first integer threshold
$n_*(x)=\min\{n\ge0:U_n\ge x\}$. With $L=\log x$ and
$\lambda=\log16$, let
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Fdef}
 F(x)=\frac{L+\tfrac{15}{2}\log L
                  -\tfrac{15}{2}\log\lambda-\log C_5}{\lambda}.
\end{equation}
There is a real function $\varepsilon(x)\to0$ such that, for all
sufficiently large $x$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:inverse-main}
 n_*(x)=\left\lceil F(x)+\varepsilon(x)\right\rceil.
\end{equation}
This is an error statement \emph{inside} the ceiling; replacing it by
$\lceil F(x)\rceil$ is not justified.
\end{theorem}

All combinatorial reductions and the estimates needed for the ratio
limit are included in this article. Standard RSK, symmetric-function
identities, Regev's avoidance asymptotic, and the binomial-sum theorem
are the explicitly cited general inputs. The contribution is stated
relative to the cited bounds and the derivation given here; an
exhaustive literature-priority claim is not intended.

\section{Cell geometry and exact gluing}\label{upo:ex5:sec:glue}
Represent a permutation by its points, ordered left to right by
position and bottom to top by value. In a permutation counted by $U_n$,
mark its unique increasing chain $c_1,\ldots,c_5$ in position order.
For any extra point $x$, let $a$ be the number of marked positions
before $x$, and $b$ the number of marked values below $x$. Thus
$(a,b)\in\{0,\ldots,5\}^2$.

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:ex5:lem:cells}
Every extra point satisfies $|a-b|\ge2$. The ten cells $b\ge a+2$
are called northern, and the ten cells $a\ge b+2$ southern.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Together with $x$, the first $\min(a,b)$ marked points and the last
$5-\max(a,b)$ marked points form an increasing chain of length
$6-|a-b|$. If $|a-b|\le1$, this chain contains a five-point
subchain involving $x$, contrary to uniqueness.
\end{proof}

A \emph{northern half} is the standardized restriction to the marked
points and all northern extras. Standardization replaces each value
by its rank, preserving both orders. The half has exactly the marked
five-chain. Define $H_s(i_2,i_3;j_2,j_3)$ by counting such halves with
$s$ extras, $i_r$ extras in position gap $r$, and $j_r$ extras in value
gap $r$, for $r=2,3$. These four sets are pairwise disjoint: a northern
point in position gap 2 or 3 has value gap at least 4, while a northern
point in value gap 2 or 3 has position gap at most 1. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:support}
 H_s(v)=0\quad\hbox{unless }i_2+i_3+j_2+j_3\le s.
\end{equation}
The all-marked half gives $H_0(0)=1$.

\begin{lemma}\label{upo:ex5:lem:mixed}
Gluing any northern and southern halves along their marked chains,
with any order-preserving shuffles in common position and value gaps,
creates no additional increasing five-chain. In fact, an increasing
chain using both kinds of extras has length at most three.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
First exclude three alternating runs. If a northern point in cell
$(a,b)$ precedes a comparable southern point in cell $(c,d)$, then
$b\le d$ and
\[
 c\ge d+2\ge b+2\ge a+4\ge4.
\]
A subsequent comparable northern point would have position gap at
least 4, impossible because northern position gaps are at most 3.
Thus an $N,S,N$ triple of extras is impossible. Transposition excludes
$S,N,S$. Any mixed chain has just one switch of type, ignoring marked
points between its extras.

Suppose the switch is from north to south. Let $x$ be the last northern
extra, in value gap $b$, and $y$ the first southern extra, in value gap
$d$. Comparability gives $b\le d$. Let $L$ be the length of the chain
up to and including $x$, and $J$ its length from $y$ onward. The first
segment is entirely in the northern half. Appending
$c_{b+1},\ldots,c_5$ gives a chain involving an extra, so
$L+5-b\le4$. The last segment is entirely in the southern half;
prepending $c_1,\ldots,c_d$ similarly gives $d+J\le4$.
There are at most $d-b$ marked points between $x$ and $y$. The length
of the whole chain is therefore at most
\[
 (b-1)+(d-b)+(4-d)=3.
\]
Transposition gives the other orientation.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Four-binomial gluing]\label{upo:ex5:thm:gluing}
For $n\ge5$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:gluing}
 U_n=\sum_{s+t=n-5}\pair{H_s}{H_t}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Take two northern halves with vectors $v$ and $w$, and transpose the
second to make a southern half. Northern and southern position gaps
overlap precisely in 2 and 3. Their sizes there are respectively
$(i_2,i_3)$ and $(l_2,l_3)$. The value gaps overlap in 2 and 3 as well,
with sizes $(j_2,j_3)$ and $(k_2,k_3)$. Independently interleaving the
two internal orders in each of these four gaps gives exactly $K(v,w)$
choices. All other cross-half comparisons are forced.

Two total orders on the same points, one for positions and one for
values, specify one permutation. A point can participate in both a
position shuffle and a value shuffle without any compatibility
restriction: prescribing the two orders independently is precisely
how a permutation diagram is specified. Lemma~\ref{upo:ex5:lem:mixed} proves
that every such gluing is valid. Conversely, the unique marked chain
in a resulting permutation identifies every northern and southern
extra, and restricting the two total orders recovers both halves and
all four shuffles. The construction is thus a bijection. There are
$n-5=s+t$ extras in total.
\end{proof}

The exclusion of three runs uses the length-five geometry. It already
fails in the analogous cell geometry for length six. No general
arbitrary-length gluing theorem is inferred here.

\section{Two explicit boundary bijections}\label{upo:ex5:sec:boundary}
The boundary locations are part of each object. Forgetting a boundary
flag could identify different objects and would change the count.
All positions, offsets, and ranks in this section are one-based.

\subsection{A single boundary intersection}
Fix $s$ and $v$, and put
\[
 m=s+3,\qquad p=i_2+i_3+2,\qquad q=j_2+j_3+2.
\]
Delete the marked points $c_2,c_4$ from a northern half and standardize
the result to a permutation $\beta\in S_m$.

\begin{proposition}\label{upo:ex5:prop:beta}
This deletion is a bijection onto the boundary-marked permutations
$(\beta,p,q)$ satisfying the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\beta$ avoids $12345$;
\item its final $p$ entries avoid $123$;
\item its $q$ smallest values, in position order, avoid $123$;
\item the two specified sets meet in exactly one point, the pivot;
\item the pivot has rank $j_2+2$ and offset $i_2+1$ in the final-$p$
block.
\end{enumerate}
In particular the pivot is neither the global minimum nor the final
entry. The inverse inserts a new rank 2 immediately before the
specified suffix, then a new rank $q+2$ immediately before the final
entry, shifting old ranks upward at each insertion as needed.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Northernness implies that $c_1,c_2$ are the two smallest values and
$c_4,c_5$ are the final two entries. After deletion the specified suffix
consists of the position-gap-2 extras, $c_3$, the position-gap-3 extras,
and $c_5$. The specified low-value set consists of $c_1$, the
value-gap-2 extras, $c_3$, and the value-gap-3 extras. Only $c_3$ is
common to them, and the claimed pivot offset and rank follow.

A five-chain in $\beta$ would already be a second five-chain in the
original half. Any increasing triple in the suffix, preceded by
$c_1,c_2$, would likewise give a second five-chain; the original marked
triple $c_3,c_4,c_5$ is unavailable because $c_4$ was deleted. The
low-value condition follows in the same way by appending $c_4,c_5$,
since $c_2$ was deleted.

For the converse perform the two stated insertions, calling the new
points $c_2,c_4$. Let $c_1$ be the old minimum, $c_3$ the pivot, and
$c_5$ the old final point. The minimum lies outside the suffix, since
it is not the unique intersection; hence it precedes $c_2$. Similarly
$c_5$ lies outside the low-value set and is above $c_4$. The five marked
points form an increasing chain.

Any other five-chain must use at least one inserted point. If it uses
$c_2$ but not $c_4$, it has at most one smaller predecessor and would
need three old suffix points in increasing order, a contradiction.
If it uses $c_4$ but not $c_2$, it has at most one successor and would
need three increasing old low-value points, also a contradiction.
If it uses both, it has at most one point before $c_2$ and at most one
after $c_4$. The only point lying between them in both coordinates is
the pivot, by the unique-intersection condition. Thus its five points
are exactly the marked chain.

It remains to check northernness. Every extra has value gap at least
2 and position gap at most 3. Extras in position gaps 2 and 3 are
outside the low-value block, hence in value gaps at least 4. The only
remaining potential violations are cells $(1,2)$ and $(3,4)$.
The former would make $c_1,x,c_3$ an increasing triple in the old
low-value block; the latter would make $c_3,x,c_5$ an increasing triple
in the old suffix. Both are excluded. All extras are northern.
Deletion and the prescribed insertions recover the same relative
positions and ranks, proving both inverse identities.
\end{proof}

\Needspace{15\baselineskip}
\subsection{Disjoint nested boundaries}
Now delete the pivot from $\beta$, and standardize to
$\gamma\in S_M$, where $M=s+2$. Use the parameters in
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Pdef}. Denote by $D_M(P,R;Q,T)$ the number of permutations
$\gamma$ with the following six properties:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\gamma$ avoids $12345$;
\item its final $P$ entries avoid $123$;
\item its final $R$ entries decrease;
\item its $Q$ smallest values, in position order, avoid $123$;
\item its $T$ smallest values, in position order, decrease;
\item its final-$P$ set and smallest-$Q$ set are disjoint.
\end{enumerate}
The natural range is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Drange}
 1\le R\le P,\quad 1\le T\le Q,\quad P+Q\le M.
\end{equation}
We define this count to be zero outside that range when using it for
halves.

\begin{theorem}[Nested boundary bijection]\label{upo:ex5:thm:D}
For \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Pdef} and $M=s+2$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:HD}
 H_s(v)=D_{s+2}(P,R;Q,T).
\end{equation}
The inverse from $\gamma$ to $\beta$ inserts a new point of rank $T+1$
immediately before the final $R$ entries. Composing this with the two
insertions of Proposition~\ref{upo:ex5:prop:beta} recovers the northern half.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
After pivot deletion, avoidance and disjointness are inherited. In
$\beta$ the pivot is smaller than every other suffix entry, so an
increasing pair among the $R$ entries following it would contradict
suffix $123$-avoidance. Those entries therefore decrease. Similarly,
all $T$ values smaller than the pivot occur before it and must decrease,
or an increasing pair would extend to a triple ending at the pivot.
This proves the forward conditions.

Conversely, insert rank $T+1$ before the last $R$ entries. The old
suffix consists only of values above all old low-block values, by
disjointness. Every old low-block entry is before the old suffix.
The inserted point is thus the unique intersection of the enlarged
suffix of size $P+1$ and enlarged low-value block of size $Q+1$.
It is neither the minimum nor the final entry. In the enlarged suffix
it is the minimum; any new triple through it would require an
increasing pair in the final $R$ entries. In the enlarged low block
it is the last point; any new triple would require an increasing pair
among the $T$ smaller values. Neither is possible.

More generally, a chain through the pivot has at most one smaller
predecessor \emph{within that increasing chain}, and at most one larger
successor, by the two decreasing conditions. Its length is at most
three. Inserting the pivot cannot create a five-chain. Thus all of
Proposition~\ref{upo:ex5:prop:beta}'s conditions hold. Its offset is
$P-R+1=i_2+1$, its rank is $T+1=j_2+2$, and the remaining statistics
are $i_3=R-1$ and $j_3=Q-T$. The operations are exact mutual inverses.
\end{proof}

\section{The six-term inverse-Cauchy formula}\label{upo:ex5:sec:sym}
We give the coupling argument explicitly. Disjointness of the two
boundaries cannot be imposed by simply multiplying independent RSK
factors.

\subsection{Symmetric functions and reversed positions}
In the Hall inner product the Schur functions are orthonormal.
The operator $s_\tau^\perp$ is adjoint to multiplication by $s_\tau$;
equivalently,
\[
 s_\tau^\perp s_\alpha
   =\sum_\nu c^\alpha_{\tau,\nu}s_\nu,
\]
where $c^\alpha_{\tau,\nu}$ are Littlewood--Richardson coefficients.
Write $p_1=s_{(1)}$, and define the truncated inner product by
\[
 \ip{s_\rho}{s_\eta}
  =\begin{cases}1&\rho=\eta,\ \ell(\rho)\le4,\\0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}
\]
Standard tableaux are equivalently saturated paths in Young's
lattice, so
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:skewbranch}
 [s_\rho]\,p_1^{|\rho|-|\nu|}s_\nu=f^{\rho/\nu}.
\end{equation}

Reverse the positions of $\gamma$, writing
$\sigma_i=\gamma_{M+1-i}$. Then $\sigma$ avoids a decreasing
subsequence of length five. Its initial $P$ entries and its subsequence
of the $Q$ smallest values avoid $321$; its initial $R$ entries and
subsequence of its $T$ smallest values increase. Finally its
first-$P$-position by lowest-$Q$-value rectangle is empty.

Under ordinary RSK, longest decreasing subsequence length is the
number of rows of the common shape. The initial $P$ recording-tableau
labels have a shape $\alpha$ with at most two rows; the first $R$
labels have single-row shape $(R)$. The number of their standard
fillings is $f^{\alpha/(R)}$, since the initial row has one filling.
The analogous insertion-tableau factor is $f^{\beta/(T)}$.
The empty rectangle couples these two boundary data.

\subsection{A literal matrix corner}
Use ordered alphabets $A=(a_1,a_2)$ and $B=(b_1,b_2)$ of exactly two
variables each, and arbitrary remaining alphabets $C,D$.
Rows indexed by $A$ precede rows indexed by $C$, and columns indexed
by $B$ precede columns indexed by $D$. For a nonnegative integer
matrix, a decreasing chain uses strictly increasing row indices and
strictly decreasing column indices, with positive entries in its
cells. Matrix RSK gives the bounded-height series
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:K4}
 \mathcal K_4(A+C,B+D)
   =\sum_{\ell(\rho)\le4}s_\rho(A+C)s_\rho(B+D).
\end{equation}
Each matrix entry in row variable $x$ and column variable $y$ has
weight $(xy)^m$ when it equals $m$. The equality follows from the
RSK weight correspondence and the decreasing-chain theorem.

Let $\mathcal E_4(A,B;C,D)$ count the same matrices with the
$A$-by-$B$ corner forced to zero.

\begin{lemma}[Harmless corner]\label{upo:ex5:lem:corner}
Restoring arbitrary entries in the two-by-two corner cannot create
a decreasing chain of length five. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:corner}
 \mathcal E_4(A,B;C,D)
 =\prod_{a\in A,b\in B}(1-ab)\,
   \mathcal K_4(A+C,B+D).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A decreasing chain through a corner cell has at most two points up
to and including that cell, because only two initial rows are
available, and at most two points from that cell onward, because
only two initial columns are available. Its total length is at most
$2+2-1=3$. Any forbidden chain would therefore avoid the entire
corner. Its four entries may be restored independently, contributing
the geometric factors $\prod(1-ab)^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

This uses full rectangular matrix RSK. It does not invoke a
Ferrers-board chain theorem for chains that may cross a missing
corner. That distinction matters: on the board with row lengths
$(2,1)$, rooks at $(1,2),(2,1)$ form a global decreasing pair,
but their bounding rectangle is not in the board. Ferrers chain
statistics in the relevant growth-diagram theorem~\cite{Krat}
require the bounding rectangle to be contained in the board.

\subsection{Why the initial fillings can be summed independently}
The next lemma supplies the saturation step behind coefficient
extraction. It is not an assumption of independence between the two
full tableaux.

\begin{lemma}[Initial-filling saturation]\label{upo:ex5:lem:saturation}
Fix the initial recording shape $\alpha$ for alphabet $A$, the
initial insertion shape $\beta$ for alphabet $B$, and the two outer
skew tableaux filled with labels from $C$ and $D$. Among the full RSK
pairs with these data, emptiness of the $A$-by-$B$ matrix block is
independent of the fillings of the two initial shapes. The assertion
holds for standard tableaux and for the semistandard tableaux of
matrix RSK.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Run inverse row insertion in the order specified by all recording
labels in $C$, leaving those in $A$ until later. The removable cells
and their order are determined by the outer recording skew tableau;
with repeated recording labels, the usual reverse-RSK tie order is
used. The actual $A$ filling is never consulted during this phase.

Call insertion entries from $B$ special and those from $D$
nonspecial. Every special entry is smaller than every nonspecial
entry; in each row, the special cells form an initial segment.
Track only the nonspecial entries and the shape occupied by special
entries. At a reverse insertion, the carried entry and its next
replacement follow the usual rule: in the preceding row replace the
rightmost entry strictly smaller than the carried entry, and carry
the displaced entry upward.

While the carried entry is nonspecial, if a smaller nonspecial entry
exists in the next row, the rightmost such entry and its value depend
only on the nonspecial data. If none exists, the selected entry is
the rightmost special entry; its position is determined solely by
the special-shape boundary. Replacing it by the nonspecial carried
entry changes that boundary in a prescribed way and makes the next
carried entry special. Once the carried entry is special, every
subsequently displaced entry is special. Replacements then keep
special cells special and do not change the nonspecial data or the
special-shape boundary. Which special values are exchanged is
irrelevant to the tracked data. The case where the first removed
entry is already special has the same conclusion.

Induction through all reverse insertions with recording labels in
$C$ proves that the remaining nonspecial data and special shape,
as well as the special or nonspecial type of every emitted value,
are independent of the initial special filling. The remaining
insertion tableau represents exactly the rows labeled by $A$.
The $A$-by-$B$ block is empty if and only if this tableau has no
special entry. This proves independence from the $B$ filling, and
the first paragraph proved independence from the $A$ filling.
The argument uses only the order separation $B<D$ and the usual
reverse-row-insertion rule, so it applies in both standard and
semistandard cases.
\end{proof}

It follows that the expansion
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:saturation}
 \mathcal E_4(A,B;C,D)
  =\sum_{\ell(\alpha),\ell(\beta)\le2}
       s_\alpha(A)s_\beta(B)\,
       E_{\alpha,\beta}(C,D)
\end{equation}
has the following meaning: each outer pair allowed by the empty
corner admits every initial filling of its specified shapes.
Replacing an initial filling by any valid filling of the same shape
preserves tableau validity across its interface: all initial labels
are smaller than all outer labels. The two Schur factors therefore
count precisely those independent choices of fillings. Restricting a standard initial tableau of shape $\alpha$
to have first $R$ labels in a row replaces its unrestricted filling
count by $f^{\alpha/(R)}$, and similarly on the other side. This is
also the empty-inner-corner instance of skew RSK~\cite{SS}; the
explicit reverse-insertion proof is included to identify the exact
independence used here.

\subsection{Coefficient extraction and the exact formula}
Dual Cauchy in two variables on both sides gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:dualcauchy}
 \prod_{a\in A,b\in B}(1-ab)
   =\sum_{\tau\subseteq(2,2)}(-1)^{|\tau|}
         s_\tau(A)s_{\tau'}(B).
\end{equation}
Both two-variable restrictions are essential: $s_\tau(A)$ requires
at most two rows, and $s_{\tau'}(B)$ at most two columns. The six
partitions are exactly $\Tset$ in \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Gdef}.

\begin{theorem}[Six-term formula]\label{upo:ex5:thm:six}
For the range \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Drange},
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:six}
 D_M(P,R;Q,T)
 =\sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^{|\tau|}
 \ip{p_1^{M-P}s_\tau^\perp G_{P,R}}
     {p_1^{M-Q}s_{\tau'}^\perp G_{Q,T}}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Insert \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:K4} and \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:dualcauchy} into
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:corner}, and branch
$s_\rho(A+C)=\sum_\nu s_\nu(A)s_{\rho/\nu}(C)$,
with the analogous expansion in $B,D$.
For $|\alpha|=P$, extracting the $s_\alpha(A)$ coefficient and
weighting it by $f^{\alpha/(R)}$ moves multiplication by
$s_\tau(A)$ to its adjoint $s_\tau^\perp G_{P,R}$.
Initial-filling saturation, Lemma~\ref{upo:ex5:lem:saturation}, proves that
this weighted extraction imposes exactly the required initial
$R$-label condition, even with the empty corner. The same extraction
in $B$ gives $s_{\tau'}^\perp G_{Q,T}$.

To standardize the remaining labels, use the Plancherel
specializations $p_1(C)=z$, $p_j(C)=0$ for $j>1$, and
$p_1(D)=w$, $p_j(D)=0$ for $j>1$. A skew Schur function of degree $q$
becomes $f^{\rho/\nu}z^q/q!$. Extract
$z^{M-P}w^{M-Q}$ and multiply by $(M-P)!(M-Q)!$.
Equation~\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:skewbranch} then gives exactly \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:six}.
In its $\tau$ term the common outer shape has size $M-|\tau|$:
both homogeneous factors have that degree. The signs and size shifts
are thus determined, rather than inferred from a factorization.
\end{proof}

For $v=0$, $P=R=Q=T=1$ and $G_{1,1}=s_1$.
Only $\tau=\varnothing,(1)$ survive, giving the useful exact check
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:H0}
 H_s(0)=A_{s+2}-A_{s+1}.
\end{equation}
The nonempty terms of \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:six} are the coupling correction.
They cannot be suppressed merely because each boundary condition
separately has a simple tableau interpretation.

\section{A fixed-dimensional binomial sum}\label{upo:ex5:sec:diagonal}
We explain why the exact expression belongs to the binomial-sum
class, rather than just asserting that a finite tableau sum is
holonomic.

For $\tau\in\Tset$ and a two-row partition $\nu$, set
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:bcoef}
 b_{P,R}^{\tau}(\nu)
 =\sum_{\substack{\alpha\vdash P\\\ell(\alpha)\le2}}
   f^{\alpha/(R)}c^\alpha_{\nu,\tau}.
\end{equation}
The six-term formula becomes the explicit fixed-height expansion
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:expanded}
 \sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^{|\tau|}
 \sum_{\substack{\rho\vdash M-|\tau|\\\ell(\rho)\le4}}
 \left(\sum_{\substack{\nu\vdash P-|\tau|\\\ell(\nu)\le2}}
       b_{P,R}^{\tau}(\nu)f^{\rho/\nu}\right)
 \left(\sum_{\substack{\eta\vdash Q-|\tau|\\\ell(\eta)\le2}}
       b_{Q,T}^{\tau'}(\eta)f^{\rho/\eta}\right).
\end{equation}
A sum over a negative size is empty. Partitions are padded with zeroes
as necessary. For $\nu=(u,v)$, $\tau=(t_1,t_2)$, and
$\alpha=(a,b)$, the two-row Littlewood--Richardson rule is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:LR2}
 c^\alpha_{\nu,\tau}=1
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 \begin{gathered}
 |\alpha|=|\nu|+|\tau|,\qquad
 r=u+t_1-a=b-v-t_2,\\
 0\le r\le\min(u-v,t_1-t_2).
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
It is zero otherwise. This is the usual $GL(2)$ tensor-product
rule; it also follows directly from two-row Littlewood--Richardson
words. Thus it is an indicator of finitely many affine constraints.

For partitions $\rho,\nu$ with at most $h$ rows, with $h$ fixed,
the skew-tableau determinant is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:det}
 f^{\rho/\nu}=q!\det_{1\le i,j\le h}
          \left(\frac{1}{(\rho_i-\nu_j-i+j)!}\right),
 \qquad q=|\rho|-|\nu|.
\end{equation}
This follows by applying the Plancherel specialization to the
skew Jacobi--Trudi determinant: $h_j$ becomes $z^j/j!$, while
$s_{\rho/\nu}$ becomes $f^{\rho/\nu}z^q/q!$.
Here a reciprocal factorial of a negative integer is zero, and a
noncontained inner shape gives zero. In the determinant term
indexed by $\pi\in S_h$, put
$d_i=\rho_i-\nu_{\pi(i)}-i+\pi(i)$. Since $\sum_i d_i=q$,
that term is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:multinomial}
 \operatorname{sgn}(\pi)\binom{q}{d_1,\ldots,d_h}
 =\operatorname{sgn}(\pi)
   \prod_{j=1}^{h-1}\binom{q-\sum_{i<j}d_i}{d_j},
\end{equation}
when every $d_i\ge0$, and zero otherwise. This cancellation of
factorials is important: no arbitrary factorial quotient is being
silently treated as a binomial sum.

\begin{theorem}\label{upo:ex5:thm:diagonal}
The sequence $(U_n)_{n\ge0}$ is a multiple binomial sum over
$\mathbb Q$. Hence there exist an integer $d$ and a rational power
series $\mathcal R(x_1,\ldots,x_d)$, regular at the origin, such that
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}U_n z^n
   =\sum_{n\ge0}[x_1^n\cdots x_d^n]\mathcal R\,z^n.
\]
There are polynomials $q_0(n),\ldots,q_r(n)\in\mathbb Q[n]$, not
all zero, with $\sum_{j=0}^r q_j(n)U_{n+j}=0$ for all sufficiently
large $n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Use \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:LR2} and the determinant expansion
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:multinomial} in \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:expanded}. Every summand is a
product of a fixed number of binomial coefficients with affine
arguments and indicators of affine equalities and inequalities.
The partition lengths are bounded by two or four, and the determinant
permutations and $\tau$ range over fixed finite sets. Every coordinate
can be bounded between 0 and $M+4$; the number of coordinates is
independent of $M$.

The gluing formula adds $s$ and the eight boundary coordinates, with
$t=n-5-s$, the affine support constraints, and the four binomial
factors of $K$. All indices have affine upper bounds in $n$.
Kronecker deltas and Heaviside functions imposing the affine
constraints are binomial sums; products, affine substitutions, and
bounded affine-index sums preserve that class~\cite[Definition 1.1
and Section 1]{BLS}. This proves the first statement, including the
finite zero initial segment. The equivalence between univariate
binomial sums and rational diagonals is \cite[Theorem 3.5]{BLS};
P-recursiveness is its Corollary 3.6.
\end{proof}

The proof is constructive in the sense of those closure operations.
It does not supply a compact rational function or a minimal recurrence.
The existence theorem is stronger than a numerical recurrence guess,
and finite agreement with a guessed recurrence would not replace it.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
The check of Remark~\ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} applies, with one reading made explicit. In \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:multinomial} the product runs over $j\le h-1$, so the condition $d_h\ge0$ that the text requires (``when every $d_i\ge0$, and zero otherwise'') must enter the summand as the indicator $h(d_h)=\mathsf B(d_h,d_h)$ of Part~II, just as Part~II's last factor $\mathsf B(e_3,e_3)$ does. This Part describes its encoding in words; Parts II and IV display theirs.
\end{writenote}

\section{Avoidance and a uniform boundary bound}\label{upo:ex5:sec:bounds}
Throughout the remaining proof, Haar measure on $U(4)$ has total
mass one, and $p=15/2$.
RSK, transposition of shapes when necessary, and unitary character
orthogonality give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:avoid}
 A_m=\sum_{\substack{\rho\vdash m\\\ell(\rho)\le4}}(f^\rho)^2
     =\int_{U(4)}|\tr U|^{2m}\dd U.
\end{equation}
Regev's bounded-height asymptotic~\cite{Regev1981}, with its explicit
normalization also recorded in~\cite[Section 3.1.4]{Regev2010},
specializes to
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Regev}
 A_m\sim C_A16^m m^{-p},\qquad C_A=\frac{1536}{\pi^{3/2}}.
\end{equation}
For normalization, the standard $h$-row constant is
\[
 \frac{h^{h^2/2}\prod_{j=1}^{h-1}j!}
 {2^{(h^2+h-2)/2}\pi^{(h-1)/2}};
 \qquad h=4:\quad
 \frac{4^8(1!2!3!)}{2^9\pi^{3/2}}
 =\frac{1536}{\pi^{3/2}}.
\]
In particular there are global constants $0<c<C<\infty$ with
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Abounds}
 c\frac{16^m}{(m+1)^p}\le A_m\le C\frac{16^m}{(m+1)^p}
 \quad(m\ge0),\qquad
 \frac{A_{m-d}}{A_m}\longrightarrow16^{-d}
\end{equation}
for every fixed integer shift $d$. The finite initial range is
absorbed into $c,C$.

\subsection{The aggregate two-row character bound}
Dropping just disjointness from $D_M$ leaves the two nested tableau
restrictions, one in each RSK tableau. Thus the resulting count is
the nonnegative empty-$\tau$ term of \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:six}. It follows that
\begin{align}\label{upo:ex5:eq:drop}
 0\le H_s(v)
 &\le\ip{p_1^{M-P}G_{P,R}}{p_1^{M-Q}G_{Q,T}}\notag\\
 &\le G_{P,R}(1^4)G_{Q,T}(1^4)
       \int_{U(4)}|\tr U|^{2M-P-Q}\dd U,
 \qquad M=s+2.
\end{align}
The last inequality uses $|s_\alpha(U)|\le s_\alpha(1^4)$ for a
unitary character. No sign assumption on the five other correction
terms is required.

For $\alpha=(a,b)$ with $a+b=P$, Weyl's formula gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:Weyl}
 s_{(a,b)}(1^4)
  =\frac{(a-b+1)(a+2)(a+3)(b+1)(b+2)}{12}
  \le\frac{(P+3)^5}{12}.
\end{equation}
Moreover
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:pathbound}
 \sum_{\substack{\alpha\vdash P\\\ell(\alpha)\le2}}
 f^{\alpha/(R)}\le2^{P-R}.
\end{equation}
Indeed a standard skew tableau specifies the successive rows used
by its Young-lattice path from $(R)$; there are at most two choices
at each of the $P-R$ additions. This is a bound on the aggregate
sum over endpoints, so no extra factor for the number of shapes is
needed. Combining the last two displays bounds $G_{P,R}(1^4)$.

On the support \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:support}, $P+Q\le M$.
For $E=2M-P-Q$ we therefore have $M\le E\le2M$.
Let $r=\lfloor E/2\rfloor$. The elementary bound $|\tr U|\le4$
and \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Abounds} give, including odd $E$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:odd}
 \int|\tr U|^E\dd U
   \le4^{E-2r}A_r
   \le C\frac{4^E}{(M+1)^p}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Anisotropic domination]\label{upo:ex5:prop:weight}
There is a constant $C$ independent of $s$ and $v$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:wbound}
 0\le H_s(v)\le C A_s w(v),
 \quad
 w(v)=(P+3)^5(Q+3)^5
 2^{-i_2}4^{-i_3}4^{-j_2}2^{-j_3}.
\end{equation}
In addition
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:mass}
 \sum_{v,w}w(v)w(w)K(v,w)<\infty,
 \qquad \pair{H_s}{H_t}\le C A_sA_t.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Insert \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Weyl}--\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:odd} into \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:drop},
and divide by the lower bound for $A_s$. Up to an absolute factor,
the geometric dependence is
\[
 4^{-P-Q}2^{P-R+Q-T}
 =2^{-4}\,2^{-i_2}4^{-i_3}4^{-j_2}2^{-j_3}.
\]
The fixed shift $M=s+2$ is absorbed into $C$.
Off support $H_s$ is zero, giving the uniform assertion.

For kernel summability, each of its four factors pairs a coordinate
with geometric weight $1/2$ and one with weight $1/4$.
The nonnegative generating series
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:kernelgf}
 \sum_{a,b\ge0}\binom{a+b}{a}x^a y^b=\frac1{1-x-y}
 \qquad(x,y\ge0,\ x+y<1)
\end{equation}
converges at $(x,y)=(1/2,1/4)$ and at a slightly larger positive
pair. Any fixed polynomial in $a,b$ is absorbed by that enlargement.
Each polynomial in sums of coordinates in $w(v)w(w)$ can also be
bounded by a product of coordinate polynomials. Taking the product
of the four convergent two-coordinate sums proves the first part of
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:mass}. The second follows from \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:wbound}.
\end{proof}

The unequal geometric weights are useful. A cruder symmetric
replacement by $2^{-i_2-i_3-j_2-j_3}$ would put a paired kernel at
$x+y=1$, losing this summability argument.

\section{The positive half limit}\label{upo:ex5:sec:halflimit}
We now identify the leading behavior for each fixed boundary vector.
This requires concentration on the whole scalar circle, not just at
the identity matrix.

\begin{lemma}[Trace-weighted character limit]\label{upo:ex5:lem:haar}
Let $F,E$ be fixed homogeneous symmetric polynomials of degrees
$d,e$, evaluated on $U(4)$. As integers $r\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:haar}
 \frac{1}{A_r}\int_{U(4)}
   (\tr U)^{r-d}F(U)
   \overline{(\tr U)^{r-e}E(U)}\dd U
 \longrightarrow\frac{F(1^4)\overline{E(1^4)}}{4^{d+e}}.
\end{equation}
Only $r\ge\max(d,e)$ is used.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The maximum $|\tr U|=4$ occurs precisely at
$\mathcal S=\{zI_4:|z|=1\}$. For any neighborhood of $\mathcal S$,
compactness bounds the trace outside it by $4-\eta$ for some
$\eta>0$. The lower bound in \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Abounds} implies that the
probability measures
\[
 \dd\mu_r(U)=A_r^{-1}|\tr U|^{2r}\dd U
\]
concentrate on $\mathcal S$; the mass outside the neighborhood is
bounded by a polynomial in $r$ times $((4-\eta)/4)^{2r}$.

On a sufficiently small neighborhood of $\mathcal S$, the function
\[
 \Psi(U)=\frac{F(U)\overline{E(U)}}
                  {(\tr U)^d\overline{(\tr U)}^e}
\]
is continuous. Homogeneity gives $F(zI_4)=z^dF(1^4)$ and the
analogous formula for $E$, so its value on all of $\mathcal S$ is
$F(1^4)\overline{E(1^4)}4^{-d-e}$. This proves the desired limit
inside the neighborhood. No division by zero is used outside it:
bound the original numerator directly by
$C(4-\eta)^{2r-d-e}$ and use the same polynomially corrected
exponential suppression. The neighborhood can then shrink to
$\mathcal S$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{upo:ex5:prop:halflimit}
For every fixed $v$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:half-limit}
 \frac{H_s(v)}{A_s}\longrightarrow h(v),
 \qquad 0\le h(v)\le Cw(v),\qquad h(0)=240.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Fix a term $\tau$ in \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:six} and write $k=|\tau|$,
$F=s_\tau^\perp G_{P,R}$, $E=s_{\tau'}^\perp G_{Q,T}$.
If either is zero there is nothing to prove. Otherwise their degrees
are $d=P-k$, $e=Q-k$. The term is the Haar integral in
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:haar} with $r=M-k$, since $r-d=M-P$ and $r-e=M-Q$.
Here $M=s+2$, so
\[
 \frac{A_{M-k}}{A_s}\longrightarrow16^{2-k},
 \qquad
 16^{2-k}4^{-(P+Q-2k)}=256\,4^{-P-Q}.
\]
The six terms therefore give exactly \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:hdef}.
Nonnegativity of $H_s$ proves $h(v)\ge0$ despite the alternating
finite formula, and \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:wbound} passes to the limit.
Finally \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:H0} and \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Abounds} give
$h(0)=256-16=240$.
\end{proof}

This proof identifies a nonnegative boundary limit but supplies no
numerical summation of its infinite convolution amplitude. In
particular it does not estimate $R_5$ by fitting large finite ratios.

\section{Endpoint convolution and the leading equivalent}\label{upo:ex5:sec:ratio}
Set $N=n-5$. For fixed $t$, dominated convergence in the boundary
vectors, using \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:wbound} and \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:mass}, gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:endpoint}
 \frac{\pair{H_{N-t}}{H_t}}{A_{N+5}}
 \longrightarrow16^{-t-5}\pair h{H_t}.
\end{equation}
Although $H_t$ has finite support, the first vector ranges over an
unbounded set as $N$ grows; the weighted-kernel bound is what
justifies that remaining limit interchange.

\begin{theorem}[Positive ratio limit]\label{upo:ex5:thm:ratio}
The series \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Rdef} converges absolutely and consists of
nonnegative terms. It is strictly positive and equals
$\lim_{n\to\infty}U_n/A_n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The uniform estimate \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:mass} and the two-sided avoidance
bounds yield, for $0\le s\le N$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:convolution-bound}
 \frac{\pair{H_s}{H_{N-s}}}{A_{N+5}}
 \le C\frac{(N+6)^p}{(s+1)^p(N-s+1)^p}.
\end{equation}
Fix an integer $L\ge1$. On the middle range $L\le s\le N-L$,
split at $N/2$. In the left part $(N+6)/(N-s+1)$ is bounded,
and symmetry gives the same estimate for the right part. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:middle}
 \sum_{s=L}^{N-L}
   \frac{\pair{H_s}{H_{N-s}}}{A_{N+5}}
 \le C\sum_{j\ge L}(j+1)^{-p}.
\end{equation}
This tends to zero as $L\to\infty$, uniformly in all sufficiently
large $N$, since $p=15/2>1$.

For fixed $L$ and $N>2L$, the endpoint ranges $0\le s<L$ and
$N-L<s\le N$ are disjoint. Apply \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:endpoint} to their
finitely many terms. Symmetry of the kernel makes their limits
equal, giving the factor two. Let $N\to\infty$ first and then
$L\to\infty$. Formula \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:gluing} and the vanishing middle
bound prove \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Rdef} and the ratio limit.

Furthermore, $0\le h(v)\le Cw(v)$ and the same kernel estimate give
\[
 0\le16^{-t}\pair h{H_t}
 \le C16^{-t}A_t\le C(t+1)^{-p}.
\]
Thus the series converges absolutely, with an analytical tail
$O((L+1)^{1-p})$ after index $L$. The unspecified constant in this
bound is not a numerical tail certificate.
Finally $H_0$ is a unit mass at $0$, $K(0,0)=1$, and $h(0)=240$.
Retaining just $t=0,v=w=0$ gives
\[
 R_5\ge2\,16^{-5}\,240=\frac{15}{32768}>0.
\]
\end{proof}

Combining this theorem with Regev's equivalent
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Regev} proves \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:main}. The exponent and
exponential rate agree with the previously known
$\Theta$ statement \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:known-theta}; the convergence to the
specified positive amplitude is the additional conclusion of this
argument. The small lower bound above is deliberately crude and is
not presented as a useful decimal approximation.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Part~VI proves the expansion to every order (Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:main}). Numerical values, from the intake dossier and uncertified: floating-point partial sums of \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Rdef} through $t=30$ reach $0.20407$, a tail estimate suggests $R_5\approx0.2044\pm0.0003$, and Richardson extrapolation of exact ratios $U_n/A_n$ for $28\le n\le40$ gives about $0.2040$. The ratio itself is not monotone ($0.166$ at $n=10$, a peak of about $0.3615$ near $n=30$, $0.3397$ at $n=40$). The two routes agree, but neither is an enclosure (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, item~3).
\end{writenote}

\section{Leading inversion with rounding justified}\label{upo:ex5:sec:inverse}
All logarithms are natural. We prove the last assertion of
Theorem~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:overview} without assuming any correction term or quantitative rate in
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:main}.

\begin{proposition}\label{upo:ex5:prop:inverse}
Let $a_n>0$ eventually and suppose
$a_n\sim C e^{\lambda n}n^{-p}$ with $C,\lambda>0$ and fixed real
$p$. If $n_a(x)=\min\{n\ge0:a_n\ge x\}$ is defined using a
nonnegative finite initial segment as well, then, for large $x$,
\[
 n_a(x)=\lceil F_a(x)+o(1)\rceil,
 \qquad
 F_a(x)=\frac{\log x+p\log\log x-p\log\lambda-\log C}{\lambda}.
\]
The $o(1)$ is a real error before rounding. Equivalently, for every
$\epsilon>0$, all sufficiently large $x$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:sandwich}
 \lceil F_a(x)-\epsilon\rceil
 \le n_a(x)\le
 \lceil F_a(x)+\epsilon\rceil.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The assumed equivalent gives $a_{n+1}/a_n\to e^\lambda>1$.
Thus $a_n$ is strictly increasing beyond some integer $n_0$ and
tends to infinity. For $x$ above the finite maximum of the earlier
terms, the first threshold lies in this increasing tail.

Let $\ell(t)$ be the linear interpolation of $\log a_n$ between
successive integers $n\ge n_0$. It is continuous and strictly
increasing. Since
\[
 \log a_n=\lambda n-p\log n+\log C+r_n,
 \qquad r_n\to0,
\]
interpolating gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:interp}
 \ell(t)=\lambda t-p\log t+\log C+o(1)
 \qquad(t\to\infty).
\end{equation}
Indeed the interpolated $r_n$ is bounded by the two endpoint
absolute values, and the interpolation error for $\log t$ is
$O(t^{-2})$ on each unit interval.

For large $x$ there is a unique real $y(x)$ with
$\ell(y(x))=\log x$. Exact monotonicity of the interpolant implies
\begin{equation}\label{upo:ex5:eq:ceil-exact}
 n_a(x)=\lceil y(x)\rceil,
\end{equation}
including the case when $x$ is exactly one of the $a_n$.
Writing $L=\log x$, equation \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:interp} first gives
$y\sim L/\lambda$. It then yields
$y=L/\lambda+O(\log L)$ and hence
$\log y=\log L-\log\lambda+O(\log L/L)$.
Substituting once more gives
\[
 y(x)=\frac{L+p\log L-p\log\lambda-\log C}{\lambda}+o(1).
\]
Together with \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:ceil-exact} this proves the real-error
formulation and the two monotone-ceiling inequalities
\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:sandwich}.
\end{proof}

Apply the proposition to $a_n=U_n$, $C=C_5$, $p=15/2$, and
$\lambda=\log16$. This gives \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Fdef}--\eqref{upo:ex5:eq:inverse-main}.
The existence of an $o(1)$ real error is stronger than merely stating
$n_*(x)=F(x)+O(1)$, but it is not an exact rounding algorithm.
If $F(x)$ is arbitrarily close to an integer, even an arbitrarily
small unknown error can change its ceiling. If for some fixed
$\delta>0$ one knows $\operatorname{dist}(F(x),\mathbb Z)>\delta$,
then for sufficiently large such $x$ the ceiling does agree with
$\lceil F(x)\rceil$. Neither an effective onset nor such a separation
condition for arbitrary inputs is supplied here.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Proposition~\ref{upo:ex5:prop:inverse} is part~(1) of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} (the front-matter section \hyperref[upo:sec:neighbours]{Relation to neighbouring reports}) with the linear interpolation of $\log a_n$, an admissible interpolation in the sense of \texttt{p0:def:three-inverses}, followed by inversion of the leading model; the separation remark above is its part~(2). Part~VI's Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:inverse} sharpens it to every fixed order.
\end{writenote}

\section{Exact checks and the reproducibility boundary}\label{upo:ex5:sec:checks}
The proofs above establish identities and limits for all valid
parameters. Finite checks are a separate safeguard against algebra,
indexing, and implementation mistakes. They do not prove an
infinite identity, Schur saturation, uniform domination, or an
asymptotic theorem.

The companion's standard replay directly enumerates full permutations
for $n=5,\ldots,8$, and evaluates the six-term half formula and
four-binomial gluing through $n=24$. The latter values are compared
with the published exact source data, including the overlapping
OEIS terms. Representative full counts and total half counts are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r r r}
\toprule
$n$&$U_n$&$\sum_v H_{n-5}(v)$\\
\midrule
5&1&1\\6&20&10\\7&270&81\\8&3142&643\\
9&34291&5254\\10&364462&44751\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The optional C++ replay exhaustively checks the two boundary
bijections through half size ten. It recovers the identical
permutation, marked spine, boundaries, and all four statistics under
both insertion/deletion round trips. The standard Python replay
independently checks all 462 supported nested-boundary parameter
tuples for $M=2,\ldots,8$ against direct permutation enumeration,
including the full $(2,2)$ inverse-Cauchy correction. It also checks
5,096 skew-dimension cases by comparing factorial determinants
with removable-corner recursion, and 599,471 RSK tableau/boundary
records for initial-filling saturation. The saturation check includes
131,854 groups with multiple initial fillings and verifies their
complete filling cardinalities, as well as corner-emptiness
invariance. These checks remain finite evidence for the corresponding
proofs. The last published full count reproduced by gluing is
\[
 U_{24}=181921327390401752024.
\]

The companion archive contains the editable article, its PDF, exact
source data with provenance, verification code, closed typed
fixtures, and a complete file inventory. Its README specifies which
checks are rerun by each command and distinguishes routine replay
from optional deeper enumeration. No decimal value of $R_5$ is
stored as a fixture. The code uses exact integer or rational
arithmetic for the identities it checks. Fixtures are data, never
executable input, and independently derived counts are compared
rather than accepted solely because a stored hash matches.

The verifier requires isolated Python before its first shadowable
import and uses explicit exceptions, including under optimized
Python. Inventory, schema, mutation, output-path, and fresh-build
checks concern reproducibility and guard behavior; they are not
formal verification of the mathematical proofs. A manifest is an
integrity record, not a digital signature. The PDF can be rebuilt
from the included source, and the archive can be reconstructed
deterministically in the stated toolchain. Exact command coverage
and byte-comparison results are recorded with the release.

\section{Questions suggested by the exact reduction}\label{upo:ex5:sec:questions}
Several concrete questions remain open in this report.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Can the fixed-dimensional binomial sum be converted into a
compact rational diagonal or a practical minimal recurrence for
$U_n$? The proof of existence does not predict a small order.
\item Can the nonnegative series for $R_5$ be turned into useful
certified intervals? This requires effective constants and useful
tail bounds, not just increasingly long finite ratio fits.
\item What corrections follow beyond the leading ratio? Uniform
boundary estimates and endpoint interactions must be justified at
the relevant orders before formal character expansions can be
used. No correction or all-orders theorem is asserted here.
\item Is the length-five generating function algebraic or
transcendental? Nonrationality and rational diagonality alone do
not decide this question.
\item Which longer monotone patterns admit a different gluing or
boundary description that controls alternating runs? The present
three-run exclusion fails already at length six, so an extension
needs an additional idea.
\item Does a similarly explicit positive ratio constant exist for
more than one occurrence of $12345$? The fixed-occurrence
$\Theta$ results do not supply such a limit by themselves.
\end{enumerate}
\subsection{Further questions and research}\label{upo:ex5:sub:further}
\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions above, 5 October 2026]
Question~1 is Part~VI's question~4: open (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, item~5). Question~2 is Part~VI's question~1: open (item~3). Question~3: \emph{answered} by Part~VI, Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:main} (every fixed order, $r_1=S_5$ explicit, no logarithms). Question~4: open (item~8); nonrationality is known (note in Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:intro}). Questions 5 and~6: open (item~9). The two uncited claims of Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:intro} are pinned in the note there. The identification of Lemma~\ref{upo:ex5:lem:saturation} with the empty-inner-corner case of skew RSK \cite{SS} is cited, not checked in the write; the Part's own proof does not depend on it. Nothing in this Part was found to be wrong.
\end{writenote}

\clearpage
\part{All orders asymptotics and inverse thresholds for unique 12345}\label{upo:all5:part}
\partsource{Bundle Report~140, archive \file{A224248_All_Orders_Asymptotics_and_Inverse_Thresholds_Source.zip}, delivered as \file{Report140.tex} (18 pages), dated 2~October 2026; title block ``All orders asymptotics and inverse thresholds for unique 12345'', author line ``Report 140''. Printed as delivered, with \textbf{[write]} notes; a section number is Report~140's plus~$54$, and equations are renumbered within sections. Its table of contents is replaced by the report's. Section~\ref{upo:all5:sub:further} is added in the write.}
\begin{center}
\small

Reading conventions of Part~VI & Elsewhere in the report\\
\midrule
$U_n$, $A_n$, $H_s(v)$, $P,R,Q,T$, $K$ as in Part~V & \\
$p=15/2$, $\alpha=13/2$, $C_A$, $\lambda=\log16$ & Part~II: $\alpha=-11/2$; Parts I--IV: $\lambda=\log9$\\
$\bfU_j$ arrays in $E_{x,y}$; $a_s=16^{-s}H_s$ & Part~III: $U_j$ in $E_r$\\
$h(v)$, $B(v)$ \eqref{upo:all5:eq:h}--\eqref{upo:all5:eq:B} & Parts II--III: $B(i,j)$\\
$q_j(n)=[z^n](1-z)^{\alpha+j}$; $v_j$, $T_j$, $\mu_\ell$, $m_\ell$ & Part~III: $b_k(n)$, $V_k$, $T_j$\\
$r_j$ coefficients, $S_5=r_1$ & Part~III: $r_j$, $S=r_1$ for $1234$\\
$Q_k=\sum\theta_a^k$; $Q$ a decay exponent; $D(\theta)$, $D(F)$, $J(F)$, $V(F)$ & Part~I: $Q_k$ polynomials\\
$d=P-t$; the constant $d$ of Section~\ref{upo:all5:sec:inverse}; $M=C_AR_5$ there & \\
$N(X)$, $L=\log X$, $n_J(X)$, $P_j$ & Part~III: $N(T)$, $Q_j$\\
