\begin{partabstract}
Let $U_n$ count permutations with exactly one classical increasing
subsequence of length five, OEIS A224248, and let $A_n$ count the
avoiders. Building on the exact half decomposition in Report 138,
we prove a full Poincar\'e expansion
$U_n/A_n=\sum_{j=0}^J r_jn^{-j}+O_J(n^{-J-1})$ at every fixed order.
The leading coefficient is the positive amplitude $R_5$ of that report,
and the first correction has an explicit absolutely convergent series.
The analytic proof has two parts: a character expansion uniform in an
anisotropically weighted Banach space, and a coefficient-level
convolution theorem for tails of exponent $15/2$. Ordinary endpoint
moments converge through degree six; degree seven is the first requiring
subtraction. Exact fractional-binomial models make all regularized
moments absolutely convergent, and the product of any two models is a
polynomial. This identity removes the entire candidate bulk sector and
proves the absence of logarithms or an additional fractional relative
power sector. We give finite coefficient recipes, a smooth-model inverse
to all orders, and integer threshold brackets with the real error placed
before the ceilings. No convergence of the infinite expansion, certified
amplitude decimals, or effective numerical onset is claimed.
\end{partabstract}
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
``Report~138'' \cite{R138} is Part~V of this report, and the ``frozen baseline dependency'' is Part~V with the \file{138-leading-} files.
\end{writenote}


\section{The continuation and its scope}\label{upo:all5:sec:intro}
An occurrence of $12345$ is a choice of five positions with increasing
values, without an adjacency requirement. Write $U_n$ for the number of
permutations with exactly one such occurrence, with $U_0=\cdots=U_4=0$,
and $A_n$ for the number with no occurrence, with $A_0=1$.
These are respectively A224248 and A047889. Put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:constants}
 p=\frac{15}{2},\qquad \alpha=p-1=\frac{13}{2},\qquad
 C_A=\frac{1536}{\pi^{3/2}},\qquad \lambda=\log16.
\end{equation}
All logarithms are natural.

Report 138~\cite{R138} supplies the exact combinatorial half
bijections, the six-term inverse-Cauchy formula, the gluing identity,
a global boundary estimate, and the positive leading ratio limit.
It also proves rational diagonality and P-recursiveness. That report is
a frozen leading-only baseline; its source, PDF, and reproducible
companion are included unchanged as a dependency. The present report
adds the analytic correction and all-orders theorems. It does not
reinterpret the baseline's stated limits as stronger results already
proved there. We restate exactly the baseline inputs needed below;
all subsequent analytic steps are proved here.

\begin{theorem}[All-orders ratio and first correction]\label{upo:all5:thm:main}
There is a unique sequence of real constants $r_0,r_1,\ldots$ such that,
for every fixed integer $J\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:ratio-main}
 \frac{U_n}{A_n}=\sum_{j=0}^J\frac{r_j}{n^j}
                      +O_J(n^{-J-1}).
\end{equation}
Here $r_0=R_5>0$, and $r_1=S_5$ is the absolutely convergent series
in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:S5}. In particular
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:first-main}
 U_n=C_A R_5\,16^n n^{-p}
 \left(1+\frac{S_5/R_5-135/8}{n}+O(n^{-2})\right).
\end{equation}
Every $r_j$ has an explicit prescription from finitely many character
and Gaussian calculations and absolutely convergent regularized
Banach-valued sums. There are no logarithmic terms and no additional
relative half-integer sector in the expansion.
\end{theorem}

The quantifier is ``for every fixed $J$.'' Constants in the remainder
may depend on $J$, and the infinite formal series is not asserted to
converge or to have any particular summability property. Nor do these
formulas certify numerical values of $R_5$, $S_5$, or a usable numerical
onset. The inverse theorem in Section~\ref{upo:all5:sec:inverse} keeps this same
fixed-order interpretation and explicitly preserves integer rounding.

\section{Exact inputs and the weighted space}\label{upo:all5:sec:inputs}
A boundary vector is $v=(i_2,i_3;j_2,j_3)\in\NN^4$. Set
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:PQ}
 P=i_2+i_3+1,\quad R=i_3+1,\quad
 Q=j_2+j_3+1,\quad T=j_2+1.
\end{equation}
The half count $H_s(v)$ of Report 138 counts the northern restriction
of a permutation with its marked unique five-chain and $s$ extra
points. The four indices record the corresponding two internal
position gaps and two internal value gaps. It is zero unless
$i_2+i_3+j_2+j_3\le s$, and $H_0$ is a unit mass at $v=0$.
For $w=(k_2,k_3;l_2,l_3)$ define
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:kernel}
 K(v,w)&=\binom{i_2+l_2}{i_2}\binom{i_3+l_3}{i_3}
          \binom{j_2+k_2}{j_2}\binom{j_3+k_3}{j_3},\\
 \pair XY&=\sum_{v,w\in\NN^4}X(v)Y(w)K(v,w).\notag
\end{align}
The kernel is symmetric. The exact gluing identity is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:glue}
 U_n=\sum_{s+t=n-5}\pair{H_s}{H_t}\qquad(n\ge5).
\end{equation}
This convention transposes a northern half to form a southern half;
it does not reverse boundary coordinates.

Let $s_\nu$ be a Schur function, and let $f^{\nu/\rho}$ count
standard tableaux of the skew shape, zero unless $\rho\subseteq\nu$.
Set
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:G}
 G_{P,R}=\sum_{\substack{\nu\vdash P\\\ell(\nu)\le2}}
          f^{\nu/(R)}s_\nu,\qquad
 \Tset=\{\varnothing,(1),(2),(1,1),(2,1),(2,2)\}.
\end{equation}
The operator $s_\tau^\perp$ is adjoint to multiplication by
$s_\tau$ for the Hall inner product in which Schur functions are
orthonormal; $\tau'$ denotes the transpose partition. For
$\tau\in\Tset$ put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:FG}
 t=|\tau|,\quad F_\tau=s_\tau^\perp G_{P,R},\quad
 G_\tau=s_{\tau'}^\perp G_{Q,T},\quad d=P-t,\quad e=Q-t.
\end{equation}
A zero character contributes zero, including when its indicated
degree is negative. Haar measure on $U(4)$ has mass one. On the
support $P+Q\le s+2$, the six-term formula becomes
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:six}
 H_s(v)=\sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^t
 \int_{U(4)}(\tr U)^{s+2-P}F_\tau(U)
       \overline{(\tr U)^{s+2-Q}G_\tau(U)}\dd U.
\end{equation}
Both exponents outside the characters are nonnegative in this range.
The character coefficients are real.

The other baseline inputs are
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:avoid-input}
 A_s&=\int_{U(4)}|\tr U|^{2s}\dd U
       \sim C_A16^ss^{-p},\\
 c\frac{16^s}{(s+1)^p}&\le A_s\le C\frac{16^s}{(s+1)^p},\notag\\
 0\le H_s(v)&\le C A_s(P+3)^5(Q+3)^5\omega(v),\label{upo:all5:eq:global}\\
 \omega(v)&=2^{-i_2}4^{-i_3}4^{-j_2}2^{-j_3}.\notag
\end{align}
The constants in the last two lines are independent of $s,v$.
The normalization in the first line is Regev's four-row constant;
the derivation and references are in Report 138. The weighted estimate
is an aggregate two-row tableau bound, rather than an estimate on
one fixed boundary.
\begin{writenote}[The inputs, proved in Part~V, 5 October 2026]
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:glue} is Theorem~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:gluing}; \eqref{upo:all5:eq:six} is Theorem~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:six} written as Haar integrals, as in the proof of Proposition~\ref{upo:ex5:prop:halflimit}; \eqref{upo:all5:eq:avoid-input} is \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:avoid} with \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Regev} and \eqref{upo:ex5:eq:Abounds}; \eqref{upo:all5:eq:global} is Proposition~\ref{upo:ex5:prop:weight}. The weighted space $E_{x,y}$ and everything after it are new in this Part.
\end{writenote}

Choose $x>1/2$, $y>1/4$ with $x+y<1$. Let $E=E_{x,y}$ be the space
of arrays with the norm
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:norm}
 \|X\|_E=\sup_v |X(v)|x^{-i_2-j_3}y^{-i_3-j_2}.
\end{equation}
Multiplication by the reciprocal weight identifies $E$ with an
$\ell^\infty$ space, so $E$ is Banach. The kernel pairing extends to a
continuous symmetric bilinear form, because
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:pairbound}
 |\pair XY|\le(1-x-y)^{-4}\|X\|_E\|Y\|_E.
\end{equation}
Indeed the four paired coordinate pairs each give
$\sum_{a,b\ge0}\binom{a+b}{a}x^ay^b=(1-x-y)^{-1}$.
The strict slack in $x>1/2$, $y>1/4$ absorbs every fixed polynomial
in $P+Q$ multiplying $\omega(v)$. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:globalnorm}
 \|H_s\|_E\le C_E A_s,\qquad
 a_s:=16^{-s}H_s=O_E((s+1)^{-p}).
\end{equation}

\section{A local character expansion with an actual remainder}\label{upo:all5:sec:laplace}
This section proves a fixed-order Laplace expansion with sufficient
uniformity to sum the boundary indices later. It is not an inference
from a formal generating-function singularity.

\subsection{The scalar maximum and the local measure}
Write the eigenvalues as $e^{i\phi_a}$. Weyl integration gives a
constant multiple of the eigenangle measure with density
$\prod_{a<b}|e^{i\phi_a}-e^{i\phi_b}|^2$. The trace magnitude has
maximum four precisely when all eigenvalues coincide. Homogeneity in
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:six} cancels the common phase between its two factors. We may
therefore quotient by the scalar circle and use a centered local chart
\[
 \theta_1+\theta_2+\theta_3+\theta_4=0,\qquad
 \theta=(u,v,w,-u-v-w).
\]
Any finite chart multiplicity and its constant Jacobian enter only the
leading normalization already fixed by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:avoid-input}.
Define $Q_k(\theta)=\sum_a\theta_a^k$,
$\Delta(\theta)=\prod_{a<b}(\theta_a-\theta_b)$,
$Z=\sum_ae^{i\theta_a}$, and $z=Z/4$. Near zero put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:phase-density}
 \Phi(\theta)=\log|z|^2,\qquad
 D(\theta)=\prod_{a<b}
 \left(\frac{2\sin((\theta_a-\theta_b)/2)}{\theta_a-\theta_b}\right)^2.
\end{equation}
The removable factors in $D$ have their continuous values on a
diagonal. Both functions are real analytic and even under
$\theta\mapsto-\theta$, with
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:phase-exp}
 \Phi&=-\frac{Q_2}{4}+\frac{Q_4}{48}
                     -\frac{Q_2^2}{64}+O(|\theta|^6),\\
 D&=1-\frac{Q_2}{3}+O(|\theta|^4).\label{upo:all5:eq:density-exp}
\end{align}
For the first line, expand $Z\overline Z$ through degree four and then
the logarithm, using $Q_1=0$. For the second, each sine quotient
contributes $1-(\theta_a-\theta_b)^2/12+O(|\theta|^4)$ and
$\sum_{a<b}(\theta_a-\theta_b)^2=4Q_2$.
The full density is $\Delta^2D$; $\Delta^2$ has degree twelve.

On a sufficiently small fixed chart there are positive constants
$c,C$ for which $\Phi\le-cQ_2$ and $|\Phi+Q_2/4|\le C Q_2^2$.
On its complement the original trace satisfies $|Z|\le4\rho$ for
some fixed $\rho<1$. Thus the local scaling $\theta=\xi/\sqrt{s}$
has degree $3+12=15$ and leads to $s^{-15/2}$.

\subsection{Why finite expansion has a controlled error}
For any fixed $J$, Taylor-expand the phase through degree $2J+2$
and $D$ through degree $2J$. After isolating the quadratic Gaussian,
expand the remaining exponential only to the powers required for
relative order $J$. The omitted phase begins at degree $2J+4$ and
is multiplied by $s$. Shrink the chart if necessary so every
exponential remainder is bounded by a polynomial times
$e^{-csQ_2}$. Taylor's integral remainder gives such a bound directly;
it does not require the formal expansion to converge after integration.
For each nonnegative integer $b$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:radial}
 \int Q_2^b\Delta^2e^{-csQ_2}\dd\theta
                   =O(s^{-p-b}),
\end{equation}
by the change of variable $\theta=\xi/\sqrt{s}$.
An omitted phase factor $sQ_2^{J+2}$ is therefore of relative
order $s^{-J-1}$. The same degree accounting applies to the finite
exponential Taylor remainder and to products with the density.
The part outside the chart is exponentially smaller. This proves
an actual asymptotic expansion through order $J$ with error
$O(s^{-p-J-1})$ for every fixed-degree polynomial insertion.
The constants are uniform when the insertion coefficients range in
the unit ball of that finite-dimensional polynomial space.

The same domination proves the moment estimate needed for
parameter-dependent insertions. For every fixed $b\ge0$ and $c_1>0$,
there are $\epsilon>0,C_b$ such that, for $0\le L\le\epsilon s$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:tilted-moment}
 \E_s[Q_2^b e^{c_1LQ_2};\ \hbox{local chart}]
                        \le C_b s^{-b}.
\end{equation}
Here $\E_s$ uses the probability measure
$A_s^{-1}|\tr U|^{2s}\dd U$. Choose $\epsilon$ so that
$c_1\epsilon sQ_2$ can be absorbed into half the Gaussian decay;
then use \eqref{upo:all5:eq:radial} and the lower bound in
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:avoid-input}. This establishes the estimate uniformly in
$L$, including when $L$ grows linearly with $s$.

\subsection{Gaussian coefficients and the avoidance correction}
Let $\E_\gamma$ be normalized integration on the centered hyperplane
with density $e^{-Q_2/4}\Delta^2$. Its polynomial moments are finite
rational numbers. One exact recipe uses the centered ordinary
Gaussian $\E_0$ with covariance
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:covariance}
 \E_0[\theta_a\theta_b]=2(\delta_{ab}-1/4),\qquad
 \E_\gamma P=\frac{\E_0[P\Delta^2]}{\E_0[\Delta^2]}.
\end{equation}
Wick's rule evaluates the numerator and denominator by finite sums
of products of these rational covariance entries. This recipe also
proves rationality without leaving the Gaussian constants implicit.
For example
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:moments}
 \E_0\Delta^2=18432,\qquad
 \E_\gamma Q_2=30,\qquad
 \E_\gamma Q_2^2=1020,\qquad
 \E_\gamma Q_4=435.
\end{equation}
The two radial values follow by scaling the normalizing integral:
$Q_2/4$ has gamma moments with shape $p=15/2$.
The $Q_4$ value follows by expanding the twelve-degree Vandermonde
square in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:covariance}; it is also an exact finite Wick
calculation, replayed by the companion.

Applying the local expansion without a character insertion yields
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:avoid-all}
 A_s=C_A16^s s^{-p}
 \left(\sum_{j=0}^J\frac{\alpha_j}{s^j}
                        +O_J(s^{-J-1})\right),\qquad
 \alpha_0=1,\quad \alpha_j\in\mathbb Q.
\end{equation}
The first correction is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:alpha1}
 \alpha_1=\E_\gamma\left(\frac{Q_4}{48}
                  -\frac{Q_2^2}{64}-\frac{Q_2}{3}\right)
          =\frac{435}{48}-\frac{1020}{64}-10=-\frac{135}{8}.
\end{equation}
All higher coefficients are obtained by the same finite expansion
and the moment rule \eqref{upo:all5:eq:covariance}.

\section{Uniformity over the six boundary characters}\label{upo:all5:sec:weighted}
\begin{theorem}[Weighted all-orders half expansion]\label{upo:all5:thm:half}
For every admissible $E_{x,y}$ there are arrays $\bfU_j\in E_{x,y}$,
independent of the choice of $x,y$, such that, for each fixed $J$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:half-all}
 \left\|a_s-s^{-p}\sum_{j=0}^J\bfU_j s^{-j}\right\|_E
                      =O_{J,E}(s^{-p-J-1}).
\end{equation}
Each entry of $\bfU_j$ lies in $C_A\mathbb Q$. For some constants
$C_j,D_j$ depending only on $j$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:coeffbound}
 |\bfU_j(v)|\le C_j(1+P+Q)^{D_j}\omega(v).
\end{equation}
The arrays are not asserted to be polynomials in the boundary indices:
the two-row skew-tableau multiplicities in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:G} remain present.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Every nonzero $F=F_\tau$ in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:FG} is Schur-positive and
homogeneous of degree $d=P-t$. Write $D(F)=F(1,1,1,1)$.
The aggregate tableau-path bound and the four-variable two-row
Weyl dimension formula give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:dim-bound}
 D(F)\le C(P+3)^5 2^{P-R},\qquad
 D(G)\le C(Q+3)^5 2^{Q-T}.
\end{equation}
For completeness, a standard skew tableau from $(R)$ to a shape
of size $P$ with at most two rows is a sequence of $P-R$ row choices,
so its aggregate count over final shapes is at most $2^{P-R}$.
The dimension of a two-row shape $(a,b)$ is
\[
 s_{(a,b)}(1^4)=
 \frac{(a-b+1)(a+2)(a+3)(b+1)(b+2)}{12}\le(P+3)^5/12.
\]
Removing any fixed $\tau\subseteq(2,2)$ has only a bounded number
of possible two-row subshapes and bounded Littlewood--Richardson
multiplicities, uniformly in $P$. This changes only the absolute
constant. The second bound is identical.

A normalized positive character is a probability average of monomials:
\[
 \frac{F(e^{i\theta})}{D(F)}
      =\sum_\eta p_\eta e^{i\eta\cdot\theta},\qquad
 p_\eta\ge0,\quad\sum_\eta p_\eta=1,\quad
 \eta\in\NN^4,\quad\sum_a\eta_a=d.
\]
Its directional derivatives of fixed order $h$, in a fixed coordinate
chart, have size at most $C_h(d+1)^h$.
Since $|z|^{-d}=e^{-d\Phi/2}\le e^{CdQ_2}$ locally, finite
differentiation of $z^{-d}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:quotient-bound}
 |D^h z^{-d}|\le C_h(d+1)^h e^{C_hdQ_2}.
\end{equation}
Consequently the real even part of
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:psi}
 \Psi_{F,G}(\theta)
   =\frac{F(e^{i\theta})\overline{G(e^{i\theta})}}
          {z(\theta)^d\overline{z(\theta)}^e}
\end{equation}
has homogeneous Taylor terms of degree $2h$ bounded by
$C_hD(F)D(G)(1+P+Q)^{2h}Q_2^h$. After degree $2J$ its remainder
is bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:psi-remainder}
 C_JD(F)D(G)(1+P+Q)^{2J+2}
    Q_2^{J+1}e^{C_J(P+Q)Q_2}.
\end{equation}
One obtains an even expansion by averaging the Taylor formulas at
$\theta$ and $-\theta$; conjugation of real character coefficients
makes that average real. The odd part integrates to zero against
the symmetric phase and density.

First consider $P+Q\le\epsilon_J s$, for a sufficiently small
$\epsilon_J>0$. Combine \eqref{upo:all5:eq:psi-remainder} with
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:tilted-moment} and the finite-order scalar expansion of
Section~\ref{upo:all5:sec:laplace}. Every omitted term has the required
relative factor $s^{-J-1}$ times a fixed polynomial in $P+Q$ and
$D(F)D(G)$. The finite shifts $r=s+2-t$ are uniformly harmless.
Outside the local chart one must bound the original polynomial
integrand in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:six}, rather than divide by the trace. It is
at most
\[
 D(F)D(G)(4\rho)^{2s+4-P-Q}.
\]
After division by $A_s$, its bound is a constant times
\[
 D(F)D(G)4^{4-P-Q}s^p\rho^{2s+4-P-Q}.
\]
In the present range the exponent of $\rho$ is at least
$(2-\epsilon_J)s+4$, giving exponential suppression uniformly in
the boundary. There is no inverse-trace singularity away from the
chart. From \eqref{upo:all5:eq:dim-bound}, all coefficient and remainder
bounds acquire the geometric factor
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:geom}
 4^{-P-Q}2^{P-R+Q-T}=2^{-4}\omega(v),
\end{equation}
times a fixed polynomial. Dividing by the Banach weights and taking
the supremum therefore preserves the asserted remainder.

Now consider $P+Q>\epsilon_J s$. Set
$\eta=\max\{(2x)^{-1},(4y)^{-1}\}<1$. The weight ratio obeys
\[
 \omega(v)x^{-i_2-j_3}y^{-i_3-j_2}
                 \le\eta^{P+Q-2}.
\]
The global estimate \eqref{upo:all5:eq:global} bounds $a_s$ in this region
by $C(s+1)^{-p}$ times a polynomial in $P+Q$ times that ratio,
which is exponentially small in $s$. The coefficient arrays from
the local calculation satisfy the same type of polynomial-geometric
bound for every $v$, independently of whether $v$ was in the
small-boundary range used to estimate the remainder. Their tail
in this complementary region is also exponentially small. The
region outside the combinatorial support is covered at once:
$H_s=0$ there, while these coefficient tails are still exponentially
small. This proves \eqref{upo:all5:eq:half-all} globally.

Finally, all Taylor coefficients involved are rational after the
common leading constant $C_A$ is removed: the finite characters have
integer coefficients, the phase and sine series have rational
coefficients, and all Gaussian moments are rational by
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:covariance}. This also yields the entrywise finite recipe
and the bounds \eqref{upo:all5:eq:coeffbound}. Uniqueness of a Poincar\'e
expansion coordinate by coordinate shows that the resulting arrays
do not depend on the admissible weights.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A single finite recipe for every half coefficient}\label{upo:all5:sec:half-recipe}
Here is a precise way to implement the coefficient construction,
without relying on an unspecified Laplace algorithm. In the
centered Gaussian variable $\xi$, introduce a formal variable $w$
and define
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:Lrecipe}
 \mathcal L_{\delta,F,G}(w,\xi)
  ={}&D(\sqrt w\,\xi)\,\Psi^{\rm ev}_{F,G}(\sqrt w\,\xi)\notag\\
 &\times\exp\left(\delta\Phi(\sqrt w\,\xi)
       +\frac{\Phi(\sqrt w\,\xi)+wQ_2(\xi)/4}{w}\right).
\end{align}
Here $\Psi^{\rm ev}$ denotes the real even part, so only integer
powers of $w$ occur. The numerator in the last quotient begins at
$w^2$, making the exponential a well-defined formal series with
constant term one. Each fixed coefficient is a polynomial in
$\xi$ calculable by finite Taylor algebra. Then
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:Urecipe}
 \bfU_j(v)=C_A256\,4^{-P-Q}
 \sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^t
      \E_\gamma\,[w^j]\mathcal L_{2-t,F_\tau,G_\tau}(w,\xi).
\end{equation}
To check the prefactor, put $r=s+2-t$, $d=P-t$, $e=Q-t$ in the
integral. The exponential normalization contributes
$16^{2-t}4^{-d-e}=256\,4^{-P-Q}$, and scaling by $s$ gives
$s^{-p}e^{-Q_2/4}$ with the remaining exponential exactly as in
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:Lrecipe}. The uniform proof above validates this coefficient
recipe and its remainder. Similarly, the avoidance coefficients are
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:alpha-recipe}
 \alpha_j=\E_\gamma[w^j]\mathcal L_{0,1,1}(w,\xi).
\end{equation}
No infinite-order Taylor interchange is made in either formula.

\section{The explicit first half correction}\label{upo:all5:sec:first}
For a homogeneous symmetric character $F$ of degree $d$, define
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:VJ}
 V(F)&=\left[
 (x_1\partial_{x_1})^2-(x_1\partial_{x_1})(x_2\partial_{x_2})
                      \right]F\big|_{x_1=\cdots=x_4=1},\notag\\
 J(F)&=\frac{15}{4}\,[4V(F)-dD(F)],\qquad D(F)=F(1^4).
\end{align}
Set all three quantities to zero for the zero character. These are
finite exact polynomial computations. By symmetry, the linear term
of $F(e^{i\theta})$ vanishes on the centered hyperplane and its
quadratic term is $-V(F)Q_2/2$. Also $z=1-Q_2/8+O(|\theta|^3)$,
so the quadratic part of $F/z^d$ is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:quadquot}
 \left(-\frac{V(F)}2+\frac{dD(F)}8\right)Q_2.
\end{equation}
Using $\E_rQ_2=30/r+O(r^{-2})$ gives
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:characterfirst}
 &\frac1{A_r}\int Z^{r-d}F\,\overline{Z^{r-e}G}\dd U\notag\\
 &\qquad=4^{-d-e}\left[
 D(F)D(G)-\frac{J(F)D(G)+D(F)J(G)}{r}
                +O\left(\frac{\operatorname{poly}(d+e)D(F)D(G)}{r^2}\right)
                          \right].
\end{align}
The error is uniform in the small-boundary range of the preceding
proof, and the weighted complementary bound extends the final half
result to all boundaries.

A sometimes convenient equivalent expression for an irreducible
Schur character of size $d$ is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:casimir}
 J(s_\nu)=s_\nu(1^4)
  \left(\kappa_\nu-\frac{d(d-1)}4\right),\qquad
 \kappa_\nu=\sum_i\nu_i(\nu_i-2i+1).
\end{equation}
This quadratic-character simplification is optional; the derivative
formula \eqref{upo:all5:eq:VJ} is the definition used in the proof and gives
an independent finite way to compute every instance.

Define the arrays
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:h}
 h(v)&=256\,4^{-P-Q}\sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^tD(F_\tau)D(G_\tau),\\
 B(v)&=256\,4^{-P-Q}\sum_{\tau\in\Tset}(-1)^t
 \bigl[-p(2-t)D(F_\tau)D(G_\tau)\notag\\[-2pt]
 &\hspace{42mm}-J(F_\tau)D(G_\tau)-D(F_\tau)J(G_\tau)\bigr].\label{upo:all5:eq:B}
\end{align}
The finite-shift avoidance expansion is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:shiftA}
 \frac{A_{s+2-t}}{A_s}
      =16^{2-t}\left(1-\frac{p(2-t)}s+O(s^{-2})\right).
\end{equation}
The coefficient $\alpha_1$ cancels in this ratio. Substituting
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:characterfirst} and \eqref{upo:all5:eq:shiftA} in the six-term formula
therefore proves
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:half-first}
 \frac{H_s}{A_s}=h+\frac Bs+O_E(s^{-2}),\qquad
 \bfU_0=C_Ah,\quad \bfU_1=C_A(\alpha_1h+B).
\end{equation}
Three useful exact checks are
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:checks-hB}
 \begin{array}{c|rr}
 v &h(v)&B(v)\\ \hline
 (0,0;0,0)&240&-3720\\
 (1,0;0,0)&224&-3600\\
 (0,1;0,0)&144&-2520
 \end{array}
\end{equation}
The first follows also from $H_s(0)=A_{s+2}-A_{s+1}$; the second
from $H_s(1,0;0,0)=A_{s+2}-2A_{s+1}$. The third is obtained directly
from the six characters. These are checks on exact formulas, not
estimates of the convolution amplitude.

\section{The first ratio correction as an ordinary convergent sum}\label{upo:all5:sec:firstratio}
The leading amplitude established in Report 138 is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:R5}
 R_5=2\,16^{-5}\sum_{t\ge0}16^{-t}\pair h{H_t},\qquad
 R_5\ge\frac{15}{32768}>0.
\end{equation}
The lower bound retains the $t=0$, $v=w=0$ summand, using
$h(0)=240$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:S5}
 S_5=2\,16^{-5}\sum_{t\ge0}16^{-t}
                 \pair{B+p(t+5)h}{H_t}.
\end{equation}
Both sums converge absolutely: the first summand is $O((t+1)^{-p})$
and the second is $O((t+1)^{1-p})$, by the Banach pairing bound.

\begin{proposition}\label{upo:all5:prop:first-ratio}
One has $U_n/A_n=R_5+S_5/n+O(n^{-2})$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $0\le t\le(n-5)/2$, put $s=n-5-t$. Uniformly on this range,
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:half-first}, \eqref{upo:all5:eq:avoid-all}, and ordinary finite Taylor
expansion at $(t+5)/n\le1/2+O(n^{-1})$ give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:endpoint-first}
 \frac{H_{n-5-t}}{A_n}
 =16^{-5-t}\left[h+\frac{B+p(t+5)h}{n}
                   +O_E\left(\frac{(t+5)^2}{n^2}\right)\right].
\end{equation}
Pair this with $H_t$ and sum. The error is $O(n^{-2})$, because
\[
 \sum_{t\ge0}16^{-t}A_t(t+5)^2<\infty,
\]
whose summand is $O((t+1)^{-11/2})$. Symmetry supplies the two
endpoints. If the central index is integral, correcting its double
count costs only $O(n^{-p})$ after division by $A_n$.
Replacing the leading coefficient sum truncated at $t\le(n-5)/2$
by the full sum costs $O(n^{1-p})=O(n^{-13/2})$.
The unweighted first-correction coefficient tail is
$O(n^{2-p})=O(n^{-11/2})$; its displayed factor $n^{-1}$ makes its
contribution again $O(n^{-13/2})$. All these errors are smaller
than $O(n^{-2})$, proving the proposition.
\end{proof}

This elementary argument continues while its required ordinary
moments converge. Since $a_t=O_E(t^{-15/2})$, the moment of degree
six is summable but the degree-seven estimate is not. One cannot
complete an all-orders proof by replacing a divergent endpoint
moment with a formally truncated sum. The next section gives an
exact coefficient-level regularization and accounts for the entire
potential interior contribution.

\section{A coefficient-level half-integer convolution theorem}\label{upo:all5:sec:convolution}
We state the analytic mechanism in a general Banach-space form.
Let $E,F$ be real or complex Banach spaces and let
$\mathcal K:E\times E\to F$ be a continuous symmetric bilinear map.
Assume $a_n=O_E((n+1)^{-p})$ and, for every fixed $J$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:abstract-input}
 a_n=n^{-p}\sum_{j=0}^J\bfU_j n^{-j}
                          +O_E(n^{-p-J-1}),\qquad p=15/2.
\end{equation}
No generating-function continuation is among the hypotheses.

\begin{theorem}[Regularized convolution]\label{upo:all5:thm:conv}
The convolution $b_n=\sum_{s+t=n}\mathcal K(a_s,a_t)$ has
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:abstract-output}
 b_n=n^{-p}\sum_{m=0}^J c_m n^{-m}
                          +O_F(n^{-p-J-1})
\end{equation}
for every fixed $J$. The coefficients are the absolutely convergent
regularized-moment prescriptions \eqref{upo:all5:eq:cm-direct} below. Every
model--model contribution is exactly a polynomial coefficient, so
there are neither logarithms nor an extra sector starting at
$n^{1-2p}=n^{-14}$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Exact models and finite triangular matching}
For $j\ge0$ let
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:q}
 q_j(n)=[z^n](1-z)^{\alpha+j}
       =(-1)^n\binom{\alpha+j}{n}
       =\frac{\Gamma(n-\alpha-j)}{\Gamma(-\alpha-j)\Gamma(n+1)}.
\end{equation}
The equality holds for every integer $n\ge0$, since $\alpha+j$
is nonintegral. More generally write $q_x(n)=[z^n](1-z)^x$ when
$x>0$ is nonintegral, and set $q_x(n)=0$ for negative integers $n$.
The gamma-ratio expansion gives, to every fixed order $H$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:qexp}
 q_j(n)=\frac{n^{-p-j}}{\Gamma(-\alpha-j)}
       \left(\sum_{h=0}^H g_h(\alpha+j)n^{-h}
                                    +O(n^{-H-1})\right).
\end{equation}
Here $g_0(x)=1$ and the rational-polynomial coefficients are specified
by the finite rule
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:g}
 g_h(x)=[w^h]\exp\left(
 \sum_{r=1}^h\frac{(-1)^{r+1}
       [B_{r+1}(-x)-B_{r+1}(1)]}{r(r+1)}w^r\right),
\end{equation}
where $B_k$ are Bernoulli polynomials. The fixed-order expansion
follows by subtracting the Stirling expansions of
$\log\Gamma(n-x)$ and $\log\Gamma(n+1)$, with their usual
fixed-order real-positive-argument remainders; see~\cite{DLMF}.
In particular
\[
 g_1(x)=\frac{x(x+1)}2,\qquad
 g_2(x)=\frac{x(x+1)(x+2)(3x+1)}{24}.
\]
An alternative finite coefficient check uses the exact recurrence
$q_x(n+1)/q_x(n)=(n-x)/(n+1)$.

Define arrays $T_j,v_j$ triangularly:
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:matching}
 T_0&=\bfU_0,\qquad
 T_j=\bfU_j-\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}g_{j-k}(\alpha+k)T_k,\notag\\
 v_j&=\Gamma(-\alpha-j)T_j.
\end{align}
Then, for every finite $L\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:remainder}
 r_n^{[L]}:=a_n-\sum_{j=0}^Lv_jq_j(n)
                  =O_E((n+1)^{-p-L-1}).
\end{equation}
The expansion proves this for large $n$; enlarging its constant
includes all the finitely many small indices. For example
\[
 v_0=\Gamma(-13/2)\bfU_0,\qquad
 v_1=\Gamma(-15/2)(\bfU_1-195\bfU_0/8).
\]
Only finitely many input coefficients are used at any stated order.

\subsection{Canonical absolutely convergent moments}\label{upo:all5:sec:moments}
Write $\fall n\ell=n(n-1)\cdots(n-\ell+1)$ with
$\fall n0=1$. Define
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:mu}
 \mu_\ell=\sum_{n\ge0}\fall n\ell
       \left[a_n-\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-7}v_jq_j(n)\right],
\end{equation}
where the inner sum is empty for $\ell\le6$. For these first seven
moments the norm of the summand is $O(n^{\ell-15/2})$, which is
summable. For $\ell\ge7$, the bracket is $r_n^{[\ell-7]}$ of order
$n^{-\ell-3/2}$, so the weighted summand is $O_E(n^{-3/2})$.
Thus every moment is an absolutely convergent Banach-valued series.
The first subtraction is at degree seven, not degree six.

The prescription is invariant under any further model subtractions
whose indices are larger than $\ell-7$. To prove this, observe that
for nonintegral $x>\ell$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:zero-moment}
 \sum_{n\ge0}\fall n\ell q_x(n)=0.
\end{equation}
Indeed its absolute convergence follows from
$q_x(n)=O(n^{-x-1})$. For $0\le z<1$, differentiate the binomial
series termwise:
\[
 \sum_{n\ge0}\fall n\ell q_x(n)z^{n-\ell}
       =(-1)^\ell\fall x\ell(1-z)^{x-\ell}.
\]
Take $z\uparrow1$ using absolute convergence, and the right side
tends to zero. Every additionally subtracted model satisfies
$x=\alpha+j>\ell$. Consequently, whenever
$L\ge\max\{0,\ell-7\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:mu-common}
 \mu_\ell=\sum_{n\ge0}\fall n\ell r_n^{[L]}.
\end{equation}
For $\ell\le6$ this says that even the unnecessary subtractions of
$q_0,q_1,\ldots$ do not change the ordinary moment.

For inverse-power formulas it is convenient to use ordinary powers:
with $0^0=1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:ml}
 m_\ell=\sum_{n\ge0}n^\ell
       \left[a_n-\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-7}v_jq_j(n)\right].
\end{equation}
This also converges absolutely and obeys
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:stirling}
 m_\ell=\sum_{k=0}^\ell
            \left\{\begin{matrix}\ell\\k\end{matrix}\right\}\mu_k.
\end{equation}
To verify it, use a common sufficiently large $L$ in every term,
apply the finite identity $n^\ell=\sum_k\{\ell\ k\}\fall nk$,
and then use \eqref{upo:all5:eq:zero-moment}. Thus \eqref{upo:all5:eq:ml} and
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:mu} express the same regularization. Subtracting more
models improves the summand decay by an arbitrary fixed polynomial
power without changing the value; it is not a free choice of a
finite-part constant.

\subsection{An exact discrete Taylor estimate}\label{upo:all5:sec:taylor}
\begin{lemma}\label{upo:all5:lem:endpoint}
Let $x>0$ be nonintegral and let $c_n\in E$ satisfy
$\|c_n\|=O((n+1)^{-Q})$. Fix $d\ge0$ and suppose $Q>d+2$.
Then $\nu_\ell=\sum_{n\ge0}\fall n\ell c_n$ converges absolutely
for $0\le\ell\le d+1$, and
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:endpoint-lemma}
 \sum_{s=0}^n q_x(n-s)c_s
   =\sum_{\ell=0}^d\frac{(-1)^\ell}{\ell!}
        q_{x+\ell}(n)\nu_\ell
         +O_E(n^{-Q}+n^{-x-d-2}).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $\nabla f(n)=f(n)-f(n-1)$, the binomial identity gives
$\nabla q_x(n)=q_{x+1}(n)$. Repeated telescoping yields the exact
identity
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:discrete-taylor}
 q_x(n-s)={}&\sum_{\ell=0}^d(-1)^\ell\binom{s}{\ell}q_{x+\ell}(n)
 \notag\\
 &+(-1)^{d+1}\sum_{k=0}^{s-d-1}
            \binom{s-1-k}{d}q_{x+d+1}(n-k).
\end{align}
The last sum is empty when $s\le d$; the identity can equivalently
be proved by induction on $s$, using the first-difference relation.
For $s\le n/2$, its remainder is bounded by
\[
 Cn^{-x-d-2}\sum_{k=0}^{s-d-1}\binom{s-1-k}{d}
       =Cn^{-x-d-2}\binom{s}{d+1}
       \le C(s+1)^{d+1}n^{-x-d-2}.
\]
Summing this against $\|c_s\|$ gives $O(n^{-x-d-2})$ because
$Q>d+2$. Extending each half-range moment to the whole series costs
\[
 O(n^{-x-\ell-1}n^{\ell+1-Q})=O(n^{-x-Q}),
\]
which is bounded by the displayed error. Finally $q_x\in\ell^1$
since $x>0$, so the complementary range $s>n/2$ is bounded by
\[
 Cn^{-Q}\sum_{t\ge0}|q_x(t)|=O(n^{-Q}).
\]
This proves the lemma using only coefficient estimates and finite
identities.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact disappearance of the entire model product}
Fix the desired order $M$, and put
$S_n=\sum_{j=0}^Mv_jq_j(n)$ and $r_n=a_n-S_n$.
Then $r_n=O_E((n+1)^{-Q})$ with $Q=p+M+1$.
The convolution splits exactly into $S*S+2S*r+r*r$, with
$\mathcal K$ understood. For all $j,k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:polynomial-cancel}
 \sum_{s+t=n}q_j(s)q_k(t)
    =[z^n](1-z)^{2\alpha+j+k}
    =[z^n](1-z)^{13+j+k}.
\end{equation}
The identity is an equality of formal power series coefficients and
requires no analytic continuation. Its right side is a polynomial
coefficient, identically zero for $n>13+j+k$.
Thus $(S*S)_n=0$ exactly for $n>13+2M$.
This eliminates the full model--model contribution, including all
possible higher algebraic orders of that contribution, rather than
estimating only a leading interior integral.

For the residual convolution, a half-range split and $Q>1$ give
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:rr}
 \left\|\sum_{s+t=n}\mathcal K(r_s,r_t)\right\|_F
                  \le Cn^{-Q}\sum_{s\ge0}\|r_s\|_E
                  =O_F(n^{-Q}).
\end{equation}
For $q_j*r$, apply Lemma~\ref{upo:all5:lem:endpoint} with
$x=\alpha+j$ and $d=M-j$. Its conditions hold since
$Q-(d+2)=\alpha+j>0$, and $x+d+2=Q$.
The moments of $r$ in the lemma are precisely $\mu_\ell$ by
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:mu-common}. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:binomial-result}
 b_n=\sum_{m=0}^MW_mq_m(n)+O_F(n^{-p-M-1}),\qquad
 W_m=2\sum_{j+\ell=m}\frac{(-1)^\ell}{\ell!}
                                  \mathcal K(v_j,\mu_\ell).
\end{equation}
This proves the full expansion and its absolute-convergence claims.
In fact the input expansion through $M$ suffices for this output
through $M$; the all-orders hypothesis provides a single compatible
family as $M$ varies.

\subsection{Two equivalent coefficient recipes}
Expanding the finite sum in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:binomial-result} gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:cm-binomial}
 c_m=\sum_{h=0}^m
       \frac{g_{m-h}(\alpha+h)}{\Gamma(-\alpha-h)}W_h.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, with the ordinary regularized moments of
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:ml},
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:cm-direct}
 c_m=2\sum_{j+\ell=m}
       \frac{\rise{p+j}\ell}{\ell!}\mathcal K(\bfU_j,m_\ell).
\end{equation}
Here $\rise a\ell=a(a+1)\cdots(a+\ell-1)$ is a rising factorial.
To verify the equivalence by finite algebra, fix an integer $s\ge0$.
The coefficient at order $n^{-p-m}$ in the shifted tail $a_{n-s}$ is
\[
 \sum_{j+\ell=m}\frac{\rise{p+j}\ell}{\ell!}\bfU_j s^\ell.
\]
Matching to the models and expanding the exact discrete Taylor
identity gives the same polynomial, now in the basis $\fall s\ell$.
Equality for all nonnegative integers $s$ implies equality of these
finite polynomials. Apply the linear replacement
$\fall s\ell\mapsto\mu_\ell$, equivalently
$s^\ell\mapsto m_\ell$ by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:stirling}, and pair the resulting
moment with the coefficient in the first factor. This gives
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:cm-direct}, without any sum over unregularized divergent
moments. The first examples are
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:cm-small}
 c_0&=2\mathcal K(\bfU_0,m_0),\notag\\
 c_1&=2\mathcal K(\bfU_1,m_0)+15\mathcal K(\bfU_0,m_1),\\
 c_2&=2\mathcal K(\bfU_2,m_0)+17\mathcal K(\bfU_1,m_1)
                          +\frac{255}{4}\mathcal K(\bfU_0,m_2).\notag
\end{align}
Every exponent in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:binomial-result} is $p$ plus an integer.
Together with the exact polynomial cancellation, this proves
Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:conv}. In particular the putative absolute
$n^{-14}$ sector, of relative order $n^{-13/2}$, is absent.
The absence of a logarithm here is a proved coefficient-level fact;
no singularity-transfer assumption is imposed on the remainder.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
For the integer exponent $4$ of one $1234$, Part~III's Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:conv} works with logarithmic models whose products are not polynomials, and logarithms survive; Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy} compares the two theorems.
\end{writenote}

\section{All coefficients for the permutation ratio}\label{upo:all5:sec:application}
Apply Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:conv} to $a_s=16^{-s}H_s$ in the weighted
space and $\mathcal K(X,Y)=\pair XY$. Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:half} and
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:pairbound} verify every hypothesis. The resulting constants
$c_m$ are given by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:matching}, \eqref{upo:all5:eq:ml}, and
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:cm-direct}. With $N=n-5$, the exact normalization is
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:normalization}
 U_n=16^{n-5}\sum_{s+t=N}\pair{a_s}{a_t}.
\end{equation}
A finite shift expansion gives
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:bcoeff}
 U_n&=16^n n^{-p}
       \left(\sum_{r=0}^Jb_r n^{-r}+O_J(n^{-J-1})\right),\notag\\
 b_r&=16^{-5}\sum_{m=0}^r
        c_m\frac{\rise{p+m}{r-m}5^{r-m}}{(r-m)!}.
\end{align}
Divide by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:avoid-all}. Since $\alpha_0=1$, the ratio
coefficients are defined recursively by
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:rcoeff}
 r_0=b_0/C_A,\qquad
 r_j=b_j/C_A-\sum_{k=1}^j\alpha_k r_{j-k}\quad(j\ge1).
\end{equation}
Division is a finite Taylor operation at any prescribed order,
and its denominator tends to one. It preserves the stated error.
This proves \eqref{upo:all5:eq:ratio-main}. Coefficient uniqueness follows
successively by multiplying the difference of two expansions by
$n^j$ at the first possibly differing order and taking $n\to\infty$.

At orders zero and one,
$m_0=\sum_t16^{-t}H_t$ and $m_1=\sum_t t16^{-t}H_t$ are ordinary
convergent moments. Substituting
$\bfU_0=C_Ah$ and $\bfU_1=C_A(\alpha_1h+B)$ gives
\[
 b_0=C_AR_5,\qquad b_1=C_A(\alpha_1R_5+S_5).
\]
Consequently $r_0=R_5$ and $r_1=S_5$, as also proved directly in
Section~\ref{upo:all5:sec:first} and Proposition~\ref{upo:all5:prop:first-ratio}.
This checks the avoidance cancellation and the shift $n-5$
independently in the all-orders formulas.

For clarity, the complete recipe at order $J$ is finite except for
explicit absolutely convergent moment sums:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Compute $\alpha_0,\ldots,\alpha_J$ and the arrays
$\bfU_0,\ldots,\bfU_J$ by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:Urecipe} and
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:alpha-recipe}, evaluating polynomial moments by Wick's rule.
\item Match $v_0,\ldots,v_J$ by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:matching} and form the
regularized moments $m_0,\ldots,m_J$ from \eqref{upo:all5:eq:ml}. One may
subtract all models through $J$ in every moment without changing it.
\item Compute $c_0,\ldots,c_J$ from \eqref{upo:all5:eq:cm-direct}, shift by
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:bcoeff}, and divide by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:rcoeff}.
\end{enumerate}
Each moment is Banach-valued and absolutely convergent, so continuity
of the pairing justifies its use. This recipe does not claim that
all desired numerical values can be enclosed to useful precision
without additional effective error estimates.

\section{Logarithms and smooth inverse thresholds}\label{upo:all5:sec:inverse}
Set $M=C_AR_5>0$ and $\beta_j=b_j/M$ for $j\ge1$.
Equation~\eqref{upo:all5:eq:bcoeff} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:full-normalized}
 U_n=M16^nn^{-p}\left(1+\sum_{j=1}^J\beta_jn^{-j}
                                      +O_J(n^{-J-1})\right).
\end{equation}
The ordinary finite Taylor expansion of the logarithm yields
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:log-count}
 \log U_n=\lambda n-p\log n+\log M
             +\sum_{j=1}^Jd_jn^{-j}+O_J(n^{-J-1}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:dcoef}
 d_j=[w^j]\log\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^j\beta_kw^k\right),\qquad
 d_1=\frac{S_5}{R_5}-\frac{135}{8},\quad
 d_2=\beta_2-\frac{\beta_1^2}{2}.
\end{equation}
The logarithmic leading factor $-p\log n$ comes from $n^{-p}$.
There are no further $n^{-j}\log n$ terms.

For a large real target $X$, let
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:inverse-notation}
 L=\log X,\quad z=\log L,\quad
 d=\log M+p\log\lambda,\quad e_j=d_j\lambda^j,\quad a=pz-d.
\end{equation}
The letter $d$ in this section is a scalar constant, unrelated to
the earlier character degree. For $J\ge0$ put
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:nJ}
 n_J(X)=\lambda^{-1}
 \left[L+p\log L-d+\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{P_j(\log L)}{L^j}\right].
\end{equation}
The polynomials $P_j$ have degree at most $j$ and are uniquely
specified by finite reversion. Define
$y_{j-1}=L+p\log L-d+\sum_{r=1}^{j-1}P_r(\log L)L^{-r}$ and set
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:P-recursion}
 P_j(z)=-[L^{-j}]
 \left(y_{j-1}-p\log y_{j-1}+d
                    +\sum_{r=1}^j e_r y_{j-1}^{-r}-L\right),
\end{equation}
expanding formally in $L^{-1}$ with $z$ held as a polynomial
variable. The leading $\log L=z$ cancels, so this coefficient
extraction is unambiguous. Adding $P_jL^{-j}$ to $y_{j-1}$ changes
that coefficient by exactly $P_j$, because the derivative of the
expression in $y$ is $1+O(L^{-1})$. Induction shows both uniqueness
and the degree bound. The first two polynomials are
\begin{align}\label{upo:all5:eq:P12}
 P_1(z)&=pa-e_1,\notag\\
 P_2(z)&=-\frac p2a^2+(p^2+e_1)a-pe_1-e_2.
\end{align}
In particular
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:n1}
 n_1(X)=\frac1\lambda\left[
 L+\frac{15}{2}\log L-d+
 \frac{\tfrac{225}{4}\log L-\tfrac{15}{2}d
                    -\lambda(S_5/R_5-135/8)}{L}\right].
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Real error before integer ceilings]\label{upo:all5:thm:inverse}
Let $N(X)=\min\{n\ge0:U_n\ge X\}$. For each fixed $J\ge0$,
there exists a constant $C_J$ such that, for all sufficiently large
$X$,
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:ceiling-brackets}
 \left\lceil n_J(X)-\epsilon_J(X)\right\rceil
   \le N(X)\le
 \left\lceil n_J(X)+\epsilon_J(X)\right\rceil,
 \qquad
 \epsilon_J(X)=C_J\frac{(1+\log\log X)^{J+1}}{(\log X)^{J+1}}.
\end{equation}
The same error order bounds the difference between $n_J(X)$ and
the large real root of the smooth truncated logarithmic model.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Define on large real $x$
\[
 f_J(x)=\lambda x-p\log x+\log M+\sum_{j=1}^Jd_jx^{-j}.
\]
Its derivative is $\lambda-p/x+O(x^{-2})$, bounded below by
$\lambda/2$ for sufficiently large $x$. It has a unique sufficiently
large root $\xi_J(X)$ of $f_J(\xi_J)=L$, with $\xi_J\sim L/\lambda$.
Under $y=\lambda x$ its equation is
\[
 y-p\log y+d+\sum_{j=1}^Je_jy^{-j}=L.
\]
The recursion cancels all powers through $L^{-J}$. Taylor's theorem
in a fixed neighborhood of $y/L=1$ bounds the residual at
$y=\lambda n_J$ by
$O_J((1+\log L)^{J+1}L^{-J-1})$.
The derivative is bounded away from zero, so the mean value theorem
gives
\begin{equation}\label{upo:all5:eq:real-error}
 |\xi_J(X)-n_J(X)|
                  \le C'_J(1+\log L)^{J+1}L^{-J-1}.
\end{equation}
This is the smooth-model inverse statement, justified by actual
Taylor remainders rather than solely by formal reversion.

At integer $k$ with $k\sim L/\lambda$, equation
\eqref{upo:all5:eq:log-count} gives
$|\log U_k-f_J(k)|\le D_JL^{-J-1}$. Choose $C_J$ larger than
$C'_J$ by a sufficient fixed margin to absorb this error after
using $f'_J\ge\lambda/2$. For
\[
 k_- =\lceil n_J-\epsilon_J\rceil-1,
 \qquad k_+ =\lceil n_J+\epsilon_J\rceil,
\]
one has $k_-<n_J-\epsilon_J$ and $k_+\ge n_J+\epsilon_J$.
The derivative estimate and \eqref{upo:all5:eq:real-error} then give
$\log U_{k_-}<L$ and $\log U_{k_+}\ge L$ for all sufficiently
large $X$.

Finally $U_n$ is nondecreasing: append a new minimum at the final
position of a permutation. It cannot lie in an increasing
subsequence of length at least two, so this is an injection
preserving exactly one five-pattern. Also
$U_{n+1}/U_n\to16$ by \eqref{upo:all5:eq:full-normalized}, so the positive
tail is strictly increasing and unbounded. Large thresholds are
past every exceptional initial index. The two integer witnesses
therefore give exactly \eqref{upo:all5:eq:ceiling-brackets}.
\end{proof}

The inverse introduces polynomials in $\log\log X$ through inversion
of $\lambda n-p\log n$; it does not introduce logarithms into the
original coefficient expansion. Near an integer the two ceilings
in \eqref{upo:all5:eq:ceiling-brackets} may differ. No eventual exact formula
$N(X)=\lceil n_J(X)\rceil$ is claimed. If the whole error interval
lies between two successive integers, its ceilings coincide and
then determine the threshold. Linear interpolation of $\log U_n$
is sufficient for the baseline leading $o(1)$ inverse, but its
$O(n^{-2})$ curvature error would obstruct arbitrary-order inverse
claims. The proof above instead compares the smooth model directly
with the integer counts.
\begin{writenote}[5 October 2026]
Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:inverse} is an instance of parts (1)--(2) of \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} (the front-matter section \hyperref[upo:sec:neighbours]{Relation to neighbouring reports}), with $n_J(X)$ as approximate inverse; its centre comes from a finite reversion, and no Lambert-function form is involved.
\end{writenote}

\section{Exact checks and the reproducibility boundary}\label{upo:all5:sec:reproduction}
The companion separates the frozen baseline dependency from the new
analytic statements. Its exact checks are finite independent
calculations that supplement, rather than replace, the proofs.
They cover rational Gaussian moments and the first avoidance
correction; the finite boundary correction examples; gamma-ratio
and triangular matching identities; the exact discrete Taylor
formula; polynomial support of the fractional-binomial products;
the change between falling and ordinary moment bases; the finite
shift and ratio recursions; and finite inverse reversion.
Checks of a model convolution are performed with rational binomial
coefficients, without estimating a slowly convergent series.

The coefficient prescriptions contain genuine infinite sums and
unquantified fixed-order remainder constants. A finite verifier
does not certify those analytic theorems merely by testing examples.
Nor does a file hash establish a mathematical proof or numerical
amplitude certificate.

The package README documents isolated-mode execution, closed typed
JSON fixtures, complete inventory checks, rejected malformed or
unexpected inputs, independent exact replay, guard mutation tests,
and clean PDF and deterministic archive reconstruction. Reproduction
requires the stated local toolchain and does not fetch or execute
remote dependencies. Integrity manifests are not digital signatures.
The unchanged Report 138 companion remains a separate dependency;
its theorem scope and its own checks are preserved. No public
repository modification or publication is part of this release.

\section{Further questions}\label{upo:all5:sec:questions}
The all-orders theorem closes the correction-expansion question left
open by the frozen baseline, but leaves several useful next steps.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Can the weighted character and regularized-moment estimates
be made effective enough to certify useful intervals for $R_5$,
$S_5$, and subsequent coefficients? Increasing a numerical cutoff
without an explicit tail constant does not answer this question.
\item How fast do the coefficient arrays and ratio coefficients
grow with the order? The present fixed-order proof supplies no
Gevrey bound, Borel summability result, optimal truncation rule, or
controlled exponentially small sector.
\item Can the regularization be implemented with rigorous interval
arithmetic and adaptive extra subtractions, while retaining a useful
bound on cancellation error? The invariance under additional
subtractions is exact, but numerical stability is a separate issue.
\item Can the baseline binomial sum be turned into a compact rational
diagonal or a practical recurrence that independently exposes these
all-orders coefficients? P-recursiveness by itself does not supply a
small or computationally convenient recurrence.
\item For which other half decompositions does the exponent sum
make every binomial-model product a polynomial? When it does not,
a surviving interior sector or resonant logarithms must be analyzed
rather than suppressed by analogy.
\item Can one extend the positive-amplitude and regularized-convolution
method to several occurrences, or to longer monotone patterns with
a new boundary construction? The length-five mixed-chain exclusion
used in the baseline does not automatically extend to length six.
\end{enumerate}
\subsection{Further questions and research}\label{upo:all5:sub:further}
\begin{writenote}[Status of the questions above, 5 October 2026]
Questions 1--4: open (Section~\ref{upo:sec:further}, items 3, 4, 6 and~5; question~4 is Part~V's question~1). Question~5: \emph{settled for one instance} by Part~III (one $1234$: not every model product is a polynomial) \textup{(Wording corrected after the independent check of 5 October 2026; the first wording read ``answered in the negative for one $1234$''.)}, as Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy} explains: that half decomposition has the integer exponent $4$, its model products $(1-z)^m\log^2(1-z)$ are not polynomials, and the resonant logarithms survive with $\kappa_3>0$ and $\kappa_4\ne0$ (Theorem~\ref{upo:all:thm:main}). For other decompositions it is open. Question~6: open (item~9). The two delivered receipts of separate checks (\file{data/140-allorders-sources-independent_extension_checks.json} and \file{data/140-allorders-sources-check_avoidance_recurrence.json}) record runs whose scripts were not delivered; the Part says that its replay does not rerun them, and nothing here rests on them. Nothing in this Part was found to be wrong.
\end{writenote}

\clearpage
\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Synthesis}
\part*{Synthesis}
\section{The six Parts together}\label{upo:sec:together}
This section is added in the write (5~October 2026). Parts I--III and Parts V--VI apply one method to two patterns: an exact decomposition of a permutation with a unique occurrence into two halves glued by binomial shuffles; tableau or character formulas for the halves; a uniform expansion of the normalized halves; and a convolution theorem. Part~IV applies the first two steps to $1243$ with a different gluing (a shared decreasing spine) and stops after the leading term and a vanishing square-root correction.

\begin{remark}[\wtag\ One method, opposite outcomes]\label{upo:rem:dichotomy}
Parts III and VI end with the same step: an expansion of the normalized half arrays $a_n$ in a weighted Banach space (Proposition~\ref{upo:all:prop:Banach}, Theorem~\ref{upo:all5:thm:half}), followed by a coefficient-level convolution theorem (Theorems \ref{upo:all:thm:conv} and~\ref{upo:all5:thm:conv}). They differ in the exponent $p$ of the half tails, $\|a_n\|\asymp n^{-p}$: $p=4$ for one $1234$ and $p=15/2$ for one $12345$. In both cases $p$ is Regev's exponent $(h^2-1)/2$ for strips of height $h$, with $h=3$ and $h=4$.

For $p=4$ the exact tail models are $b_k(n)=[z^n](1-z)^k\log(1-z)$, $k\ge3$. A product of two models is $(1-z)^m\log^2(1-z)$, whose coefficients \eqref{upo:all:eq:log2} carry a harmonic number and hence a $\log n$, and products of a model with the regular part give further single-logarithm models. The first logarithm comes at relative order three, with coefficient $\ell_3=40\langle U_0,U_0\rangle_{\mathcal K}$. This is strictly positive because $U_0=C_0C$ has positive entries by \eqref{upo:amp:eq:Cpositive} and the kernel is positive, and it gives $\kappa_3=43020\sqrt3/\pi$; Part~III computes $\kappa_4=-5041845\sqrt3/\pi\ne0$. For $p=15/2$ the models are $q_j(n)=[z^n](1-z)^{13/2+j}$, the product of two models is the polynomial $(1-z)^{13+j+k}$ \eqref{upo:all5:eq:polynomial-cancel}, all model--model terms vanish for large $n$, and no logarithm and no extra sector at $n^{-14}$ arises.

This settles one instance of Part~VI's question~5: the half decomposition for one $1234$ does not make every model product a polynomial, and the resonant logarithms survive, with nonzero coefficients. For one $1243$ the spine convolution of Part~IV has a different endpoint law, $e_t\sim Dt^{-3/2}$ (Theorem~\ref{upo:1243:thm:profile}), and its expansion beyond the vanishing square-root term is open. \textup{(Wording corrected after the independent check of 5 October 2026; the first wording read ``This answers Part~VI's question~5 in the negative for one $1234$''.)}

A heuristic, not a theorem: if a two-half decomposition of this kind existed for one $12\cdots k$ with $k\ge6$ (Part~V notes that its mixed-chain exclusion already fails at length six), its half exponent would presumably be $((k-1)^2-1)/2$, an integer for even $k$ and a half-integer for odd $k$, and the parity of $k$ would then decide between the two outcomes.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Further questions and research across the report}\label{upo:sec:further}
Under Vladimir's standing rule of 4~October 2026, here are the open problems of all six Parts, with their sources, what is known and what is missing. Each Part's subsection ``Further questions and research'' says which of its own questions a later Part answers.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\arabic*.,ref=\arabic*]
\item \emph{Certified values of the $1234$ constants} (Report~134, Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:reproduction}; Report~135, question~1; Report~137, question~1). Proved: $R>0.50009$ (Part~I) and $R>0.50153$ (Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper}); no upper bound and no closed form. $S$, $S_2$ and $r_3$ are exact convergent series (Parts II--III) whose values and signs are uncertified. The intake's exact partial sums of Part~II's signed series for $S$ are positive and increasing, $7.16$ at $T=44$; since the series is signed, this is not a bound. Missing: effective tail constants for the domination and convolution steps.
\item \emph{Is $R_{1243}=149/160$?} (Conway and Guttmann, numerically; Report~136, question~1.) Proved: $R_{1243}>L_{35}\approx0.6214$. Numerical evidence: the note after \eqref{upo:1243:eq:lower}, consistent with $149/160$. Missing: effective control of the endpoint tails, which decay only like $T^{-1/2}$ (Report~136, question~2).
\item \emph{Certified values of $R_5$ and $S_5$} (Report~138, question~2; Report~140, question~1). Proved: $R_5\ge15/32768$. Uncertified estimates: $R_5\approx0.2044$ (note after Theorem~\ref{upo:ex5:thm:ratio}).
\item \emph{Effective constants and onsets} for the direct and inverse remainders in every Part (Report~134, Corollary~8; Report~135, question~1; Report~137, question~2; Report~136, question~2; Report~138, Section~9; Report~140, question~1). Without them no statement here gives an exact threshold algorithm; a shrinking error interval does not decide the ceiling when it crosses an integer.
\item \emph{Usable annihilating equations} (Report~135, question~2; Report~137, question~4; Report~136, question~4; Report~138, question~1 = Report~140, question~4): explicit differential equations or recurrences for the three generating functions, whose existence Parts II, IV and V prove. Also open: whether Part~IV's $40$-variable constant term is a rational diagonal of the kind whose existence is proved.
\item \emph{Growth and summability of the full expansions} (Report~137, question~3; Report~140, questions 2--3): coefficient growth, Gevrey order, Borel summability, optimal truncation, exponentially small terms, and rigorous interval implementations of the regularized sums.
\item \emph{One $1243$ beyond the square-root cancellation} (Report~136, question~3): the first nonzero correction, an $O(n^{-1})$ rate, every order, and whether logarithms occur. Parts III and VI suggest a route through a Banach expansion of spine arrays; the spine convolution has three indices and the endpoint law $e_t\sim Dt^{-3/2}$, so their theorems do not apply as stated.
\item \emph{Algebraicity for one $12345$} (Report~138, question~4). Known: D-finite (Part~V) and not rational (\cite[Theorem 5.3]{BB}); the obstruction of \cite[Lemma 6.3]{BB} needs an integer exponent and does not apply to $15/2$.
\item \emph{Longer patterns, other patterns, several occurrences} (Report~134, Section~\ref{upo:amp:sec:reproduction}; Report~137, question~5; Report~138, questions 5--6; Report~140, questions 5--6). Part~V's mixed-chain exclusion fails at length six. The parity observation in Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy} is a heuristic.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Answered inside the report} (dated notes at the questions): Report~134's first and higher corrections (Parts II and~III) and its all-orders inverse (Part~III, without effective constants); Report~135's question~3 (Part~III) and, in part, its question~4; Report~137's question~5, in part (Parts IV--VI); Report~138's question~3 (Part~VI); Report~140's question~5, settled for one instance, one $1234$ (Part~III, Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy}; first wording ``in the negative for one $1234$'', corrected after the independent check of 5~October 2026).

\emph{Wrong claims.} The one wrong claim on record is external: Conway and Guttmann's conjecture $R=1/2$, refuted in Part~I (Sections \ref{upo:amp:sec:gluing}--\ref{upo:amp:sec:certificate}, Proposition~\ref{upo:amp:prop:sharper}). Nothing in the six manuscripts was found to be wrong. Report~138's two uncited claims are pinned in the note in Section~\ref{upo:ex5:sec:intro}.


\emph{Independent check of the write} (dated note, 5~October 2026). An adversarial check made by the intake after the write (commit \file{84c52f028}) reread Part~I's refutation of $R=1/2$ end to end and found no gap: Conway and Guttmann's normalization (their Section~5.1, Figure~5, $\psi_1/\psi_0\to1/2$) is exactly $R=\lim u_n/A_n$; an independent prefix-state count of $u_n$ (no tableaux, no halves) reproduces $u_4,\dots,u_{15}$, the OEIS terms through $n=20$ and at $n=48,50$; an independent rebuild of the half counts reproduces $L_{40,10}$ and $\Pi_{44}=0.501537884\ldots$ as the printed fractions ($\Pi_{44}>0.50153$ is true, $\Pi_{44}>0.50154$ false); the gluing formula from those half counts equals the direct counts for $n\le20$ and at $n=25,30,35,40,46,48,50$. It confirmed Remark~\ref{upo:rat:rem:bls} against Bostan--Lairez--Salvy (arXiv v2, Definition~1.1 and Theorem~3.5/Corollary~3.6), the Bóna--Burstein and Waite citations of Part~V, $\kappa_3=43020\sqrt3/\pi$ and $\kappa_4=-5041845\sqrt3/\pi$ from its own pairings, and the merge (offsets $0/10/22/29/43/54$; Report~136's Sections~4--5 are Report~134's word for word apart from the closing paragraph, printed as delivered). Two wordings were corrected, each with a dated note keeping the first wording: Remark~\ref{upo:amp:rem:numerics} compared the logarithmic terms with the proved margin, not the unknown true one, and its ``below $n\approx70$'' is replaced by the crossover $n<|\kappa_4|/\kappa_3\approx117$; Remark~\ref{upo:rem:dichotomy} and Part~VI's status note now say that question~5, which asks for which decompositions, is settled for one instance.
